‘The end of Tesla!’: Cybertruck diehards turn on Elon Musk after he pulls the plug

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Tesla’s polarizing pickup is heading to the scrap heap, leaving its biggest online boosters feeling betrayed and questioning Musk’s next big pivot.

Tesla’s polarizing pickup is heading to the scrap heap, leaving its biggest online boosters feeling betrayed and questioning Musk’s next big pivot.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

The Cybertruck is heading to that shiny cyber-dumpster in the sky, leaving the folks who built online identities around Elon Musk’s polarizing pickup feeling bereft. Usually, Musk’s fanbase treads lightly around their idol’s mercurial decisions. This time, however, the truck’s demise has sparked rare backlash within the Tesla community — aimed squarely at the guy who signed its death warrant.

Troy Meekhof, who posts on numerous social platforms under the moniker “The Cybertruck Guy,” has been vocal about his disappointment in Musk’s decision to wind down Cybertruck production by the end of summer 2027. (The original plan was to start winding down production in the spring, but then Musk decided to extend it a few months.)

In a 33-minute video on YouTube, Meekhof argues that Tesla never fully committed to making the Cybertruck a success, citing a lack of advertising, confusing pricing changes, and the decision to shift focus before the truck could fully establish itself in the market.

“It feels like they didn’t try to make Cybertruck a success,” he said.

He also had choice words for Musk’s reasons for ending the Cybertruck’s short run to make room at Giga Texas for the fully autonomous Cybercab: “Cancelling all of your best products... in favor of an autonomous platform that remains both software and regulatory constrained... seems like an absolutely terrible idea,” Meekhof vented.

The Cybertruck wasn’t the only Tesla product to meet an untimely end — at least according to those with stakes (and often stock options) in the company’s success. Earlier this year, Tesla discontinued the Model S and Model X, two of its flagship vehicles. With the end of the Cybertruck, Tesla is now left with just the Model 3 and Model Y as vehicles you can actually buy. (The Semi is strictly a commercial vehicle, while the Cybercab’s availability as a consumer product remains dubious, at best.)

“Cancelling Cybertruck after all the years of getting it to production to ‘make room for CyberCab’ is a lame excuse,” @TeslaXplored wrote on X. “There are like 500 CyberCabs in one city that only Tesla influencers are riding in. Taking away consumer cars instead of building what the people want will be the end of Tesla!”

He added, “Very sad what happened to Tesla over the last 5 years.”

Meanwhile, at least one prominent aftermarket company is offering to step in and pick up the mantle. Unplugged Performance, which makes aftermarket accessories, said it would produce the Cybertruck and other vehicles on the truck platform under license, with investors funding the effort: “Worth a conversation?”

Musk, for his part, seems less interested in litigating the particulars of Cybertruck production and more intent on selling a vision of Tesla’s future centered around the Cybercab and humanoid robots. “Cybercab will do a majority of all passenger miles in any jurisdiction where it is not prohibited by law,” Musk wrote on X earlier this week. “Obvious to anyone with eyes and a brain.”

He also pushed back on claims that the Cybertruck was killed because it was unprofitable. “The Cybertruck order rate far exceeds our production rate,” Musk said. “We will try to make as many as possible before shutting down the line in about 8 months, but will have to return deposits for a lot of people.”

To be sure, Cybertruck sales have been disappointing over its entire production run. Musk initially theorized that Tesla could sell 250,000-500,000 Cybertrucks annually, but the actual numbers have been far below those projections. Last year, Tesla is estimated to have sold around 20,000 trucks; the company is on track to sell fewer than that this year.

Whether Musk’s autonomous utopia pans out or ends up joining the Cybertruck in the vaporware graveyard remains to be seen. For now, the stainless-steel wedge that was supposed to be the ultimate symbol of an indestructible future has become a monument to Musk’s shifting attention span — and a stark reminder to Tesla’s most loyal boosters that in Elon’s world, nothing should be taken for granted.

Frosinone al lavoro per la sfida al Milan, tanti assenti per Alvini

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Other  |  Read Source →

(ANSA) - FROSINONE, 12 NOV - Una marcia d'avvicinamento a ranghi ridotti. Il Frosinone ha iniziato a preparare la difficilissima gara di San Siro contro il Milan senza diversi elementi. Oltre agli 11 nazionali, Alvini deve fare i conti con infortuni e malanni di stagione. Oltre a Grillitsch e Raimondo, si sono fermati Cichella, Calvani e Terzic. I primi due sono rientrati dal ritiro dell'Under 21 per una forma influenzale. Non si sono allenati sottoponendosi alle terapie del caso. Tuttavia c'è grande fiducia che possano recuperare. Terzic non ha risposto alla convocazione della Serbia per un problema fisico. Le sue condizioni saranno valutate. Se non ce la farà sarà emergenza sulla fascia sinistra tenendo presente la squalifica di Bracaglia.

A quel punto Alvini dovrà dirottare Oyono a sinistra e inserire Tchato come d'altronde ha già fatto nel secondo tempo di Cagliari. "Alla ripresa avremo una serie di gare difficili (Milan, Parma, Inter, Udinese e Lazio ndr) ma noi ci alleniamo per andare su ogni campo con la nostra identità - ha detto Alvini a margine del Premio Clessidra ad Anghiari in provincia di Arezzo - Giocheremo le partite per determinare e fare il massimo per avvicinarci all'obiettivo della salvezza". (ANSA).

‘The Empress’ Delivers a Satisfying Ending for One of Netflix’s Best Hidden Gems | Review

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

German period drama The Empress is one of Netflix’s most underrated gems, a historical series that dramatizes the story of Elisabeth I of Austria (Devrim Lingnau Islamoğlu), the daughter of a Bavarian duke who married an emperor for love and became famous the world over for her beauty and independent spirit. Set not in Tudor or Regency England—as so many of these sorts of series often are— but in 19th-century Austria, the story blends fairytale romance with political conflict and social intrigue as Elisabeth and her husband, the Emperor Franz Joseph (Philip Froissant), attempt to navigate both threats to their throne and the stifling responsibilities of power.

Much like the historical period it’s set in, the show’s third and final season takes on a darker and increasingly tense tone. Struggles for independence in various crown lands spark war, political upheaval, and questions about what the future of the Habsburg Empire can and should look like. The fate of one marriage is perhaps a small thing next to the intensity of international geopolitics, but it is Franz and Elisabeth’s relationship that remains the heart of The Empress until its final frames.

The final season picks up a few months after the dramatic conclusion of Season 2, which saw Franz leave to command his army on the front lines of the Sardinian War. Elisabeth, who has finally managed to give birth to the male heir that secures the Habsburg family succession, is left behind in Vienna, alongside her difficult mother-in-law, the Archduchess Sophie (Melika Foroutan). But while Franz ultimately makes it back from the fighting safely, the emperor who returns from the front is a very different man from the one who left.

Traumatized by all the death he has both witnessed and been responsible for, he’s rude and standoffish, repeatedly pushing away his family and rejecting the comforting advances of his wife. Froissant does some of his best work in these early episodes focused on Franz’s return, where it's not only clear how much he’s hurting, but how ill-equipped he is to deal with the mental and emotional fallout from a war he chose to fight.

Only through his affair with an unassuming woman named Terese (Marlene Burow) does Franz begin to find something like peace again, though that decision ultimately costs him in other areas of his life. It’s a dark period for both the Austrian royals: the discovery of her husband’s infidelity, along with a debilitating health scare, sends Elisabeth out of Vienna—and, more importantly, away from her husband and his mother—for the first time since her marriage.

The real Elisabeth loved to travel, and this shift is an important part of the character’s understanding of both herself and her relationship to the larger world. Lingnau is luminous as a young woman restored to the freedom that has long been denied her, and she effortlessly conveys determination to do right by the people who have entrusted her with power over them. On her journey home from the picturesque island of Madeira, she meets the dashing Count Andrássy (Dejan Bućin), a mysterious Hungarian revolutionary. Their intense connection happens almost laughably quickly–-over the course of what is maybe two conversations—but his charismatic personality draws her swiftly into his country’s political fight for self-determination, even though it frequently puts her at odds with Franz and Sophie’s ideas about Austria’s future.

Still, despite all the political wrangling between empires, vassal states looking for the chance to determine their own destinies, and Habsburg sibling Maximilian’s (Johannes Nussbaum) ascending to the throne of Mexico, the chemistry between Lingnau and Froissant remains the emotional glue that holds this series together. Sure, Franz and Elisabeth struggle with both marital and political problems, but no matter what happens, they still can’t seem to give up on one another.

Behind the scenes of 'The Empress', Netflix’s popular historical drama - from the sets and filming locations to costumes, stunts, and the approach the cast took to portraying this fascinating chapter in history.

Foroutan pulls double duty this season as both Archduchess Sophie and her twin sister, Princess Maria Anna of Bavaria, who appears just long enough to ruin a key political negotiation with Kaiser Wilhelm II (Sebastian Blomberg). And Nussbaum gets to play Maximillian at both his most idealistic and utterly defeated. While his character has long been notable for being... let’s just call it performatively over the top, this is perhaps the most human version of him we’ve ever seen. Also, it should be noted that costume designer Gabriela Reumer once again knocks it out of the park, crafting a stunning series of sumptuous gowns and lavish uniforms that serve as various forms of armor for almost all of the show’s characters.

In keeping with its overall fairytale feel, The Empress’ ending blends both fiction and fact. The show simply doesn’t mention some of the more harrowing events of Elisabeth’s later life and reign, glossing over the eating disorder-like obsession with her own weight and leaving out the tragic loss of her only son Rudolf, who was found dead with his mistress in what was suspected to be a murder-suicide. Her assassination at the hands of an Italian anarchist is reduced to a footnote that appears onscreen before the finale’s closing credits.

Instead, The Empress turns the establishment of the Austro-Hungarian Monarchy into a turning point for many things — a new dawn for independence in Europe and a fresh start for Franz and Elisabeth’s relationship, grounded in an understanding that this particular empress needs the freedom to be herself. Is it an ending that's particularly truthful about the events of these people’s lives and their relationships with one another? Not really. But is it a satisfying (if poignant) conclusion to the story we’ve spent three seasons watching? Absolutely. In a season festooned with so much darkness, it offers a welcome burst of hope that maybe, sometimes, love really can conquer all.

The Empress Season 3 is now streaming on Netflix.

A satisfying, poignant conclusion for one of Netflix's best hidden gems.

Ball x Pit’s Risen Ballbylon DLC is a ball-bustingly difficult good time, and just as cheap

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

Ball x Pit was one of my top indie titles of 2025, and its balls have been buffed several times over since then with free updates.

But now, a paid DLC has hit Ballbylon, and at $6.99, it’s worth every penny so long as you can withstand the ball-busting difficulty. The Risen Ballbylon expansion is here, and it takes the fight above as opposed to below as you ascend in your elevator to find new enemies, riches, and biomes to explore.

I can’t overstate the value that this DLC brings for just a few bucks. Four new biomes/stages, eight new playable characters, 24 new balls, 20 new ball evolutions, 20 new buildings, 46 new passive abilities…you get the picture. There’s a lot more Ball in here, and you need every bit of balls you have because this DLC is tough, which is to be expected considering it’s an endgame activity only available once you’ve completed the base game.

The ballbreaking begins with the first new stage, FLOODEDxSEWERS, which is filled with sharks and mermen and all sorts of other deep-sea-like creatures throwing spears at you, and never really slows down from there. Gathering the new playable characters is crucial, because they are indeed powerful, leaving a lot of the base game’s characters in the dust. But the progress is slow at first thanks to a new material that is hard to come by in the beginning: Crystals.

These Crystals are found within new levels, like gold and collectible power gems, but in scarce supply to begin with. These are needed to build basically all of the new buildings, but especially the houses that unlock the new playable Hunters. I think my favorite so far is the Wallbreaker, whose balls hit the wall and some come out the other side, making for some very fun geometry and wildness when baby balls start taking up the screen.

Your base will need to be fully upgraded from the jump, so if you’re like me and didn’t play too much New Game Plus or continue on the expansion after finishing the story, you may have some grinding to do early on because the new stages hit hard, and they hit you right in the balls.

Mastering the new ball abilities, and especially the new Hunters and their plethora of new passive abilities you can select on any given run, will be crucial for success. I’m still trying to grind my way through the levels, collecting crystals where I can, but it’s slow-going…and still just as fun as I remember Ball x Pit to be.

But really, at $6.99, this really is a no-brainer addition to your library if you even got the smallest amount of joy out of Ball x Pit when it launched last year. It’s just a whole lot more of the great base game, with a new challenge for those who are looking for it.

The only question that remains: do you have the balls required?

Understated, Earthy, and Endlessly Wearable: The Best Olive Green Polishes

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Lifestyle  |  Read Source →

The earthy shade adds an edge to fall manicures while still wearing like a neutral.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Like all nail polish colors, green has a vast spectrum, one that runs from electric neon to bold kelly to soft sage and all the way down to deep, moody olive. Of all the greens out there, olive sits in a quieter place. The hue is earthy and a little mysterious, somewhere between a vintage army jacket and an actual juicy olive. It’s a shade we love for fall, since it pairs so seamlessly with black, rich browns, burgundy, red, and, of course, cream.

Once reserved for the brave few, olive has now gone mainstream. Part of the appeal is how wearable it is, offering an unexpected twist without ever feeling too loud. It’s also soft and soothing to the eye, making it an easy shade to wear day after day.

If you’re new to the shade, the easiest way in is to treat it like a neutral. Try it with a camel coat and chunky knits when the weather turns, or a crisp white shirt for something fresh and unexpected. A creamy, opaque finish feels polished and modern, while a glossy jelly or a hint of shimmer adds depth in low light. However you wear it, olive looks effortlessly considered and adds an edge to your style.

Below, we’ve rounded up the best olive green nail polishes to shop now.

A personal favorite, Elia is a muted olive green that looks rich from the very first stroke. The opaque formula glides on evenly in a single swipe and dries to a soft gloss that lasts through daily wear.

Manucurist’s take on olive green feels a touch brighter. It sits beautifully on light and dark skin tones alike and delivers an opaque wash of color. Part of the “Green Flash” collection, it cures under an LED gel lamp and lasts for nearly 21 days.

Essie’s Totally Plaid is an olive green polish with a cream finish that delivers a gel-like shine and depth while still removing as easily as a regular polish. The formula applies smoothly in two coats, giving you rich, opaque color without the need for a UV lamp.

This deep and moody olive green shade is the epitome of effortless chic, pairing easily with everything from neutral knits to bold jewel tones.

Inspired by the lush landscapes of the Mediterranean, this olive green evokes a lighter take on the olive color spectrum. It’s a tad brighter and slightly more yellow-toned, making it a great pick if you want a fresh, sunlit green this fall.

O.P.I’s Suzi is the chic olive green and wardrobe staple of the olive family. It’s a bit darker-toned, radiating earthy warmth and understated elegance. Plus, it’s fast-drying and offers up to seven days of wear.

For those seeking a little more sparkle, this olive green jelly is packed with large green holographic glitters that create a textured finish and catch the light with every movement, adding a spark of joy to your manicure.

Calming and serene, this moss green shade is part of the “Campground Collection”, a four-shade assortment of earthy, comforting colors. Its buttery-smooth application matches the shade’s peaceful look.

This may not look like your average olive green at first glance, but that’s because it has taupe undertones. Once it’s on your nails, you’ll notice the hint of green was there all along. The result is a soft, grounded neutral that pairs effortlessly with cozy fall knits and earthy accessories.

This nail color may be named Pickle, but we see the olive in there. This tangy green with muted yellow undertones feels lively while remaining neutral enough to pair with everything in your wardrobe.

A true olive green, Extra Dirty brings rich, earthy depth to your nails. All you need is one to two coats for a creamy manicure worthy of a fall-season spotlight.

Jeep Just Got into the Retro-Gaming Groove with ‘Moab Run’

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The game lets you play as one of seven special-edition Jeeps on Utah's Sand Flats Road.

Modern driving games like Snowrunner or Gran Turismo harness massive computing power to accurately model the physics of piloting a vehicle at speed. Sometimes, however, you just want an overexcited announcer to shout “Awesome!” while you sail a Jeep Gladiator over a crest, misjudge the landing, and impact an immovable sandstone massif at 85 mph.

Such is the appeal of Moab Run, a new retro-themed off-roading game from none other than Jeep itself. Playable on mobile or in a browser window, the game channels the best of 1990s-era 3-D games like Sega Rally or Offroad Challenge, infusing it with playable examples of the very real special-edition Wranglers Jeep has launched over the past year or so.

See all results for new 2027 Jeep Wrangler for sale near 80516

At launch, you can play seven of these Wranglers, from the synthwave-look Rewind to the military cosplay Sarge, with the full dozen coming to future game expansions. There’s also currently just the one course set in Utah at present, but two other tracks will become playable in due course.

Controls are very basic: accelerate, brake, and steer, with an option to powerslide and build up boost for bigger jumps. Jeep includes a somewhat hilarious disclaimer up front: “Do not attempt to recreate these actions in real life,” because, again, 85 mph into sandstone is not so fun in the non-digital world.

As befits a company that’s all about off-roading, the roads in Moab Run are basically just a guideline. You can switch between various overlapping routes, climb sandstone ridges to pick up collectible star points, or just ignore things completely and fly off the road into a canyon river. There are also hidden collectible ducks scattered across the map, because of course there are. It’s a Jeep Thing.

You can’t damage the Wranglers, you seemingly can’t get stuck, and if you purposely drive in the wrong direction, all that’ll happen is a large green arrow will appear to point to where you’re supposed to go. It’s basically a penalty-free way to blow off some steam, and the opposite of what technical real-world off-roading is like.

Also, it’s free. If you fancy getting in a little Jeep Wranglin’ action during your coffee break, Moab Run will do the trick. Go hunt down those hidden ducks, and aim for the hangtime challenge award.

When a battery dies, you replace it — how jumpstarting cells with fresh mitochondria could revolutionize medicine

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Mitochondria can become compromised in a number of medical conditions. What if they could be replaced like batteries?

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

A newborn baby's heart is roughly the size of a walnut — which makes open-heart surgery challenging.

Each year, about 10,000 babies in the United States are born with critical heart defects that may require surgery to fix, often fairly soon after birth. In these infants, the two major arteries of the heart could have swapped positions or the left side of the heart may be far too small to pump properly.

Even if their surgeries go well, these babies face perilous recoveries. A complication can arise when blood flows back into the repaired heart, triggering what's known as an ischemia-reperfusion injury. This can stun the organ, triggering extensive damage that requires doctors to place the baby on a life-support machine that pumps and infuses oxygen into blood to sub in for the hampered heart and lungs. Only about 40% of newborns make it off the machine, because often, their tiny hearts don't recover.

Doctors and scientists at Boston Children's Hospital have been working to improve those odds using a creative idea: replacing the fuel source in damaged heart cells to help them bounce back from injury. That fuel source is mitochondria, the "batteries" found in most cells in the body. In this case, the mitochondria are isolated from an abdominal muscle and then injected directly into the heart.

As of April, 17 babies have undergone this procedure, and 80% made it off ECMO. It's too soon to say if the treatment improves overall survival rates, but the early data hints that it might. A clinical trial is currently underway.

James McCully (left) and his colleague Dr. Sitaram Emani of Boston Children's Hospital have developed a technique to transplant mitochondria into the hearts of children recovering from surgery.

"The first one or two for me were pretty nerve-wracking," said James McCully, a research associate at Boston Children's Hospital and associate professor of surgery at Harvard Medical School whose pioneering work paved the way for these procedures. His team isolates and purifies the mitochondria that a surgical team then transplants into patients.

"But you know, the heart comes back — you can actually almost see it coming back" in real time, he told Live Science. A heart deprived of energy looks pallid, while a freshly rejuvenated heart blushes a healthy pink.

Baby heart infusions are just one example of the promise of "mitochondrial transplantation." Following McCully's lead, other scientists have started investigating whether infusions of mitochondria could revitalize donated organs destined for transplantation, help stroke patients recover, and treat kids with devastating diseases. This work is still highly experimental, and its route to regulatory approval remains unclear.

But at its core is a straightforward idea: When a battery dies, you replace it.

The human body contains tens of trillions of cells, most of which contain hundreds to thousands of mitochondria. Following some back-of-the-envelope math, that means the total potential energy in each person "is the equivalent of a lightning bolt," Douglas Wallace, director of the Center for Mitochondrial and Epigenomic Medicine at Children's Hospital of Philadelphia, told attendees last year at a symposium on mitochondrial medicine.

Mitochondria, in a word, are powerful. They produce most of the chemical energy used by complex cells by churning out a molecule called adenosine triphosphate (ATP). The idea of transferring these fuel factories from one cell to another as a medical treatment emerged less than two decades ago — but cells have been doing such swaps on their own for eons.

A leading theory posits that mitochondria evolved from simple microbes. And today, they still retain distinctly microbial features.

Dr. Jon Brestoff, an associate professor of pathology and immunology and director of the Immunometabolism Initiative at the Washington University School of Medicine in St. Louis, describes this ability to move from cell to cell as a relic of mitochondria's bacterial past. "It is hypothesized that mitochondria transfer has likely been retained throughout evolution of eukaryotic life," the huge domain of life that includes all plants, fungi, protists and animals, Brestoff told Live Science.

Mitochondria are the powerhouses of nearly all complex cells on Earth, and there are a few theories as to how they adopted that ubiquitous role. A leading idea posits that, some 1.5 billion to 2 billion years ago, one simple cell engulfed another. The bacterial cell that got swallowed carried a circular loop of DNA inside its membrane, and that captured cell later became what we now know as the mitochondrion. The new organelle enabled cells to take advantage of the abundance of oxygen on our planet by converting it into usable fuel.

Mitochondria retained bacteria-like features, such as having their own DNA and membranes. But in 2006, researchers reported that mitochondria can also exit their "host" and survive the journey into a totally different cell — a phenomenon known as mitochondrial transfer.

"That was a complete revolution in thinking," said Dr. Lance Becker, professor and Chair of the Department of Emergency Medicine at Northwell Health and the Feinstein Institutes for Medical Research. "With that notion that mitochondria might be mobile, that they might cross boundaries, that they might go from one cell to another cell, came the possibility that maybe that was something we could use to help people."

The 2006 study showed that cells that had been depleted of their own mitochondrial DNA could obtain healthy mitochondria from neighboring cells. After receiving other cells' powerhouses, the once-depleted cells were able to conduct energetically costly processes, such as cell division.

This research provided the first evidence that one cell can "donate" metabolic properties to its neighbor by sending over a fresh batch of mitochondria. Scientists once thought cells had to make all of their own mitochondria, but now, "I think cells have the ability to 'put their hand up' when they need mitochondria," Becker told Live Science.

Jon Brestoff is studying the use of mitochondrial transplantation for genetic diseases that impair the function of these cellular powerhouses.

Because those first experiments were done in lab dishes, initially, "it was unclear whether or not it was really biologically meaningful or relevant," Brestoff said. But within the past five to six years, improved tools and techniques have enabled scientists to watch mitochondrial transfer unfold within living organisms, he noted. It's been shown that cells of the same type can exchange mitochondria, as can cells of different types.

As scientists' fundamental understanding of mitochondrial transfer grew, the notion of doing the same thing therapeutically gained more traction.

"That procedure," Brestoff said, "is rooted in the biology of mitochondria transfer."

Just two years after the landmark 2006 paper, McCully's research group released data that hinted at the medicinal potential of mitochondrial transplantation.

Since then, these complementary lines of research — the foundational biology and the preclinical investigation — have continued in parallel. McCully's preclinical work helped to inspire other labs to explore the use of mitochondrial transplantation for a range of medical applications, including organ transplants and treatments for stroke and genetic disease.

McCully is "the pioneer — the guru of the field," said Dr. Giuseppe Orlando, a professor of surgery and regenerative medicine at Wake Forest University School of Medicine who is exploring mitochondrial transplantation as a way to maintain the integrity of transplant organs.

McCully's road to mitochondrial transplantation started in the 1980s. As a molecular biologist, he wanted to uncover mechanisms that protect the heart from damage, particularly when the organ's oxygen supply is compromised — such as during ischemia, or low blood flow. With an eye on older adults, he wanted to spare patients' heart tissue from the damage and cell death that can occur before and during heart surgery.

It changed color, went back to a pink hue, and just kept on going.

McCully and his collaborators initially "didn't care about the mitochondria one iota," he said. They were more focused on the nucleus, the control center that houses most of a cell's DNA, and its influence on cellular pathways that might boost the heart's resilience to ischemia. But "what we found was that everything we did started coming back to the mitochondria," he said.

They found that ischemia triggers damage and dysfunction in mitochondria that persists even after the heart's oxygen supply is restored; this dysfunction ultimately kills cells. The damaged mitochondria swell up, leak their innards, and set off a chain reaction that causes a cell to self-destruct.

McCully and colleagues observed that biological pathways that seemed to reduce this cell death often centered on the mitochondria. "We were looking around at either pathways upstream of the mitochondria or downstream of the mitochondria," he said.

So, logically, they started looking directly at mitochondria, studying what the organelle looks like before, during and after ischemia. One day, while isolating mitochondria from a laboratory pig, the animal developed fibrillation, meaning its heart began beating very quickly, with an uncoordinated and rapid rhythm. The team tried to reset the heart's rhythm with electric shocks and medications, to no avail. But McCully had an idea.

"At that point in time, the heart's kind of [got a] gray pallor, and it looks dead," he said. "And I said, 'Go upstairs; grab some of those mitochondria that we've isolated from the non-ischemic type. Just bring them down.'" They'd previously gathered some healthy mitochondria from the same pig, in hopes of generating a "before-and-after" picture of ischemia. Instead, McCully suspended those healthy mitochondria in a solution and then injected them directly into the pig's fibrillating heart.

"And the heart came back — started beating normally. It changed color, went back to a pink hue, and just kept on going," he said.

Surgeons sample a small piece of muscle from the patient. That muscle will serve as the source of healthy mitochondria.

After mitochondria have been isolated from the muscle sample, they are prepared in a syringe and injected into the heart tissue.

That serendipitous experiment led to a formal test of the technique in rabbit hearts, the results of which were published in 2008. In the study, they introduced healthy mitochondria to the rabbits' hearts during ischemia, just before reperfusion — when blood is reintroduced into the oxygen-starved tissue. They found that the mitochondria improved the organs' recovery and reduced tissue death. Further research in animals suggested that mitochondria that originated from one type of tissue, like liver tissue or skeletal muscle, could be introduced to other tissue types, such as cardiac muscle.

After seeing McCully's results in various animal experiments, including more with pigs, Dr. Pedro del Nido, chair of cardiac surgery at Boston Children's and a professor of child surgery at Harvard Medical School, approached him about testing the technique in human patients — namely, in babies on a life-support machine known as ECMO.

"He saw it in the animals; he saw what was going on," McCully said. "And he deemed it as being a possible intervention to save these kids."

They sought permission from the Food and Drug Administration (FDA) to test the procedure and cleared the required regulatory hurdles. The process was made slightly easier because the mitochondria were coming from the patients' own bodies, not from a donor, McCully noted. Once given the go-ahead, they set forth with the help of Dr. Sitaram Emani, a heart surgeon at Boston Children's. The first procedure took place in 2014.

During each procedure, Emani and the surgical team collect a small sample of skeletal muscle from the patient, and McCully then isolates whole mitochondria from the tissue and prepares them in a syringe. He refined the isolation technique so the time from collecting the muscle sample to injecting mitochondria is only 30 minutes. Lately, the team has been developing a technique they can perform in the operating room, rather than in a lab, which could cut the time to 20 minutes. Emani uses various tests, including echocardiography, to see which areas of the heart tissue move the least, and injects mitochondria straight into those areas.

"It's great to be able to see something you've done and worked on for so many years actually provide a benefit," McCully said.

Dr. Giuseppe Orlando thinks that mitochondrial transplantation could serve as a potential method for fortifying donated organs that are intended for transplant patients.

While McCully is trying to protect infant hearts, other scientists want to do the same for other organs — including those destined for transplant surgeries.

To Orlando, mitochondria represent a potential way to make donated kidneys work better. "In transplant medicine, one of the major issues is making sure that the organs that are procured are well stored and kept viable until implantation," said Orlando, who is also president of the Cell Transplant and Regenerative Medicine Society.

Maintaining an organ's viability means preserving mitochondrial function as much as possible, but the transplantation process is tough on the organelles. "That energy plant is damaged. So it's pretty obvious that you should start thinking about a way to replace the damaged mitochondria," Orlando said.

Donated organs suffer some damage when they're unplugged from the donors' bloodstream, in part because this cuts off mitochondria's crucial oxygen supply. And then, paradoxically, the donor organs also get damaged when they're plugged into the recipient's bloodstream. That's because the fast flow of blood triggers the production of free radicals — unstable molecules that damage tissues.

While healthy organs normally produce some free radicals, reperfusion unleashes a flood. "It's more than the organ can bear and sustain," Orlando explained. This damage contributes to a phenomenon called delayed graft function, in which a transplanted kidney doesn't work right away so the patient ends up needing dialysis in the first week after surgery.

"You want to make sure that the kidney wakes up faster," Orlando said. That's where mitochondria come in.

Orlando was introduced to the idea of mitochondrial transplantation around 2019 through a collaboration with Dr. Benedetta Bussolati, a nephrologist and full professor of laboratory medicine at the University of Turin in Italy. Her lab had been studying mitochondrial transplantation in isolated kidney cells in lab experiments, and they reached out to Orlando about extending that work to lab animals.

In pigs, the researchers ran experiments in which the animals' kidneys were collected and stored in the same way human kidneys commonly would be prior to being transplanted. One set of kidneys received a placebo treatment, while another got an infusion of mitochondria from the donor pigs' muscle tissue. Upon being reperfused, the treated kidneys produced fewer free radicals and accrued less damage than the untreated kidneys did, the team reported in 2023.

They backed up this finding by analyzing RNA, a molecule that reflects which genes are active in cells. Compared with the untreated kidneys, the treated kidneys showed greater activity in genes involved in mitochondrial metabolism and lower activity in genes involved in repairing injuries.

Orlando thinks that mitochondrial transplanations could help organs "wake up" faster after being transferred from an organ donor to a recipient.

Some groups are exploring mitochondrial transplantation in other donor organs, like the lungs — specifically, lungs treated with "ex vivo perfusion" (EVP), an emerging approach that helps retain the viability of transplant organs. Rather than placing a donor organ in a cooler of ice, as is generally standard, EVP flushes an organ with nutrient- and oxygen-rich fluid, helping to maintain its function until the transplant surgery. In lab experiments with pig and human lungs, scientists found that introducing fresh mitochondria during EVP further improved the organs' function while reducing inflammation and free radicals.

Across different organ transplants, Orlando sees mitochondria as a potential means to improve patient outcomes and expand the pool of available organs. There are scenarios in which would-be organ donors die unexpectedly of cardiac arrest and their organs are damaged in the process; mitochondrial transplants may help make such organs fit for patients, Orlando has said.

In the near term, Orlando and his colleagues are continuing to study the basic science of mitochondrial transplantation for this clinical purpose; they've also been cleared to run a clinical trial with donor kidneys but need more funds to proceed.

Looking to the more distant future, he and other experts in the field envision a world where transplant organs can be bioengineered and stored in banks, ready and waiting for the patients who need them, Orlando said. If that vision materializes, he suggested, mitochondrial transplants might be useful for preparing banked organs for transplantation "by giving them kind of an energy boost."

If infusions of fresh mitochondria could help the heart, kidney and lungs, what about the brain? That's the question Dr. Melanie Walker and colleagues at the University of Washington School of Medicine (UW Medicine) want to answer.

Walker, a clinical professor of neurological surgery, treats patients who have had ischemic strokes, which are caused by blood clots blocking blood flow to the brain. The immediate aim of post-stroke treatment is to clear out the blocked "pipe" and restore circulation to the brain as quickly as possible, she said. But no matter how quickly this is done, some brain cells are damaged. And once the blood clot is removed, the brain may then suffer a reperfusion injury.

Standard treatments can dissolve the clot but don't protect the brain, Walker said. "What about all those cells that are maybe not quite dead — they're on the verge of maybe dying? What can we do to support those guys?" she asked.

The UW Medicine team had heard about McCully's work in Boston and wondered if something similar could be applied in stroke patients. But while heart surgery exposes the tissue in need of new mitochondria, accessing the brain is trickier. "You don't do a craniotomy to give an IV medicine," Walker told Live Science.

But Walker realized that a harmful downstream effect of stroke might provide an opportunity for treatment. Immediately after a stroke, the brain's protective shield — known as the blood-brain barrier — becomes more permeable than usual, allowing more substances to pass from the bloodstream into brain tissue. That might open a door for mitochondrial transplantation.

One method of treating ischemic strokes is to physically remove the clot using a procedure called a thrombectomy, which a surgeon is preparing for in the photo above. Dr. Melanie Walker and colleagues tried combining this approach with mitochondrial transplantation.

A 2023 mouse study spearheaded by University of Virginia researchers and co-authored by Walker backed this idea, finding that mitochondria entered the brain after stroke and integrated into brain cells. These mitochondria had been gathered from muscle tissue, as in McCully's studies, and introduced to the brain via an artery. The treatment increased ATP production while reducing cell death caused by the stroke, compared with untreated mice's brains.

This work paved the way to the first trial using mitochondrial transplant in the human brain. The small safety trial included adult patients who'd had ischemic strokes and underwent thrombectomies ‪—‬ surgeries to remove the clots from their blood vessels. Four received mitochondrial transplants during their procedures and 12 did not. Mitochondria were injected near the site of each patient's stroke, into the closest branch of one of the brain's major arteries.

No negative side effects were tied to the transplants, hinting that the procedure is safe in humans. "But the next question is, how do we understand if it helps? And those are harder trials to design," Walker said. That's partly because the transplants are tacked onto the clot-removal procedure, so it's difficult to separate their effects from those of the clot removal itself, she noted.

Future trials will also need to explore exactly where the transplanted mitochondria end up in the human brain, how well they function once inside brain cells, and how the dose and timing of the transplant affect patients' outcomes, the trial runners wrote in their report. For the moment, Walker said her team is focused on exploring the safety aspects of the approach and have no further trials planned at this time.

Beyond heart defects, transplant organs and stroke, researchers are looking to mitochondrial transplantation as a solution for genetic conditions that harm mitochondria directly. Brestoff and colleagues are investigating the therapy's potential for treating Leigh syndrome, a rare neurological condition caused by genetic mutations that disrupt the fuel-producing machinery in mitochondria.

"Those kids have a terrible prognosis," Brestoff said. Most children with the most common form of the syndrome, whose symptoms show up by age 2, die by age 3. There is no cure for the syndrome, and treatments to relieve its symptoms are limited. So if it works, mitochondrial transplantation could fill a big gap.

So far, the approach shows promise in lab mice with a severe form of Leigh syndrome, improving the rodents' function and extending their median lifespan by 19% to 24%, the researchers demonstrated in 2024. They're now studying the approach in cells derived from patients with the disease.

I can get the mitochondria from you and put them into me, me into you — doesn't really matter.

Some companies, such as Israel-headquartered Minovia Therapeutics, are already running clinical trials with patients with other types of mitochondrial disease. Minovia is running one with children with Pearson syndrome, in which missing mitochondrial DNA hinders the function of bone marrow, which normally makes blood and immune cells.

While patients with a heart or brain problem may be able to pluck mitochondria from healthy muscles, people with mitochondrial disease don't necessarily have healthy mitochondria to spare. Especially for these patients, getting mitochondria from other people ‪—‬ known as heterologous transplantation ‪—‬ likely makes the most sense. What's more, for transplantation to be used widely for any purpose, there would need to be an "off-the-shelf" mitochondrial product available, Brestoff said.

Such a product could eliminate the step in Boston Children's heart procedure where mitochondria have to be isolated from a patient while they're waiting, open on an operating table. "Getting them from the recipient can sometimes be problematic, because the recipients are sick. All the organs, all the tissue is quite distressed," McCully noted. A standardized and safety-tested mitochondrial product would therefore be a boon for the field.

Brestoff is on the scientific advisory board of LUCA Science, a company developing mitochondria as therapeutic agents, and a consultant for Cellergy Therapeutics, which is making mitochondrial products to combat conditions from muscle degeneration and aging. These companies exemplify the effort to come up with ready-made products that could someday bring the therapy to more patients.

A shift to heterologous transplantation does raise concerns about immune reactions. "You have some variations in the proteins that are present in your mitochondria that I don't have," Brestoff noted. "So it is possible that our immune systems recognize heterologous mitochondria as non-self."

But McCully doesn't expect immune reactions to be a big problem, based on his research in animals. "I can get the mitochondria from you and put them into me, me into you — doesn't really matter," he said. Early trial results from Minovia and others suggest heterologous transplantations come with minimal side effects, at least at doses tested so far.

Another roadblock is that the FDA has not approved any organelles as therapies yet, so the standards and regulatory framework for the field are still being established, Brestoff said.

Despite that hurdle, the field has advanced significantly since its start as a niche, theoretical interest and then a highly experimental procedure for the sickest newborns. Scientists are now exploring the treatment as a solution to many common conditions, from rheumatoid arthritis to chronic pain.

Becker compared the state of the field to the early days of organ transplantation.

"It just turned out that it was revolutionary, when all of a sudden you could just give a person a kidney transplant," he said. "I think it's going to be kind of similar with mitochondria."

You can also catch the show through BBC iPlayer after it airs on TV.Outside the U.K.? You don't have to miss the series if you are a Brit living abroad because you can watch your usual streams from anywhere with a VPN.

Unfortunately, as the release is tied to the BBC, there is no way to watch "Shetland" season 11 on any linear TV channels in the U.S., Canada, or Australia (or anywhere outside of the U.K.) at the moment.

The show will not be playing on any streaming services immediately, either, but past seasons of the show are on BritBox in all three countries. We expect season 11 to land there eventually, too.

The solution? NordVPN can unlock your stream if you're a British resident so you can watch "Shetland" season 11 from anywhere.

Pakistani astronaut set to become 1st international visitor to China's Tiangong space station this month

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Join The Club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

Latest space missions, discoveries and exploration updates.

Breaking space news, the latest updates on rocket launches, skywatching events and more!

Breaking space news, the latest updates on rocket launches, skywatching events and more!

Sign up to our monthly entertainment newsletter to keep up with all our coverage of the latest sci-fi and space movies, tv shows, games and books.

Discover this week's must-see night sky events, moon phases, and stunning astrophotos. Sign up for our skywatching newsletter and explore the universe with us!

Space.com's Sci-Fi Reader's Club. Read a sci-fi short story every month and join a virtual community of fellow science fiction fans!

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

"Having the Pakistani astronaut on board China's spaceships will mark the first-ever joint mission of Chinese and foreign astronauts in the history of China's manned space program."

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

An astronaut from Pakistan is set to launch on China's Shenzhou 24 mission in the coming weeks and become the first-ever international visitor to the Tiangong space station.

A pair of astronaut candidates from Pakistan have been training at the Astronaut Centre of China in Beijing, following an agreement reached between China and Pakistan in Islamabad in February 2025 and a subsequent selection process.

China's human spaceflight agency confirmed in a Nov. 7 press statement that the upcoming Shenzhou 24 mission will carry one of the astronauts. The agency also released a mission patch.

"China and Pakistan are all-weather strategic cooperative partners," Foreign Ministry spokesperson Guo Jiakun said Nov. 9. "Having the Pakistani astronaut on board China's spaceships will mark the first-ever joint mission of Chinese and foreign astronauts in the history of China's manned space program."

It is not yet known which of the two candidates will fly on Shenzhou 24. They were named in Pakistani press reports as Muhammad Zeeshan Ali and Khurram Daud, both pilots from the country's military forces. The pair were chosen from a wider pool of around 20 finalists.

The chosen candidate will fly as a payload specialist on Shenzhou 24 along with two Chinese colleagues, while the other will be on standby as a backup. While the Chinese astronauts will be starting a six-month-long stay in space, the Pakistani astronaut will spend roughly a week in orbit aboard Tiangong during a standard crew rotation period. He will then return to Earth aboard the departing Shenzhou 23 spacecraft, along with two Chinese astronauts who will have completed a six-month-long stay in orbit.

The arrangement means changes to China's usual "three in, three out" Tiangong crew rotations. One of the three Shenzhou 23 astronauts, who arrived at Tiangong on May 24, will stay aboard and complete China's first-ever year-long stay in orbit, while the Pakistani astronaut will take their seat aboard Shenzhou 23 to return home.

During his stay in space, the Pakistani astronaut will be responsible for four research payloads selected from more than 300 proposals, according to Pakistan's space agency, SUPARCO.

China has not announced a timeline for the launch of the Shenzhou 24 mission, but it is expected to lift off before the end of November. The Shenzhou 23 astronauts currently aboard Tiangong are closing in on 180 days in orbit, nearing an end to their six months in space.

Shenzhou 24 will launch from Jiuquan Satellite Launch Center in the Gobi Desert on a Long March 2F rocket. The identities of the crew for China's human spaceflight missions are typically closely guarded, and will only be revealed in a press conference a day ahead of launch. Shenzhou 24's Long March 2F rocket will be rolled out to the pad around a week ahead of launch.

The partnership between the two countries has already produced Chinese-launched Pakistani satellites, including a lunar cubesat, and a Pakistani-built rover due to fly on the Chang'e-8 lunar landing mission.

Mormon Wives' Jen Affleck Alleges Zac Affleck Gambled Away Their Money

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The Secret Lives of Mormon Wives: Orange County’s Jen Affleck claimed her husband Zac Affleck gambled all of their money, alleging he spent over $100,000 that was in their bank account.

Jen Affleck is an open book about what went wrong in her marriage to Zac Affleck.

Months before the Secret Lives of Mormon Wives star filed for divorce from Zac after seven years of marriage, Jen—who is pregnant with the couple's fourth baby—detailed what led to their breakup on the spinoff series The Secret Lives of Mormon Wives: Orange County, claiming that her husband gambled away their savings.

And not only that, but Jen alleged he did it numerous times throughout their marriage.

“He was being really weird,” she shared during a conversation with her costar Bobbi Althoff in the fourth episode of the Hulu series, out Nov. 12. “It seemed like he just wasn't being honest. So, I went to check our bank. The money's gone, and there's over $100,000 in there. So, I called him and I'm like, ‘Why is the money gone?’”

“He’s done this before multiple times,” Jen continued, “whether it was through online poker or it was through investing in crypto, I said, ‘You're not allowed to do that anymore.“

Bobbi chimed in, asking an emotional Jen for clarification that Zac has “just spent all of your money” before, to which Jen replied, “Yes.”

Breaking down in tears, the 27-year-old went on to tell Bobbi that Zac “admitted” to emptying their bank account.

“He’s like, ‘I f--ked up and you wanted to separate, so I didn't tell you this because I knew if you found out you would divorce me,’” Jen shared. “And I said, ‘Yeah, I'm done. I'm divorcing you. F--k you. I’m literally pregnant.”

The MomToker added that she only took a closer look at the family’s finances when someone “came to Zac parents’ house yesterday and was trying to serve him papers.”

“They were looking for him,” she recalled. “He's like, ‘Yeah, well, remember we lived in that home in Arizona. I guess I didn't pay all the extra charges or whatever for us to move out.’ And I was like, ‘You told me you took care of that.’”

“Zac and I have moved a lot in the past couple of years,” Jen continued. “We rented a house in Arizona for a year. We ended up moving back to Utah. We literally moved out of that house in Arizona two years ago. So, I thought everything was taken care of. I mean, Zac was handling the finances during that time, but finding this out has made me question everything.”

And, according to the Dancing With the Stars alum, she’s still unsure about the extent of Zac’s alleged debts.

E! News has reached out to Zac for comment on the gambling allegations but has not heard back.

However, the 30-year-old recently addressed his split, sharing in a Nov. 5 TikTok, “Jen came to me about a year ago, after Dancing With the Stars, and told me she was unhappy and wanted a divorce. And I basically begged her to stay, told her work on things, fix things, become whoever she needed me to be."

"I don't want anyone speaking bad about Jen," he continued. "I'll be the first to say I've made plenty of mistakes in our marriage, and it just really was time for us to go our own ways. We got married so young, and it sucks because I really did feel like we had something pretty special when we got married."

Jen and Zac’s marital issues played out on their original Hulu show, with the pair ultimately deciding to move to Orange County for a fresh start. But by the spinoff’s first episode, Jen said she had already hit her “breaking point.”

“I never thought I’d be in the same situation again where I’d be on the verge of divorcing Zac and get pregnant again,” she admitted. “There’s already judgment around divorce when it comes to the church, throw on a pregnancy, and that’s gonna become worse.”

"No, it was him," she emphasized in the confessional. "He said he had just kissed her on the couch. No, he went on the couch, they hooked up on the couch, and then they went to the room and they hooked up."

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/regulatory/cms-pauses-hospice-home-health-medicare-enrollments-fraud-crackdown" hreflang="en">CMS extends moratorium on new Medicare enrollments for hospice, home health agencies</a>

📅 2026-11-12  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →
CMS extends moratorium on new Medicare enrollments for hospice, home health agencies" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

The Trump administration is extending its moratorium on new Medicare enrollments from hospice and home health agencies.

The Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services said Thursday that it will extend the moratorium by six more months as part of its efforts to combat fraud, waste and abuse in the Medicare program.

CMS said that the initial moratorium window has allowed the agency "to conduct targeted investigations and deploy advanced data analytics" to identify potential cases of fraud. An extension was deemed necessary to continue that effort, as the "circumstances warranting the imposition of the moratoria have not yet abated."

The agency said that hospice and home health have long been considered high risk areas for fraud.

“We’ve made real progress over the past six months, but fraud in hospice and home health persists and our work isn’t finished,” said CMS Administrator Mehmet Oz, M.D., in the announcement. “Medicare beneficiaries deserve protection from these bad actors, and American taxpayers need to know the programs they rely on aren’t being bled dry by fraudsters.”

Like the original moratorium, the extension applies nationwide. However, the extension will allow currently enrolled hospice providers or home health agencies to add new practice locations to their existing enrollment in Medicare. Providers currently enrolled in Medicare will not be impacted and can continue to provide services to beneficiaries.

The Trump administration has issued a six-month moratorium on hospice and home health agencies enrolling in Medicare as part of its efforts to combat fraud.

The Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services said in an announcement on Wednesday morning that the "data-driven" decision targets a key source of fraud activity. It follows a similar announcement made earlier this year of a moratorium on durable medical equipment, prosthetics, orthotics and supplies companies.

During the six-month window, CMS said it will focus on identifying and investigating bad actors and deploying advanced analytics that help accelerate the removal of hospice and home health providers that are likely perpetrators of fraud.

CMS added that recent action under the broader White House fraud push has suspended payments to 773 hospice providers and 23 home health agencies in the Los Angeles area, totaling about $70 million in funds.

“We’ve seen systemic and deeply troubling fraud in the hospice and home health space, with bad actors exploiting some of our most vulnerable Medicare patients and stealing money from the American taxpayer,” CMS Administrator Mehmet Oz, M.D., said in the agency's press release. “Today we’re shutting the door on fraud—preventing new bad actors from entering Medicare while we aggressively identify, investigate and remove those already exploiting them."

"This is about protecting patients, restoring integrity and safeguarding taxpayer dollars," Oz said.

CMS said that the moratorium will not affect providers currently enrolled in Medicare, and they can continue to offer key services to members. It will apply, however, to new applicants for participation and "certain changes in majority ownership," which CMS said can be used to "obscure control by bad actors."

The moratoria will also prevent suspected fraudsters from evading punishment by moving across state lines, CMS said.

The announcement, the agency said, builds on a number of steps CMS has taken of late to address fraud in hospice and home healthcare, including increased oversight in states with elevated risk of fraud, such as Arizona, California, Georgia, Ohio, Nevada and Texas.

CMS is also conducting hospice site visits across the country to monitor operations and potentially identify suspicious behavior. In addition, it expanded a demonstration across several states—Florida, Illinois, Oklahoma, Ohio, North Carolina and Texas—that allowed for pre- and post-claim reviews of submissions from HHAs, which may stop improper payments before they're made.

The National Alliance for Care at Home, in a statement responding to the news, said it welcomes the administration's scrutiny and appreciates that "providers will still be able to conduct face-to-face recertification visits via telehealth during the enrollment moratorium, which will help avoid unnecessary care disruptions for patients and families." Still, it said the action raises concerns over access to care in high-demand areas and urged CMS to focus its efforts on "high-fraud markets" or actors without catching the rest of the sector in its net.

“CMS must use data-driven, risk-based program integrity measures and focus resources on boots-on-the-ground surveys and enforcement of existing oversight mechanisms that root out the blatantly bad actors without potentially limiting patient access to care or punishing high-quality providers operating in good faith," Alliance CEO Jennifer Sheets said in the statement.

Mortgage Rates Fall for Fourth Straight Week Despite Looming Fed Hike

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Mortgage rates continued their slow retreat for a fourth consecutive week, mirroring the trajectory of bond yields, although this week's hotter-than-expected inflation print suggests the relief will be short-lived.

The average rate on 30-year fixed home loans decreased to 7.11% for the week ending Nov. 12, down 7 basis points from 7.18% the week before, according to Freddie Mac. For perspective, rates averaged 6.24% one year ago.

"Mortgage rates dropped to their lowest level in seven weeks, a welcome development that gives borrowers greater flexibility as they navigate the current housing market," says Sam Khater, Freddie Mac's chief economist.

A month of falling mortgage rates comes as welcome news for homebuyers, though Realtor.com® economist Jiayi Xu warns that a reversal could come as soon as next week.

The stronger-than-forecast October jobs report showed the labor market holding up well, clearing the way for the Federal Reserve's next federal funds rate hike as part of its mandate to curb inflation.

Fed Governor Christopher Waller warned this week that a larger 50 basis point increase should be considered in December’s Federal Open Market Committee (FOMC) meeting if inflation remains hot.

Shortly after Waller's warning, the October Consumer Price Index report exceeded economists' expectations, pushing the 10-year Treasury yield toward 5%.

A December hike is now essentially priced in, with odds of a 25-basis-point increase topping 93%, according to CME FedWatch. The odds of another quarter-percentage-point hike in January 2027 currently stand at 67%.

"The debate is no longer if the Fed hikes, but how far and how fast," says Xu.

Today's homebuyers find themselves squeezed by both high mortgage rates and high inflation, and Xu contends that inflation is the bigger long-term problem.

"High rates raise monthly payments and shrink the loan a buyer can qualify for, and the recent pullback in pending sales shows how sensitive buyers are," notes the economist. "But persistent high inflation is what keeps the Fed hiking and rates elevated."

Crucially, inflation also erodes households' purchasing power, especially when wages lag consumer prices.

In October, existing-home sales dropped to their lowest level since Aug. 2010, according to the latest data  from the National Association of Realtors®.

In this economic environment, first-time buyers are hit the hardest because they have no existing equity to use as a cushion, forcing them to save toward a down payment from already-stretched budgets.

"Even if rates ease, living costs rising faster than pay will keep weighing on demand for years," says Xu.

Sellers have responded to cooling buyer demand by pulling back. This week, new listing activity fell 10.3% year over year after six weeks of more incremental declines, according to the latest weekly Realtor.com housing trends report.

"Sellers are discouraged because of how disengaged buyers are," says Realtor.com senior economist Hannah Jones. "The prices buyers are open to are less appealing to sellers than they were when mortgage rates were lower."

Your credit score plays a role when you apply for a mortgage. A credit score will determine whether you qualify for a mortgage and the interest rate you'll receive. The higher the credit score, the lower the interest rate you'll qualify for.

The credit score you need will vary depending on the type of loan. A score of 620 is a "fair" rating. However, people applying for a Federal Housing Administration loan might be able to get approved with a credit score of 500, which is considered a low score.

Homebuyers with credit scores of 740 or higher are typically considered to be in very good standing and can usually qualify for better rates, which can reduce monthly payments.

Snejana Farberov is a reporter at Realtor.com covering the U.S. housing market and the latest domestic real estate trends. She has worked as a general assignment journalist in New York City and Long Island for 16 years, writing for New York Post, Daily Mail, and News 12. Snejana earned bachelor's degrees in journalism and Italian from St. John's University, followed by a master’s degree from Columbia University School of Journalism.

Canaan Revenue Falls 90% as Bitcoin Holdings Take a Bigger Role in Capital Plans

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Strict editorial policy that focuses on accuracy, relevance, and impartiality

Morbi pretium leo et nisl aliquam mollis. Quisque arcu lorem, ultricies quis pellentesque nec, ullamcorper eu odio.

TL;DR: Canaan’s results put hardware sales, treasury assets and a newly announced share-buyback program under scrutiny. Operating performance and accounting gains tell different stories.

Canaan’s quarter calls for a closer look at which activities generate cash, which change reported earnings and which consume accumulated capital. A share repurchase, an equipment sale and a change in Bitcoin’s valuation can each affect shareholders through a different route.

For investors, the practical task is to follow three different activities: selling mining equipment, operating miners and managing accumulated digital assets. Each contributes to the company’s economics in a different way.

In its November 10 earnings release, Canaan reported $4.8 million in products revenue, versus $118.6 million a year earlier. Total revenue fell from $150.5 million to $14.5 million. The company attributed the equipment decline to lower computing power sold and weaker selling prices.

That combination matters. Selling fewer units affects scale, while lower prices can squeeze the amount available to cover development and overhead. A recovery in demand would therefore need to be assessed alongside margins, rather than judged solely by the number of machines shipped.

Canaan reported $8 million in net income alongside a $42.9 million operating loss. Its financial tables separately identify gains from cryptocurrency revaluation and financial derivatives. Those items help explain why the bottom line and the operating result point in different directions.

A rising value for assets already held can improve reported earnings without creating a matching amount of new cash. Equally, selling part of a reserve can generate cash while reducing future exposure to the asset. Neither observation makes the accounting result invalid; they explain why it is incomplete as a measure of trading health.

The contrast between revenue, backlog and losses at Bitdeer is another example of why mining-related companies need more than a headline earnings comparison.

Canaan’s September operating update, issued October 15, said it had monetized 3,952 ETH and 54.5 BTC for approximately $13.9 million. It also described repurchases funded through its existing program. Those sales were previously disclosed activity, not a new November transaction.

The latest release adds an initial $20 million repurchase program under a $100 million board authorization. Authorization gives management permission to act; it should not be confused with completed purchases of that amount.

Buying back shares and retaining Bitcoin involve competing uses of capital. A repurchase may benefit remaining holders if the price and funding choices are attractive, but it also uses resources that could support equipment development, liquidity or infrastructure. The decision needs to be judged on those terms, rather than treated as automatically bullish for either asset.

The company’s second-quarter report had already described reducing inventory, matching production to demand and preserving financial flexibility. It reported $31.9 million in revenue for that earlier period, showing that the latest result follows an existing slowdown.

NewsBTC’s account of MARA selling Bitcoin while developing AI infrastructure provides a related capital-allocation comparison, though the companies’ operating models differ.

The next useful checkpoints are cash generated by operations, actual repurchases and disclosed project commitments. A larger infrastructure ambition or an improved crypto valuation can change the investment story, but neither by itself demonstrates that demand for Canaan’s core products has recovered.

Nvidia's CEO Jensen Huang Resigns Following Infidelity Scandal

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Company produces custom processor for Switch 2

Nvidia, one of the largest tech firms in the world and the company responsible for the Switch 2's custom processor, has found itself embroiled in scandal over recent days.

As reported by The Guardian, Nvidia's CEO Jensen Huang, who you might recognise from the 'Creator's Voice Special Edition' video for Switch 2, has now resigned after reported affairs with an alleged Chinese spy and a company employee.

He will be replaced on an interim basis by Nvidia’s chief financial officer, Colette Kress, until a full-time successor is named. Nvidia's share price began to sharply decline following reports of the scandal, to the point where it is now no longer the most valuable company in the world.

The BBC speculates that Huang's departure could set him up to be a "formidable opponent" to the very company he co-founded, with Elon Musk stating that it would be "an honour" to work with him at SpaceX.

With regards to Nintendo, it's likely that none of this will have any immediate impact on Nvidia's relationship with the Japanese company. Nvidia will no doubt be under contract to supply custom chips for the Switch 2, but what happens with the inevitable Switch 2 successor is under question.

Hopefully everything will go smoothly for Nintendo, but if it wants to utilise Nvidia's architecture for its next console – particularly for backwards compatibility – then its efforts may be impacted by Huang's departure along with Nvidia's sharp pivot towards AI.

Of course, it's really early to be speculating about the 'Switch 3', but you can bet that Nintendo's R&D department will be looking at all of this very closely and considering its potential impact.

Ebenezer Review: Johnny Depp Neither Helps Nor Hurts This Passionless Retelling

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Charles Dickens' 1843 novella "A Christmas Carol" is one of the greatest stories ever written. It's a tale that is both very much of its time as well as timeless, speaking to systemic issues of cold-hearted greed and inequality as well as the ability to change one's ways for the better. It's a malleable text that can be calibrated for all ages, just as it can be told with everything from a lighthearted touch to a more dour and sinister horror-centric one.

This versatility has led it to become one of the most commonly adapted stories of all time. To put it mildly, we are not lacking for versions of Dickens' "Christmas Carol." That doesn't mean further adaptations shouldn't be made, but rather that the artists behind them should have a unique take on the material; like Shakespeare, it's well known enough to not have to play completely straight.

Yet playing it straight seems to be the entire ethos for "Ebenezer," the new adaptation from director Ti West and starring Johnny Depp as Scrooge. If a "Christmas Carol" adaptation isn't taking a big swerve with either a singing Will Ferrell or Muppets, for example, the general assumption is that some renowned actor will give their take on Scrooge, as luminaries like Alastair Sim, Albert Finney, and George C. Scott have done. That seems to be what West and company are assuming with Depp in the role, and it's likely why the rest of the film around him isn't taking too many big swings. Yet it leaves "Ebenezer" feeling a little too plain on the one hand, and potentially unsavory where Depp's personal status is considered. It's a movie that seems to exist on Depp's shoulders for better or worse, a choice that leaves it being technically competent but ultimately confused.

If you already know "A Christmas Carol," then "Ebenezer" will not hold many surprises for you. Ti West and screenwriter Nathaniel Halpern treat Charles Dickens' novella in much the same way the Royal Shakespeare Company might treat "Hamlet," which is to say that they retain the bulk of Dickens' dialogue and structure (the film is even separated into five staves, as in the novella) while coloring outside the lines ever so often. After an original opening sequence detailing the literal end of the partnership between Marley (Ian McKellen) and Ebenezer Scrooge (Johnny Depp), the film chronicles the usual beats of the tale, from establishing Scrooge's miserly, calloused attitude to the gradual thawing of his soul via his haunting by three Christmas ghosts.

Although Paramount Pictures' ghastly marketing campaign promised that "Ebenezer" would present a Scrooge we'd never seen before and go places other adaptations hadn't dared, the true extent of any changes or embellishments to the Dickens tale lies in the way that certain very small supporting characters who purport to either be in service of the needy or in need themselves are revealed to be greedy con artists.

The choice does add a nice little layer of moral complexity to what is typically a straightforward story, yet West and company don't do all that much with it in the end. Given his extensive indie horror background, West does lend the proceedings a welcome macabre aesthetic. This is certainly the spookiest "Christmas Carol" since Robert Zemeckis' bizarre 2006 version. The three ghosts are especially ghastly and eerie, and the whole film has a Victorian London by way of '70s British films feel to it, like if Ronald Neame's "Scrooge" was mashed up with West's "The Innkeepers."

While much of "Ebenezer" is solid and skillfully crafted, it's all clearly in service of the character and the performer at its center. To say that Johnny Depp brings some baggage to the role is to make an understatement, for even setting aside the considerable issue of his offscreen life, his onscreen persona would be its own monolith to reckon with. Especially in this material, as it blatantly conjures up his past successes with director Tim Burton.

What made the Depp/Burton duo special was how the director was able to draw out some truly bizarre character choices from the actor while making sure the film around him fit the outrageous, heightened tone of the performance. Here in "Ebenezer," Depp appears to be shooting more for respectable prestige than crowd-pleasing outrageousness. He's not trying to pull a Jack Sparrow, in other words, and instead is letting the prosthetic makeup do much of the transformation for him.

With the exception of a small moment here and there, Depp's Scrooge is largely solid yet unremarkable. Which begs the question: why bother in the first place? It seems like the entirety of "Ebenezer" was arranged to allow Depp to chew scenery and showboat around, which the actor perversely refuses to do. It's possible that Depp now considers such an approach something he's no longer interested in, and it's not like he hasn't given grounded performances before, like his turn as John Dillinger in "Public Enemies," for example. Depp's performance as Scrooge simply doesn't feel fully realized. It's fine on paper, and even has flashes of depth, yet it ultimately is more calibrated than inspired.

I have a deep affection for "A Christmas Carol," and generally find something to like in all of its many adaptations. In this way, "Ebenezer" is no different. Ti West, Nathaniel Halpern, Depp and the rest haven't twisted the story beyond recognition, nor have they sullied it. Yet in playing things so generally safe, "Ebenezer" becomes a muted film.

West throws in a flourish here and there, like the great work with the three ghost characters or a moment where Scrooge is abducted through his bed that deliberately recalls Depp's cinematic debut in "A Nightmare on Elm Street." There's even an extended denouement to the tale which stands in contrast to most versions of "A Christmas Carol," where instead of rushing through Scrooge's change of heart, the film takes its time as the man makes his reparations.

Yet even that choice feels calculated in a meta sense to better present Johnny Depp, Movie Star, back to the public after his cancellation. After all, this story is famously one about a loathsome man who learns contrition and seeks to make amends, and while the film itself never breaks the fourth wall with this idea, it comes damn close. Even with that held back, it feels like "Ebenezer" is more a proving ground than impassioned adaptation, with West demonstrating he can do more than just indie horror, and Depp showing more of his restraint as a performer. What will happen to Depp the person is not for me to say, but he's going to have to work harder and take more risks than "Ebenezer" if he wants to give his screen career a second chance.

"Ebenezer" opens in theaters on November 13, 2026.
