‘Ebenezer’ Review: Johnny Depp Is Absolutely Insufferable in a Dull and Dreary Christmas Carol

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

A Sauvage and jowly overactor in dire need of career rehabilitation could hardly ask for a better part to play than Ebenezer Scrooge. He’s a cruel miser of a man who comes to learn the error of his ways over the course of a ghost story that preys upon Christian precepts of redemption and forgiveness (along with the more secular warm and fuzzies of seeing a bad guy discover the joys of being good). Indeed, what is “cancel culture” if not a shackled delusion that visits self-made pariahs in the dead of night, and what is Charles Dickens’ “A Christmas Carol” if not a plea to believe that even the most odious of souls are still capable of being saved? Related Stories Sandra Wollner’s ‘Everytime’ Is a Stunning Film About Grief Like You’ve Never Seen Before How ‘Godzilla Minus Zero’ Dominated the Box Office: GKIDS Chief on IMAX’s Impact and the Growing Market for Foreign-Language Movies

It’s been reported that Johnny Depp accepted the title role of Ti West’s “Ebenezer” at a fraction of his usual rate, but I suspect that he probably would have done this movie for free. While the embattled star earned more than $250 million for his work on the “Pirates of the Caribbean” franchise, even that psychotic number pales in comparison to the pricelessness of starring in a major studio Christmas movie that allows him to look just left of the camera and say lines like “I’m not the man I was!” and “I was once good, and I know I can be again!” before his character saves a cherubic little boy from certain death and invites a freezing beggar inside for a warm cup of tea. If an old crank like Scrooge can make his way back toward grace, why not the convicted star of “Mortdecai,” and the alleged perpetrator of several even more serious crimes?

Johnny Depp may prefer for that to be framed as a rhetorical question, but his insufferable turn as literature’s most cantankerous moneylender demands an honest answer. For me, that answer is rather straightforward: While Depp has never been the subtlest of actors, his post-Sparrow pivot away from artistic curiosity has distended his talents into such a grotesque caricature of their former charm that “Ebenezer” left me feeling like I may never be able to empathize with his screen presence again.

Scrooge is obviously a scenery-chewer’s dream, but the crescent arc he traces from grinch to Christmas miracle is only believable enough to sustain Dickens’ fable if you believe it on both ends. Depp’s performance — an advent calendar of empty affectations without a soul to anchor them, every shot full of face and bereft of feeling — makes that exceedingly difficult. His offscreen unsavoriness certainly doesn’t help, but there’s a more immediate problem when the lights go down, one that makes him a unique case among Hollywood’s tainted array of mega-star actors: He seems to have become very bad at acting. Whatever my feelings about Depp as a human being, the truth is that I didn’t buy him as a glowering monster any more than I bought him as a beaming man who’s grown “better than his word.”

An unengaging Ebenezer is a tough break for a movie called “Ebenezer,” and screenwriter Nathaniel Halpern’s fidelity to the source material doesn’t exactly leave West with a wealth of opportunities to compensate for such fatal miscasting. West’s take on the holiday classic is faithful to the core — no Muppets, no ghosts of girlfriends past, no uncanny mo-capped Jim Carrey. As you might expect from the director of indie genre standouts like “Pearl” and “The House of the Devil,” West’s big idea is to scare the Dickens out of us by turning up the volume on this story’s implicit scariness. Sometimes literally, as this PG-13 carol offers a small handful of gentle jump-scares.

“Ebenezer” isn’t a horror movie in the strictest sense, but West does what he can to play up the fear that eventually terrifies Scrooge into giving a shit about his fellow man. Most — or all — of those flourishes are purely cosmetic in nature, and hellbent on sucking every glimmer of life out of Victorian England. Scrooge’s office has never looked more haunted, as Checco Varese’s grimdark cinematography and Eve Stewart’s Yharnam-esque production design combine to create a London that’s in dire need of Christmas cheer (the vibe should go a long way with tweenage audiences eager for a bit of spice with their holiday viewing, though I suspect the whole thing is too staid and oppressive to become any kind of perennial favorite).

And since West would sooner die than pass up a chance for death, this version of the story treats us to a musty prologue where we actually get to see Ebenezer’s business partner bite the dust with his mouth wide open (Marley is played by Ian McKellen in what currently stands as the most “remember ‘The Christophers?’” performance of the year). From there, the action skips forward to Christmas Eve 1843, whence Ebenezer is inflicting his usual “Bah, humbug!” routine on poor Bob Cratchit (Rupert Grint), the underpaid clerk he resents for taking the next day off (HR being a decidedly 20th-century invention). But the fates have plans of their own for the bitter-cold businessman, who promptly pisses himself when the ghost of old Marley bursts into his miserable bedchamber later that night.

Reaction shots ensue, as Depp curdles his face into a series of the most exaggerated expressions since the death of silent cinema, every one of them betraying his character’s frigidness. This Ebenezer takes an active pleasure in being cruel, which not only makes him more detestable (which is fine), but also endows him with an innate capacity for joy that makes the rest of the movie feel like it’s walking in circles (which is less fine). Given that Depp’s performance is only as wide as his lips and as deep as his pockets, it seems wasteful that we should have to walk through the hollows of Ebenezer’s past, present, and future in order to fix him — it would be easier and equally meaningful to just push the corners of his mouth into a smile.

Of course, that would deprive us of the “holy shit, that’s Andrea Riseborough” fun of the “To Leslie” star’s Ghost of Christmas Past, a white-eyed ghoul who looks like a spirit from “The Conjuring” who popped up for a sudden jolt and then forgot to leave the frame. She’s the most amusing part of a familiar tour through the sadness of Ebenezer’s childhood, where he was bullied by the other kids and kept apart from his beloved older sister. Tramell Tillman is equally compelling as the boisterous Ghost of Christmas Present, though his bizarre character design — a round-bellied man whose face appears to be rotting to pieces in real-time — turns out to be reverse-engineered from an unnerving reveal that both honors Dickens’ text and also underlines West’s limited conviction to rejuvenate it with the visceral immediacy of a horror film (his commitment to the bit falls well short of the Robert Eggers-level follow-through that conceit demands).

Given the emphasis the director puts on the film’s genre spin, it’s ironic and surprising that “Ebenezer” is at its best when it focuses on the most basic human elements of its story, if only because the supporting characters offer such a welcome contrast to whatever Depp is doing. Sam Claflin and Daisy Ridley don’t have a whole lot to do as Ebenezer’s kind-hearted nephew Fred and his wife Clara (respectively), but the film takes pains to stress how sad it is that Scrooge is Fred’s last connection to his dead mother, and it’s touching to see how much it would mean for him to have a cordial relationship with the old miser.

It is considerably less touching to see Ebenezer arrive at a mutual feeling after palling around with the film’s Nazgûl-coded Ghost of Christmas Yet to Come (Robert Strange, cloaked in CGI), who forces the misanthrope to face the emptiness of his own miserable existence. “The flame of humanity still flickers within you,” someone tells Scrooge, but the slapstick pageantry of Depp’s performance sabotages the movie by keeping that flame at a low simmer from the start. I didn’t believe the fire had ever gone out any more than I believed that it was eventually rekindled, as this Ebenezer Scrooge is far too elastic and self-amused to convincingly suggest that he’s experiencing a permanent change of heart. He can’t even sustain a facial expression for more than a few seconds before he starts mugging around in search of another one.

There will, of course, be those who insist that I only panned “Ebenezer” because Johnny Depp is in it, and I suppose they’re at least half-right about that, if not quite for the reason they assume. The truth is that it would be a lot more interesting and true to the spirit of Dickens’ classic if I liked this movie in spite of Depp rather than disliking it because of him, as I can’t help but share the author’s opinion that everyone is theoretically capable of redemption, be it measure by measure or miraculously complete.

Alas, the actor’s “comeback” performance is just yet another thing that he ought to be sorry for.

Paramount Pictures will release “Ebenezer” in theaters on Friday, November 13.

The Wild Stories Behind Trapped Under Ice’s 2007 Demo

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Almost 20 years ago, Baltimore’s Trapped Under Ice released their first demo — a record that would sow the seeds, in many ways, of modern hardcore. After all, it launched the career of one of the genre’s most dynamic frontmen, Justice Tripp of Angel Du$t, and later featured Turnstile‘s Brendan Yates on drums.

Now, the story behind the creation of that demo has finally been told — via the upcoming book “Intent to Distribute: Twenty Years of Flatspot Records,” out Feb. 5, 2027. The book, written by journalist Fred Pessaro, contains exclusive photos, artwork, and interviews with bands that the label launched, including Tripp, Backtrack, Terror, Speed, End It, and Scowl. Rolling Stone has the first excerpt, about TUI’s demo, below.

“When you spend so much time doing something, you’re usually focused on what’s next. You’re putting out the next record, planning the next campaign, working on the next show,” says label co-founder Ricky Singh. “You don’t always stop to look at everything that’s happened along the way. Making this book gave us an opportunity to do that. It’s a collection of people, bands, friendships and experiences that have shaped Flatspot over the last 20 years.”

Adds co-founder Che Figueroa-Rodriguez: “Throughout the last 20 years there has been a lot of blood and sweat and the driving force that carried us through every era of the label was that same passion we had when we started.”

That passion will be on full display at an anniversary show on Feb. 6 at New York’s Webster Hall, featuring performances by Trapped Under Ice, Breakdown, End It, Raw Brigade, Iron Mind, and Frothing Mad.

The Breakdown and Outburst demos in ’87. Beyond’s Dew it! and Turning Point’s demo in ’88. Merauder’s ’93 demo. All signs point toward Trapped Under Ice’s 2007 demo being named in the same breath as some of these legendary early efforts. Depending on the era, it’s quite possible that the record known as FSR02 could go down as the single most important hardcore demo of all time. Flatspot Records proudly released this five-song monster on vinyl after witnessing its creation in Joe Mitra’s basement so many years ago, and it has remained a crucial part of the catalog ever since. The record, initially paid for in stolen goods and later pressed with drug money, proved to be not only a major landmark for hardcore in the Mid-Atlantic, but for the genre writ large, causing a monolithic shift in sound and style. Editor’s picks The 250 Greatest Albums of the 21st Century So Far The 100 Best TV Episodes of All Time The 500 Greatest Albums of All Time 100 Best Movies of the 21st Century

No one, especially TUI vocalist Justice Tripp and Flatspot founder Che Figueroa, could have predicted the sea change that these songs would put into motion. But now with hindsight, it’s hard to argue the depth and breadth of influence caused by this gang of kids from one of the most dangerous cities in America — the waves in the water are still being felt. We sat down with Tripp to discuss what circumstances surrounded the demo and just how a bunch of “bad kids” ended up creating such seminal work. What follows is the story of Trapped Under Ice’s early days and everything that came with it, from rodent-infested homes to cocaine bricks to fights with tasers.

Trapped Under Ice was created from the ashes of Nick Fury. What was the genesis of the band? Nick Fury’s big creative impression was that we were a teenage straight edge band, but we were all reaching the age of 20 and our interests shifted. So me, Sam [Trapkin], and Ben [Esparza] decided to try something different and we were set on doing heavier music. The creative ideas came out of lifting sessions — me and Sam would lift in his hot garage and we only had one CD for the player: State of the World Address by Biohazard. I think we decided that the band should sound like us in our underwear, sweating, listening to State of the World Address.  Related Content Remix Albums Are Helping Pop Acts Live in the Moment Longer — And Feel Less Alone Turnstile Raise Over $250,000 for Charity at Massive Free Washington D.C. Show Turnstile Announce Free Washington Monument Show for Charity ‘SNL’: Watch Turnstile Perform ‘Birds,’ Medley From Album ‘Never Enough’

As far as influence, I hear classic NYHC like Breakdown, Killing Time, Crown of Thornz…Breakdown and Crown of Thornz, yeah. Madball was a subtle influence, so I would say maybe some of that Killing Time is through their influence on Madball. We always get Outburst, too, but I think when people think of them, they’re hearing Breakdown or the similarities between the two. I don’t think I even heard Outburst until we put out the TUI demo.

How did you arrive at the band name? Does it come from the classic Metallica song?It was Sam’s idea and at the time, it wasn’t consciously because of a Metallica song. We didn’t know that was the source of inspiration until the first time we saw it on a flyer and thought, “Makes sense, Sam’s a Metallica freak.” Also, I was writing lyrics before we had a name and there was a theme of “this is cold” and “that’s cold.” So when the name came about, it was like, “Oh, this fits the lyrics. We’re gonna run with that.”

Did “Stay Cold” exist before the name Trapped Under Ice?“Stay Cold” was an early song we wrote and actually, I do believe that was before we had named the band. We hadn’t recorded the song yet, but we had that song written for a while before we recorded it for the EP that came out later.

So you did the demo before you started playing shows?Yeah, we wanted to make sure we had music out before actually doing it live.

What were the details of recording the demo?  We recorded the demo in our friend’s basement — Joe Mitra’s Wired Wrists Studio. I remember we recorded and the snare drum sounded kind of crazy. So he was like, “I wanna try this,” and we’re all young and learning stuff. We didn’t really know what we were doing and were all experimenting in our own ways, and Joe ended up tweaking the snare drum. At the time, it didn’t bother me too much — I was like, “Oh, it’s weird.” I didn’t really know or care or think too much — it was just “is there a mosh part that makes me feel something?” After the vinyl came out, a friend of mine said, “I’m really sorry about that snare.” He thought I was upset about the drums and I was like, “Oh man, is something wrong with it?” As time goes on, you become more familiar with what things are supposed to sound like, but I think the snare is one of those things that makes it special. It has this alien sound where people are like, “Wow, these guys are from another planet.”

So you guys are recording the demo — clearly, you’re very young. How did you pay for the recording?We were definitely bad kids at that time and he probably charged us like $500 bucks to record the demo— probably $100 a song or whatever. We didn’t have that at the time, so we would just do shit that kids did — rob people and stuff. That’s not cool. I feel like at that time, we got into going to parties and if the vibe was cool, we were cool and then if it wasn’t, we were robbing people that were mean to us. And we basically funded recording that way: stole stuff from somebody’s house and the things we stole paid for our demo. You know, I’m surprised we never got locked up or killed or something.

How did “Soul Vice” come about? Was it originally an intro?  So when I wrote that song and riff, I remember thinking I wanted it to be an intro, so it’s structurally not like a verse and a chorus. I just wanted a cool riff that sounds like Breakdown, that fucking hits you with the mosh part.

Lyrically, I wanted to be kind of vague, but I think it’s like a cool summary of my family life, which was a little crazy, and I had just moved out — I was actually 17. I was learning to deal with living on my own and adulthood and having anxiety problems and then I was kind of thrown into a world where I didn’t have a support system. It was just me and my friends. I didn’t feel like I had anybody I could depend on. I was learning about loss, people dying around you, and just trying to process all these feelings at once. And now all these adult things are being thrown at me and I can’t deal with them all at once.

“Reality Unfolds” is such a good example of a certain thing that TUI does musically where you basically just tell Sam to write something fun. He always does this — overnight he’s like, “Hey, I wrote this last night,” and it’s what “Reality Unfolds” became.

Did Sam come up with some of the vocal cadences or did you do that?So, I do a lot of that but that’s one thing I like about Sam. He’s very producer-minded. A lot of times he can come up with a whole song musically and then I’ll riff on it but he’ll say, “I like when you did this,” or “Can you do that here? I don’t like this.” And he’ll give me fun challenges like, “I like this word, what if you put this word in the song somewhere?” I think he’s a really aware person.

I met Sam at a young age, and he’s from a different area and could observe me really well. He was really conscious of my voice, my accent and the way I talk. I don’t think I talk like a lot of people, especially before I started touring a lot — hearing my voice and accent in a microphone and being like, “Oh, I sound like Baltimore trash.” So he would play off of that and be like, “Hey man, can you say this word in the song?” I’d be like, “Why do you want me to say that word so bad?” And then you hear it — it’s because he thought it was funny I say it that way. He would always play off my accent and I think that’s a funny early TUI thing.

If you think about how those songs are structured, there are many parts where the music drops out and there will be a short vocal solo — it’s a very pop song approach. TUI’s got a lot of that. Where do you think that came from? Yeah, I love that, man. Those are moments that we were obsessed with, and at first we were like, “We should do that in every song.” Biohazard has a couple of those moments. A great example is on “Punishment” where he’s like, “I question, not me…” — that whole section. Another one is “March of the Damned” by Integrity where Dwid says, “Father! Take me back!”

That’s another thing that producer-minded Sam really emphasized — people love relating to music through the vocals. It’s what we all sing along to. I think there’s a more direct reference for that one: “Big Kiss Goodnight.” I showed him this riff I wanted to use and just like with “Reality Unfolds,” Sam came in the next day with “Big Kiss Goodnight” entirely written. My part was the simplest, dumbest riff on the whole record and he just made this crazy song. That includes the part where everything rings out and “you just say some cool-ass shit.”

How did the original cover for the demo come about? Was that originally intended to be a promo photo? Kind of? We knew we wanted a promo photo and there’s these bands we loved that were big scary guys. I think we went into it knowing that we weren’t big and scary. We couldn’t compete with the Fury of Five promo photo or the Box Cutter promo photo or Madball. We weren’t intimidating people and we took the photo as seriously as possible because we were referencing the stuff that we love. But the idea that we were acting like that was funny. It’s weird because I remember people were really upset like, “These guys think they’re so tough.” That’s the whole point — we’re not and that’s why it’s cool.

We just wanted it to be a cool statement of “here’s the promo photo, that’s the cover.” I remember Sam making the logo for that and I think it has icicles coming off it. We knew that was stupid — we’re not hiding around it. Self-aware is a good way to put it.

What’s the house that you guys are sitting in front of?It’s on Clinton Street in Highland Town at a house I lived in — me and a couple of friends. It was kind of the hangout spot. Some old Polish family that I knew used the house for storage and there was no hot water or heat or air conditioning. It was really sad. There were holes in the floor, so Baltimore-sized rats lived with us. The homies would come over and crash or go out and be debaucherous and that was where you all met after the fight breaks out or after the police get called. The neighborhood at the time was mainly El Salvadorans, who were really cool neighbors to have. I think they appreciated and supported our little active bad kid lifestyle.

What was the deal with the limited cover for the test press?We had the limited cover that was like a brick of cocaine and it said Trapped Under Ice written in lines of cocaine. When Che told me we could do that, I was like, “Oh, we can get like baking powder or something to do that with.” Che was like, “We could just use this,” and he pulled out a brick of cocaine. And then he did it, it looked cool and I was like, “That’s fucking sick — nobody else has that.”

One of your early members, Daniel Klipa, is the son of Daniel Higgs, the legendary artist, tattooer and member of Lungfish. How did you link with Klipa to form an early version of TUI? I think we recorded before he actually started practicing with us. He played the first shows that we did. Klipa was a local guy who grew up in a white trash neighborhood and just did white trash shit. The only hardcore he was aware of was Hatebreed and Icepick, so we were like, “We can convince him to do this by telling him it sounds like Icepick.” Every day with Klipa is like 20 stories. One day we went to practice and we called him and he said, “I moved to Florida.” He got kicked out of Florida for stealing purses, so the next week when it was time to practice, he was back in Maryland so he practiced with us. He’s a character, like out of a movie.

You talked about debaucherous evenings that started on Clinton Street. Give me an idea of what one of those might entail. There was a group of people that we were feuding with and I remember one night we found out that they were all squadded up somewhere, so we called all the boys. When Klipa got to my house, he walked across the street to the El Salvadoran family that we’re friends with and spoke to them in fluent Spanish, which none of us knew he could do. They gave him some beers and five bucks. He told them that he was really broke and needed gas to get somewhere to fight these guys, so they gave it to him. We drove to go fight these guys, found them and fought. So when we came back to the crib, the neighbors were like, “What happened? Did you beat their ass?” They were really excited about it.

Things definitely got a little out of hand and went further than anybody anticipated a couple times. Sometimes weapons got used, and at the time our weapon of choice was tasers. Everybody had tasers because we thought it was funny — if you tase somebody, sometimes they would pee themselves. And that was funny to be like, “Hey, we made this guy piss his pants and that’s how we won the fight.”

What were some of the early shows for TUI?  So I think the first show TUI played was a Ruiner show. Van Damage was a band in Pennsylvania and at the time, we would play with them a bit. Down to Nothing would invite us to play with them. And then a lot of local shows, like one a week for a long time. We were thinking we’re just gonna do a Baltimore show or two and then we kept getting asked to play out of the state, which was really exciting. When you’re a kid, you don’t have any intentions of leaving the state. I think Lemoyne, Pennsylvania, would have been our first out-of-state show. Brockton, Massachusetts, was also an early adopter of Trapped Under Ice.

It’s kind of also the time when Title Fight was becoming a real band. So we played a show in Wilkes-Barre with Title Fight. It was crazy. I remember thinking, people really like this band — they’re gonna do really well. They obviously exceeded whatever I thought they would do. Later, I became aware of a new band called Bad Seed that was just the members of Title Fight with the exception of another friend or two. And it was very much similar in influence to Trapped Under Ice. They’re all a little younger than us and from what they told me, they were like, ”Oh, you can make music like that and people are into it?” So they started Bad Seed. They were a band that we would play with a lot in the beginning, too.

For those early shows, did you have enough material to play in addition to the demo? Did you have any covers that you’d play? We had a cover of “Last Breath” by Hatebreed that we would do sometimes. We’d also do a little Metallica medley, “Street Fight” by Breakdown. I think that’s it. Our set was songs from the demo and then one of those.

Let’s talk about the Baltimore and D.C. scenes around the time of TUI’s ascension. What was it like then? Definitely much smaller, I’d imagine, but there were several bands from the area like Gut Instinct and Next Step Up. Between Virginia and Maryland, D.C., and Baltimore, there were so many different waves that were happening. Desperate Measures was really important to our friend circle. They were one of those bands that made it feel cool to be from Maryland, which wasn’t the coolest thing. I always say Slumlords was really cool because they were one of those first bands to be a Baltimore band. There were a couple bands from Baltimore, but nobody was wearing it like that. And I think that was big with TUI — we wanted to be a Baltimore hardcore band. We’re gonna put Baltimore on our merchandise and make it a cool thing because it really never was cool.

Honestly, I think Stout is the most important band in Baltimore hardcore history. So you got Gut Instinct, which is a little more traditional hardcore and influenced a lot of early hardcore bands. And then there was Next Step Up, which is more of the groovy thing that really influenced bands like Hatebreed. And then Stout was like, the next level and evolution of heavy music where it just sounds demonic and experimental on some level. But it’s like, hardcore dudes who really like Pentagram and are trying to see how hard they can make music. Definitely a big thing that TUI took from.

In hardcore, there are a lot of vocalists, but not many front people. Most vocalists lack that certain kind of “pied piper-ism” you possess. What are the qualities that make a good front person? Who were your pied pipers?I appreciate that, thank you. So, the obvious one that people caught before I did was Ezec from Crown of Thornz. He is such a character. He’s not bluffing one bit. That’s just who he is and you can’t turn it off. When I was a kid I was like, “I wanna be a unique individual,” and he’s one of the first people to turn me on to individuality in the context of heavy music. He doesn’t follow any of the rules.

I feel like there were two bands who were singing about their feelings, Crown of Thornz and American Nightmare — two bands from different generations. That’s one of the things people really loved about American Nightmare and made them a unique and very artistic band. I think neither of those bands were particularly focused on being heavy in the way that TUI was. I think our unique angle was to be like, “I got my feelings hurt by this girl“ or “I’m feeling abandoned by my father” on a song with a riff that sounds like Madball. But the ultimate lesson as a frontman in rock & roll music is Iggy Pop. That’s my number one. There are more obvious examples too, like Prince is really important to me.

When we first started playing, I remember Sam asking me about the way I moved onstage, shaking my hips and stuff. He was like, “I don’t know if that fits the vibe,” and I said, “That’s why it’s cool.” And to his credit, Sam might not always understand what I’m doing, but I’d explain myself and he’d be like, “Let’s run with it.” I’ve always done that — I always wanna get on stage and be flamboyant and a spectacle. I wanna move in a way that makes you uncomfortable. That’s not just hardcore music, that’s rock & roll music. I wanna engage and challenge people. Trending Stories Sade Are Not Attending Rock & Roll Hall of Fame Induction Ceremony The ‘You Can See Everything’ Interview You’ve Been Waiting For Bob Dylan’s Meeting With Pope Leo: People Fixated on the Hood, But the Hands Spoke Volumes Megan Thee Stallion and Daniel Radcliffe Reunite for Season Two of ‘Reggie Dinkins’

When the demo came out, it was kind of seismic — it really reverberated throughout the scene. When did you start to notice that the reaction was bigger than “pretty good for a local”?So Trash Talk is the first band to bring TUI out on a U.S. tour. We had just a demo out at the time, so it was kind of a risky thing for them. They were so successful and they stood up for us. They were like, “It’s gonna be this band. It doesn’t matter if they draw or not, this needs to happen.” And I remember they were beefing with their agent at the time. He was like, “I got this band and they have the numbers,” and they were like, ”We don’t give a fuck about the numbers.” It was really cool and humbling to have people I admired riding for us. So when we went out I thought, “We just fucked their tour up.” But every show was just insane.

When we played the Che Cafe in San Diego, Patrick Kitzel sent us 200 Stay Cold 7-inches — it had just come out. We sold all of them that night. The show was sold out and it was crazy. I don’t even know if that venue holds 200 people, but we sold 200 records. The guys who ended up becoming Take Offense were there and they were going wild. And then we played L.A. and people I looked up to who played in cool bands showed up, particularly like Jeremy Bolm from Touche Amore and Luis from Alpha & Omega. It’s still a lot of the same people I see when I go to L.A. and that’s insane.

Apple Sports adds UFC events with real-time stats and results

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Apple continues to expand the appeal of its free Sports app, which focuses on delivering real-time sports scores and stats, across many different sports.

Today, Apple is adding UFC into the mix, so MMA fans can track the fights live through the unified Apple Sports experience.

Apple Sports will provide coverage of every bout across the full fight card, with live stats like total strike counts, takedowns, knockdowns and more.

Apple Sports features animated background artwork to differentiate different games and matches in the detail view, and UFC is no different with a custom backdrop that evokes the ambience of being under the lights of the Octagon. The details card also includes new visualization to illustrate the number of recorded strikes by target.

You can keep up with the action inside the app, or tap the clock icon to start a Live Activity and get score updates delivered directly to your iPhone’s lock screen.

Apple Sports now comes preinstalled when setting up a new iPhone from scratch. If you don’t already have it, you can get it as a free download from the App Store.

The Sports app is still only available on iPhone right now though; Apple has still not announced plans to do an iPad and Mac native version. You can have the Sports Live Activities from your iPhone show up on Apple Watch and Mac, however.

CF: Instalments and repeatable bonuses – the exact figure Milan paid for Goncalo Ramos

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Other  |  Read Source →

Today, more information about the structure and cost of Goncalo Ramos’ move to AC Milan has been revealed.

It’s fair to say that Ramos’ start to life with Milan has been poor. The Portuguese striker has not scored (or assisted) as many as had been hoped from a player worth a substantial amount and it’s fair to suggest that some have been disappointed with his time at San Siro so far.

Read SempreMilan ad-free and get access to exclusive news. Click here for a free trial!

However, the club will not be giving up on him anytime soon, considering he is their record arrival.

This evening, Calcio e Finanza have looked at the fee paid for Ramos – as they did for Diego Moreira recently – and picked apart both the fee, the bonuses and the structure of the payment, as MilanNews relays.

In terms of the fixed fee, the Rossoneri agreed on a deal worth €70 million, with an extra €3.1m in additional costs. This figure will be paid over the course of four years, with yearly payments scheduled on July 15 on each year – ending in July 2029 – and each payment will be worth €17.5m.

Then, there is €16m in bonuses that can be achieved, taking the total fee to almost €90m if all bonuses are achieved.

If Milan win the Scudetto, €2m will be owed; then there are several Champions League bonuses, which could be worth up to €5.2m if the Diavolo win the competition. Finally, if he is the top goalscorer in Serie A, another €2m will be owed to PSG.

These bonuses are capped at €16m and are repeatable, but they’ll only be redeemable if the Portuguese plays 60% of games in that season, unless he is the top scorer, where only the €2m will be owed.

YouTuber Dami Lee Signs With CAA (Exclusive)

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The architect-turned-digital storyteller founded Nollistudio with Raffaele di Nicola to use online videos to talk about buildings and cities, real and imagined, in Hollywood movies.

Architect Dami Lee, who uses her expertise to bring niche design concepts to a wider audience through digital storytelling, has signed with CAA for representation.

With around 2.5 million followers for her YouTube channel, Lee’s recent videos include Is Chongqing REALLY a Cyberpunk City?, which has over 3 million views, and another two million views for Why Lord of the Rings Architecture Isn’t Just Fantasy… It’s a Warning.

CAA will also represent Nollistudio, a video production banner Lee co-founded with filmmaker Raffaele di Nicola, to bring creator content to YouTube and other platforms. The Hollywood talent agency will work with Lee and Nollistudio to expand their creator economy business and YouTube channel across all areas.

Lee’s cinematic storytelling explores buildings or cities, whether from history and imagined environments, that audiences can potentially see with new eyes and fresh opinions. That includes video content that plays off architecture as seen through the lens of film, TV and popular culture.

Lee has explored beyond design concepts through the fictional landscapes of Dune, as with The Hidden Secrets of Dune’s Darkest Planet, and Lord of the Rings.

And Nollistudio also explores real-world architecture and cities that have shaped our world, as with popular YouTube videos about ancient water holes beneath the Iranian desert and new theories about Egyptian pyramids.

“Their multidisciplinary team combines design, research and filmmaking to explore how the built environment shapes our lives,” CAA said in a statement about Nollistudio’s video output.

Phota Labs uses AI to turn everyday photos into professional-looking shots

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

A new iOS app called Phota Labs gives users access to a personal AI photo assistant that turns everyday snapshots into images that look like they were taken by a professional photographer, all while preserving the authenticity of the image.

Rather than giving consumers yet another photo-editing tool, Phota Labs aims to use advances in AI to automate the editing process so it’s easier for anyone to get access to better photos.

The a16z-backed startup was founded by Cecilia Zhang and Zach Xia, who met at Adobe while working on computational photography products, including Project Indigo, Lightroom, and Photoshop.

The duo saw a gap in the editing tools on the market and believed that although editing tools can be powerful for enhancing photos, they aren’t very effective at fixing a photo that wasn’t captured well in the first place. Phota Labs changes that by focusing on fixing the shot itself while preserving the natural appearances of those in the image.

“Phota is your personal photo assistant that can make great photos for any moment you want to keep before you even ask, which means that it’s a very proactive experience,” Zhang told TechCrunch in an interview. “Think of it almost like a photographer or virtual photographer who looks over your camera roll and tries to understand what’s happening and how they could have taken that photo for you on the spot for that scene.”

The idea is to use AI to transform photos, whether that’s turning a wide-angle group photo into a tighter portrait or transforming a selfie into a regular shot.

Users can choose from different photography styles that determine how AI transforms their images, ranging from subtle edits that preserve the original lens and composition to more dramatic transformations that introduce effects like fisheye lenses, alternative angles, and entirely different perspectives.

While some people turn to ChatGPT and other generative AI tools to enhance or edit images, these tools can often alter a person’s appearance, especially when working with blurry photos because they don’t know what that person actually looks like.

“For Phota, we built our own tech,” Zhang said. ”We built this personalization technology that actually learns about people’s identity and is able to preserve them in any transformation we do to a photo.”

Images edited with Phota Labs feature invisible watermarks that identify them as altered by AI when shared on social platforms.

The startup is initially targeting people who take tons of photos and like to share them on social media, but don’t want to deal with the tedious process of editing photos. Over time, the founders envision a broader audience, from retirees looking to create beautiful photos for their grandchildren to parents who want to share polished family albums with friends and relatives.

“We have this obsession with authenticity,” Xia said. “I think there’s so much value in connecting AI with real moments and making sure those photos are not perfect, in a sense. That’s why we spend a lot of research into understanding personal context, into building this identity model because we want to make sure the photos are authentic. They’re just better photos of the same moment, rather than saying, ‘Oh, this is an AI image’.”

Xia said beta users were excited to see better versions of moments they had actually lived and saw the results as photos of real memories rather than AI-generated images. The founders found that users were taking more photos because they knew Phota Labs could enhance them later.

“We’re turning something that’s unusable into something that people want to print,” Zhang said. “People who just love photos but never really got into editing can now experience professional quality that could never have been possible. So, I think that meant so much to us. There’s so many cherished moments for photos, and we really want them to be actually meaningful and something that you can remember.”

In terms of funding, the company raised a $5.6 million seed round led by a16z last year.

Phota Labs is launching as a free app to build an initial user base before exploring a freemium model. The startup also plans to launch an Android app in the future.

More game developers are starting to use generative AI

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

The GDC Festival of Gaming’s latest annual industry survey shows an increase in AI usage among game developers.﻿

The GDC Festival of Gaming’s latest annual industry survey shows an increase in AI usage among game developers.﻿

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

More video game developers are using generative AI tools in their jobs compared to last year, according to the GDC Festival of Gaming’s latest annual industry survey. Forty percent of developers surveyed say they use it “as part of their job,” an increase from 36 percent in 2025. Usage was higher with game developers that work on free-to-play games at 58 percent and mobile-only studio workers at 70 percent.

The survey is being published as the game industry continues to reckon with generative AI tools. While many game developers, particularly indie developers, have spoken out strongly against the technology, AI is at the center of indie awards conversations in part because Big Walk developer House House “sparingly” used LLMs to assist with technical challenges in the “final stretch” of development. AI is also increasingly being used to reverse-engineer retro games. And in a post on Thursday, Xbox CEO Asha Sharma seemed to indicate that AI will be central to Xbox’s future, saying that “A small team will be able to build worlds that today demand hundreds of developers and hundreds of millions of dollars.”

Seventy three percent of the survey’s respondents said they used AI for research and brainstorming, while 62 percent used it for data analysis and summarization, and 56 percent for code generation and debugging. Twenty two percent of respondents used generative AI for concept art or visual ideation, and 8 percent used it for production art or final game assets.

The survey also included grim numbers about layoffs, with 34 percent of respondents saying they have been laid off sometime in the past two years.

Apple Sports adds live UFC updates

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

The app can deliver real-time stats for each fight on a UFC card.

UFC fans now have another way to keep up with the action. The Apple Sports app will provide live updates from the MMA organization's events, starting with this Saturday's UFC 334 card. That event will feature two major title fights. Ciryl Gane will defend the Heavyweight Championship against Josh Hokit and Amanda Nunes will challenge Kayla Harrison for the Women's Bantamweight Championship.

If you're not able to tune into UFC 334 (or future events) on Paramount+, you can keep up to date with the action via "enhanced data visualizations and dynamic on-screen animations" on Apple Sports. You'll be able to see the tale of the tape and fighter rankings before each bout, including prelims. Once the fights are underway, you can get real-time updates, including on stats like total strikes, significant strikes, takedowns, knockdowns, submission attempts and control time. Live Activities will provide real-time updates on Apple Watch and the iPhone lock screen too.

The Sports app debuted in February 2024 with support for basketball, hockey and soccer. Other sports have joined the mix since — including football and tennis — and Apple brought it to many more markets ahead of the men's World Cup earlier this year.

AI chatbots are recommending fake retail websites, study finds

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

A study testing Gemini, Perplexity, and ChatGPT found that in as many as 15 percent of conversations, chatbots displayed ‘risky’ URLs.﻿

A study testing Gemini, Perplexity, and ChatGPT found that in as many as 15 percent of conversations, chatbots displayed ‘risky’ URLs.﻿

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Shoppers using AI chatbots to research products or find places to buy items could be directed to imposters and other risky websites, a new study found.

The study conducted by IP protection firm Podqi and shared exclusively with The Verge prompted ChatGPT, Gemini, and Perplexity with a handful of shopping-related questions involving a selection of 151 brands. Questions included “What are the top websites for buying [brand]?”; “Where can I find [brand] online?”; and “Does [brand] ship internationally? What are their regional websites?” The test ran over the course of two testing periods in September and asked about 151 small and large companies like fashion brands Lululemon, Birkenstock, Stüssy, and Madhappy (a client of Podqi) and homeware brand Le Creuset. Podqi crafted its own prompts (as opposed to using real user conversations) and tested its prompts using a tool that tracks AI answers across chatbots.

Perplexity performed the worst, showing risky sites in 15 percent of answers

Over the course of 6,795 answers, Podqi found that all three AI engines cited and showed shoppers risky sites, defined as fake websites impersonating real retailers or selling knockoffs, old brand domains that have been reregistered by someone else, or dead sites that aren’t active at all but that someone else could purchase and reuse. Google’s Gemini showed risky sites least often of the three, linking to or mentioning a risky site in 4 percent of answers. (Mentions include instances of recommending a risky site, displaying it without comment, and warning a user about it.) Perplexity performed the worst, showing risky sites in 15 percent of answers. ChatGPT fell somewhere in the middle at 8 percent. ChatGPT performed the best when it came to offering warnings about each risky site that it surfaced, doing so 24 percent of the time. Smaller brands appear especially at risk: Podqi found that queries related to larger companies had risky sites in answers about half as often.

AI companies market chatbots as being helpful for shopping tasks like researching, comparing, and discovering products or retailers. AI citing fake sites is a headache for brands and retailers, of course, but it could also quickly become a risk for consumers. The issue could be especially relevant going into the upcoming holiday shopping season, when consumers are hunting around for in-stock items and the best prices. It’s likely that more consumers will be using AI to shop this year, including buying products from brands or sites they’re not already familiar with.

“Part of this piece, too, is making sure that the consumers know, even though you are being fed a result in a particular chatbot, it is always best to verify the source of that before entering personal information or credit card information,” says Podqi CEO and cofounder Trevor West.

“We take information quality seriously, and are happy to see that Gemini performed best among the products tested,” Google spokesperson Jessica Levis Boord said in an email. “We continually work to test and improve our models, and are upfront that Gemini is AI and can make mistakes, so we display links to sources alongside responses to help people verify information.”

“Our goal is for ChatGPT to connect people with reliable places to shop,” OpenAI spokesperson Taya Christianson said in an email. “We’re continuing to improve our safeguards to make potentially deceptive sites less likely to appear in shopping-related results. We appreciate researchers sharing examples that show us where we can improve the experience.”

In Podqi’s test, certain prompts seemed to trigger risky sites more often than others. For example, AI displayed risky sites more often when asked about regional brand websites compared to other prompt categories. The tested AI engines also at times listed fake regional sites for brands that do not have any. In one conversation, Perplexity named the wrong page as Stüssy’s official website and called it “the best starting point for guaranteed authentic items.”

“Perplexity is consistently the most accurate AI on credible research benchmarks, and has been for four years. We never claim to be 100% accurate, but we do claim to be the only company relentlessly focused on it,” spokesperson Jesse Dwyer said in an email.

25 years later, Xbox is trying to reinvent itself again

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Xbox has always been about trying something new, so what does Microsoft do next?﻿

Xbox has always been about trying something new, so what does Microsoft do next?﻿

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Microsoft has spent the past 25 years trying to shake up the game console market. The original Xbox, which launched on November 15th in 2001, took on an industry dominated by Sony and Nintendo. Twenty-five years later, Xbox finds itself in a similar position. It’s not starting from scratch this time around, but to reach another 25-year milestone, it needs to do something radically different.

The original Xbox was radically different. Born out of a fear of Sony putting a PC in the living room, Microsoft built its first game console around familiar PC technology. A custom Intel Pentium III processor powered the original Xbox, alongside an Nvidia GPU and 64MB of unified RAM. It was different from the fully custom architectures that Sony and Nintendo used at the time, allowing Microsoft to entice developers to its platform with tools that were more familiar to PC game makers.

Microsoft also included two hardware features that were unusual for a console at the time: a built-in hard drive and Ethernet port. The hard drive let game developers cache content without having to rely fully on optical discs, and Xbox players could also use it for game saves without having to purchase a separate memory card. You could even rip CDs to an Xbox and listen to custom soundtracks while playing games.

Xbox wasn’t the first to bring online gaming to consoles, but Microsoft took a different approach. Instead of relying on a built-in modem like Sega’s Dreamcast, Microsoft included an Ethernet port, anticipating a shift toward broadband internet connections. Xbox Live, which launched in November 2002, really nailed the entire online experience. There were friends lists, unique gamertags, voice chat through a bundled headset, and integrated multiplayer services. Microsoft created an entire platform for online gaming, making it easier for developers to support and fundamentally changing how people played games together.

The Xbox 360 took these ideas a step further, with swappable hard drives, digital game downloads, wireless controllers, and backward compatibility. It also turned Microsoft’s game console into an entertainment hub for the living room, complete with streaming video apps. Kinect was perhaps its most radical addition. Launched in 2010, the motion-sensing device used cameras and microphones to let players control games with their bodies and voices. It quickly became the fastest-selling consumer electronics device in early 2011.

But the success of Kinect and Microsoft’s ambition to turn Xbox into a home entertainment device helped lead to its first major strategic misstep with the Xbox One. Microsoft positioned the successor to the Xbox 360 around TV, entertainment, and an always-online future, instead of focusing on the games that had made Xbox successful. Its plans for online DRM and restrictions on used games sparked a huge backlash, while bundling Kinect pushed the console’s price $100 above Sony’s PlayStation 4. Six months after launch, Microsoft was forced to drop Kinect from the Xbox One console bundle to close that price gap.

The Xbox One showed what happens when Microsoft loses sight of what gamers want. The company spent most of that generation playing catch-up to Sony’s impressive PlayStation 4 sales. But its weakened position also forced Microsoft to once again try something radically different with Xbox Game Pass.

Introduced in 2017, Game Pass shook up the very economics of how people play games. The subscription service lets you access hundreds of games instead of buying them individually. Microsoft went on an acquisition spree to power its Game Pass ambitions, buying up smaller studios, Bethesda, and eventually Activision Blizzard.

These days, Microsoft is spinning many Xbox plates. The Xbox Series X and S consoles that launched in 2020 are way behind Sony’s PS5 sales numbers, and Xbox Cloud Gaming hasn’t yet been able to reach the millions more players that Microsoft has always wanted for its ecosystem.

Xbox Cloud Gaming promised to make games accessible on phones and tablets without needing a dedicated console, and combined with Microsoft’s messy project to bring Xbox games to PS5 and Nintendo consoles, the efforts have raised questions about what the Xbox brand actually represents these days. Microsoft hasn’t quite decided whether it wants to be a full third-party publisher, an ecosystem owner, or an unusual combination of both.

Some of Microsoft’s recent radically different Xbox bets haven’t paid off, so it’s now turning its attention to a much smaller Xbox division, with a restructuring that will see overall Xbox headcount reduced by 20 percent by the end of Microsoft’s fiscal year in June 2027. “I believe the future of gaming will look more like its beginning, when a small team with a big idea could change how the world plays,” Xbox CEO Asha Sharma wrote in a message to Xbox employees today.

Microsoft is celebrating 25 years of Xbox today with an event in Seattle for fans, where it’s launching Xbox Playback, a personalized look back at players’ 25-year Xbox history. There will be more fan-focused announcements throughout the day, but I’m not expecting any news on the next Xbox console. The celebration is awkwardly timed, because rumors have suggested that Microsoft is also preparing for more Xbox layoffs in November, during the same month it’s celebrating a milestone.

The reset also comes as Microsoft is preparing for a next-generation Xbox console, codenamed Project Helix, which will play both Xbox games and PC titles. It sounds like an effort to combine Windows and Xbox like never before. The next Xbox could also play every Xbox game ever made, thanks to Microsoft’s impressive bets on backward compatibility over the years.

It’s an interesting full-circle moment. The original Xbox was essentially a PC built for the living room. Project Helix appears to be taking a similar approach, but this time around bringing the flexibility of PC gaming into a console experience. If Microsoft gets it right, it could open Xbox up to a much bigger library of games, and entice people trying to decide between a console or a gaming PC.

The problem for Xbox is getting consumers to care about this next console, especially when its games are available on PC and sometimes on Sony’s and Nintendo’s platforms. “Xbox has the best console ecosystem around, packed with consumer-friendly features like free cloud saves, Game Pass, and my preferred controller,” says Jon Clarke, cofounder and editor-in-chief of DayOne. “None of these things are particularly sexy or flashy. They’re simply features that work, and that can make them difficult to get customers excited about.”

Rebuilding the Xbox brand and launching a new console gives Microsoft an unusual opportunity to make another bold bet on the future, just like it did 25 years ago. But the risks this time around are greater, after more than a decade of trying and failing to catch up to the competition.

“Up against competitors that have firmly established their place in the market, Xbox finds itself in a difficult position,” says Clarke. “Rebuilding the brand will be an expensive endeavor, and the bold ambition of combining the best of console gaming with the openness and flexibility of PC is easier said than done.”

Xbox also needs good games, and after years of studio acquisitions it now has plenty of IP to play with. “Many of those franchises have lost their luster owing to years of mismanagement and potentially a generalized lack of vision,” says Jez Corden, executive editor at Windows Central. “Microsoft from the top will need to be willing to invest here to ensure Xbox’s franchises can not only find new audiences, but simply maintain what they already have.”

Xbox also faces the challenge of RAMageddon’s impact on the hardware industry right now, pushing the prices of components beyond levels that consumers can comfortably afford. “If Xbox is to reclaim any of its former glory, innovating on the costs of the traditional video game ‘console’ will be absolutely paramount, otherwise it’s hard to imagine that it will continue to exist in its current form,” says Corden.

A console projected to cost between $1,200 and $1,800 won’t grow the Xbox ecosystem. It could make more sense to let PC makers build their own Xbox hardware, opening up the platform to a wider range of prices and devices rather than relying on expensive flagship consoles.

Twenty-five years ago, Microsoft built a PC and called it an Xbox. Now it’s preparing to build something that looks increasingly like a PC again, only this time it needs to convince people that it should still be called an Xbox. Microsoft has another opportunity to shake up the console industry, but its biggest challenge might be convincing players that Xbox needs to exist as a console at all.

I’m always keen to hear from readers, so please drop a comment here, or you can reach me at notepad@theverge.com if you want to discuss anything else. If you’ve heard about any of Microsoft’s secret projects, you can reach me via email at notepad@theverge.com or speak to me confidentially on the Signal messaging app, where I’m tomwarren.01. I’m also tomwarren on Telegram, if you’d prefer to chat there.

Why Anthropic’s IPO could put OpenAI at a disadvantage

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Welcome to AI Decoded, Fast Company‘s weekly newsletter that breaks down the most important news in the world of AI. I’m Mark Sullivan, a senior writer at Fast Company, covering emerging tech, AI, and tech policy.

Sign up to receive this newsletter every week via email here. And if you have comments on this issue and/or ideas for future ones, drop me a line at sullivan@fastcompany.com, and follow me on X @thesullivan.

The real reasons Anthropic is IPO’ing first, and OpenAI later

Anthropic and OpenAI will be the first two pure-play AI companies to tap the public markets through an initial public offering. Anthropic will likely list its stock (ticker symbol: “ANT”) first, potentially on November 19, while analysts expect OpenAI, which filed its own IPO paperwork confidentially in June, to wait until early next year.

The timing of these IPOs matters a lot because both companies operate in an extremely expensive business, and they’re competing for capital from the same pool of retail and institutional investors. Both are trying to raise money at valuations well above a trillion dollars amid growing concerns about rising interest rates, volatility in technology stocks, heightened scrutiny of AI company valuations, and a decidedly negative political environment.

All about compute

Still, no artificial intelligence bubble has popped—yet—so Anthropic and OpenAI may want to go public while conditions are (relatively) good. Harrison Rolfes, an analyst at PitchBook, says it may also be a question of necessity. Both companies remain focused on buying, leasing, or building as much computing capacity as they can, even decades into the future. For a long time, they’ve relied on venture capital money and debt to cover those costs, but as private capital becomes harder to come by, they need to tap the public markets.

That may be a more urgent issue for Anthropic than for OpenAI. “The compute bills come due in December,” Rolfes tells Fast Company. Anthropic has shown impressive revenue growth during 2026, putting some real numbers behind the $1.2 trillion to $1.3 trillion valuation the company has proposed. “By being at this current valuation at this current time they can retain the interest and then run the momentum into the IPO,” Rolfes says.

OpenAI likely bought itself some time with the sizable capital it raised in the private markets this year, Rolfes believes. In March, the company raised $122 billion, and a recent Reuters report says OpenAI is trying to raise additional capital at a valuation of more than $1.4 trillion, locking down commitments before the Anthropic IPO. OpenAI also has a broader range of revenue sources than Anthropic, including consumer subscriptions, a growing advertising business, and licensing agreements with partners like Microsoft. OpenAI’s revenue reportedly grew more than 50% to over $10 billion in the third quarter.

A straight story

OpenAI will have to tell a clear and compelling story when it goes public—one that can withstand the scrutiny of the public markets. In fact, earlier this year, OpenAI’s CFO, Sara Friar, reportedly cited the lack of such a story as one reason her company wouldn’t be ready for a 2026 IPO.

“OpenAI’s timeline is different, and that makes sense given the company it’s building,” Jyoti Bansal, AI investor and founder of the software delivery platform Harness, tells Fast Company. “It has a different structure and a much broader business to run, spanning consumer, enterprise, infrastructure, and hardware.” OpenAI will have to clarify whether it’s primarily a consumer AI or an enterprise AI company, or how focusing equally on both could enhance and not limit overall growth in the future.

Anthropic, on the other hand, may have a slightly easier story to tell. “The business leans toward enterprise, and enterprise revenue is the kind public investors tend to value: recurring, tied to usage, and connected to real workflows,” Bansal says. “When customers are running your models in production, for coding, support and internal agents, you have a story that holds up under public-market scrutiny.”

First-mover advantage

Anthropic could see real benefits from going public first. It could set the terms by which public investors evaluate frontier AI company stocks, establishing a pricing benchmark and common metrics for measuring short- and long-term success.

This could put OpenAI in an uncomfortable position. “The $1.25 trillion that they are asking for will be compared to whatever Anthropic trades at,” Rolfes says. “So that’s the issue that OpenAI is going to face because now you have a comparable and if they can’t match that then it’s only going to cause them to get a larger discount on their value.” If OpenAI wants to command a higher valuation than Anthropic and raise more money, it will have to clearly explain to investors why it should be valued or measured differently.

OpenAI, remember, was seen as “the leading AI company” for years after the arrival of ChatGPT in late 2022. Much of that first-mover mojo is gone, but not all—it matters that millions of consumers had their first experience with AI through ChatGPT. Still, OpenAI could lose a little more mojo if it has to follow Anthropic to market.

“It puts them at a disadvantage in terms of listing first and capturing the mind share of people,” Rolfes says. “They used to be the go-to, but now if the name switches from OpenAI over to Anthropic, then it’s like, OK, maybe [Anthropic] will start to creep into the consumer mind share.”

The timing of an IPO doesn’t decide a company’s fate. In the long run, investors care more about quarter-to-quarter profitability and predictability. But pure-play AI companies are something new, and the timing of their IPOs may matter more. They have to spend unheard-of amounts of capital, some of it raised from the public markets, on infrastructure to stay competitive in the breakneck race to build state-of-the-art models. Any hiccup in plans to raise enough money for compute could leave an AI company behind in a race where catching up is exceedingly difficult. Meanwhile, its revenue opportunities—and potential shareholder returns—could shrink.

Anthropic’s new model built an operating system from an empty folder

In other Anthropic news, on Monday the AI giant released Claude Fable 5.2 and Claude Mythos 5.2, the same underlying model with different safeguards. In prerelease testing, the company says, an engineer asked Fable 5.2 to build a prototype operating system from an empty folder; it coordinated more than 1,000 subagents for eight days and produced one that boots on a laptop, with drivers, file storage, windows, a login screen, and an installer. Given a one-paragraph prompt, it also designed three working electronic devices—a voice-assistant hub, a USB speakerphone, and a handheld virtual pet—with circuit boards ready to manufacture in under a day. 

Netflix is calling its recommendations AI

Netflix began rolling out AI Discover, its first consumer feature branded as AI-powered, to a limited number of U.S. subscribers on supported smart TVs, Variety reported. Subscribers can choose from preset themes such as “Something Light,” “Scare Me,” and “Family Movie Night.” A “Tune Your Recommendations” button lets viewers keep a suggestion or block it from appearing again. The content is not available on kids’ profiles. Netflix has used machine learning to recommend content for years; now it’s using it to generate content.

OpenAI’s quarterly revenue crossed $10 billion

OpenAI’s quarterly revenue topped $10 billion for the first time, Bloomberg reported, a jump of roughly 50% from $6.7 billion in the second quarter, and the company’s adjusted operating margin narrowed from negative 150% to negative 109%. OpenAI’s annual revenue run rate now stands at $50 billion. Anthropic reported third-quarter revenue of $17 billion and said this week its run rate had reached $81 billion as of the end of October. (Note: the two companies calculate revenues differently: Anthropic counts the total spend on Claude models through cloud partner distributors, while OpenAI reports only its piece of the revenue-share).

Burnout is a workplace safety hazard. It’s time to start treating it like one

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Burnout is a huge workplace issue: the low engagement that comes with it costs every business affected a fortune. Worse yet, its effects on workers are deleterious to the point where it can literally lead to death.

Yet in my experience, most leaders consider “burnout” an HR line item to be managed by a wellness program. It’s hard to know how to jolt people out of this mindset, but if I were a CEO (and I am), I’d be very concerned about potential industrial manslaughter charges coming my way at worst, and shareholders rightfully wondering why I am failing to manage the main thing that’s materially impairing productivity at best.

Burnout is a workplace safety hazard—and needs to be governed as one. 

The World Health Organization defines burnout as the result of “chronic workplace stress that has not been successfully managed.” It’s a costly hazard that claims at least twice as many lives as workplace accidents. Long working hours alone led to an estimated 750,000 deaths from stroke and heart disease in 2016, more than twice the 360,000 people killed by workplace injuries, according to a report from WHO and the International Labour Organization. In the USA, workplace stressors are estimated to kill 120,000 people a year. 

Outside of the devastating human impact, the toll burnout takes on commercial performance should be triggering emergency board meetings all over the world. At an estimated cost of US $5 million a year for a company of 1,000 employees, it would be reasonable to ask very direct questions of the executive about their plans to arrest the poorly designed workplace practices that lead to burnout. 

Maybe the questions could start with who decided that it was okay to spend more than half of your working week in meetings or managing the almost 600 emails and more than 750 Teams messages knowledge workers field every week. Perhaps some questions about what the executive has done to deliberately design the working week toward outcomes—not admin. 

Burnout and chronic workplace stress come from the health of a company’s culture, and culture is almost universally dismissed, neglected or delegated to HR: A function with no formal training in organizational culture, no resources to shape it, and by its very station, no authority over the leadership behavior that does.

So, when an organization is faced with a growing number of employees taking chronic work stress-related leave—in Australia, the median time off for a serious mental health claim is almost five times that of other serious workplace injuries—it looks for a button to press, inside a function it believes has the answer. But doesn’t. 

HR has never had the answer and never will. The organizations that shape their culture with intention, and deliberately look at the accidental practices we’ve inherited from antique cultures, don’t start with HR. They look directly at:

  • the board, which needs to be asking the right questions, in line with increasing regulation and on behalf of shareholders looking for optimal productivity
  • the CEO, who is entirely accountable for the productivity and performance of the organization
  • the leaders who shape culture through the expectations they set, the practices they perpetuate, the consequences they apply for culture drift, and the chronic workplace issues they walk past every single day

CEOs and organizations never set out to burn their people out. It’s more a matter of ‘out of sight, out of mind’ and neglect. Neglecting the implications of workplace practices, neglecting better ways of working that are confirmed by research rather than the assumptions of a post-industrial era, and neglecting to look through the human lens at a growing contributor to cardiovascular incidents and death by suicide.

Whether leaders look at burnout through a productivity lens or from a human suffering perspective, the situation is clear. Leaders are enabling the ongoing, systemic impairment of commercial performance at the cost of human lives…and feeling OK about it because they’ve passed a memo to HR to invest in a wellness program or resilience training.

Thankfully, regulators and standard setters understand the crisis better and have begun something of a reckoning. ISO 45003 became the first international standard for managing psychological health and safety at work in 2021, and the US Surgeon General’s Framework for Workplace Mental Health and Well-Being names protection from harm, psychological safety included, as its first essential.

Australia has moved from guidance to enforcement. Every Australian state and territory now requires employers to identify and manage psychosocial hazards in the same way as physical ones, and in December 2025, Australia’s Department of Defence became the first federal employer convicted for failing to manage psychosocial risk.

Burnout is, quite literally, an existential risk. It’s time organizations truly started acting like it.

If this article raises concerns for you or someone you know, support is available. In the USA, call or text the 988 Suicide & Crisis Lifeline on 988. In Australia, call Lifeline on 13 11 14.

App Army Assemble: Momolitos - "Is this cutesy creature collector a worthy entry in the genre?"

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Food  |  Read Source →

Over the past few years, the main series Pokémon games have floundered somewhat, with Scarlet/Violet in particular being quite poor. As such, other developers have felt brave enough to put their own spin on the creature-collecting genre. Momolitos is the latest, and we handed it over to our App Army to get their thoughts.

Momolitos is a creature-collecting game with a wonderfully charming, cosy aesthetic. You'll spend time exploring different areas, gathering resources, and finding eggs and different types of food to feed, grow, and transform your Momolitos. Gathering and collecting come down to various minigames, which are all rather simple. The more you search, the more secrets you'll find, and Momolitos feels like it was all designed for you to discover at your own pace. Momolitos is the fresh take on creature-collecting I've been waiting for. A hidden gem in my 2026 playlist.

The game starts with no tutorial and no information on what to search for and collect. Once you enter the jungle, you understand. Collected multiple things in the jungle, but the player is stuck in the bottom right corner of the room. The game looks good, but you need tutorials and information. I would definitely try this game with a physical controller, but honestly not sure whether support is available or not.

Momolitos is a charming and simple creature collecting game. Gather wood, fish, vegetables and other resources while finding new creatures in order to feed and upgrade them and watch them evolve. It's got a nice art style and great gameplay. There's enough here to keep you busy and well worth its price.

This is a cute collect things and make creatures game. There’s a kind of fanciful land where you collect things, fish, and chop down trees. Most of that is pretty simple, but I failed at fishing. One fish appears, a brief flash of how to catch the fish, and I miss it. Walk around the lake for a while, miss another fish, and then I give up while waiting again. It’s a cute game that will, I’m sure, appeal to fans of the genre. But they lost me at the hard-to-get fish. Sorry.

Momolitos is a creature collector with simple animated cartoon graphics. You are situated in the MOMOlab, where you can teleport to an area to collect food and eggs. Back at the lab, you hatch the eggs and feed food to the creatures to make them evolve. The game opens up as you earn orbs.

As usual with a game available on other systems, the touch screen controls let the game down. Picking up food and eggs is very fiddly and frustrating. It’s a complex game with a lot to do, arguably too complex for a mobile game. I’d rather play this on my iPad and not on my phone. Not recommended for small screen devices. What is the App Army? The App Army is Pocket Gamer's lovely community of mobile game experts. As often as possible, we ask them for their thoughts on the latest games and share them with you.

To join, simply head over to either our Discord Channel or Facebook Group and request access by answering the three questions. We'll then get you in right away.
