SOLRITE and sonnen collaborate on integrated VPP breaker

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

SOLRITE and sonnen plan to digitally integrate VPP smart breakers into what the companies are calling the SOLRITE sonnen VPP. The expanded VPP Node combines sonnenHub with ADAPT Energy’s VPP smart breaker technology to manage major household loads. The VPP smart breaker, controlled by ADAPT Energy, can also participate in on-grid VPP events, enabling the same electrical infrastructure to be used for both backup and grid participation.

“Our work with ADAPT Energy is a meaningful step forward in how we think about residential battery storage and VPP participation,” said Regan George, CEO and founder of SOLRITE Energy. “We have already demonstrated the tremendous value of connecting sonnen batteries together as a pooled grid asset. Now, we’re going inside the home and unlocking another layer of flexibility that has been sitting there all along. By combining the battery with additional load management made possible by the VPP smart breaker technology, we’re creating a more valuable node for the grid while also giving homeowners a clever backup solution.”

The system can shed large loads without requiring those circuits to be relocated. ADAPT Energy keeps the smart breakers synchronized with the sonnen battery during backup mode and automatically restores the breakers when grid power returns.

This integration is designed to manage whole-home electrical loads in the background, not requiring homeowners to reset circuits during an outage.

“This is about making whole-home backup work the way homeowners expect it to,” said Troy Morgan, president of PanTech Design, the developer of ADAPT Energy. “Instead of putting the pressure on homeowners to change the way their home is wired, and to manually manipulate loads during an outage, we’re putting intelligence into their energy system, which provides a seamless and automatic backup power experience for their home. Our technology empowers home loads to supply optimal backup power during an outage or provide valuable flexibility to the grid.”

When a VPP event happens, the sonnen battery can discharge or charge with direct load shedding via ADAPT. ADAPT Energy will strictly shed pre-approved household loads. A customer who has opted to include a pool pump, for example, can have that load temporarily turned off while the sonnen battery is discharging to the grid.

“With the addition of ADAPT Energy’s VPP smart-breaker technology to the SOLRITE–sonnen ecosystem, we are expanding the virtual power plant from a battery-centered resource into a fully orchestrated home-energy platform,” said Blake Richetta, chairman and CEO of sonnen. “By synchronizing energy that has been harnessed in our battery with intelligent, real-time control of household electrical loads, we can deliver more flexible, responsive capacity to the grid precisely when it is needed most. This integration materially strengthens the value of SOLRITE’s virtual power plant—supporting grid reliability, creating greater customer value, and enabling SOLRITE to continue delivering industry-leading solar-plus-storage VPA solutions to homeowners.”

Billy Ludt is managing editor of Solar Power World and currently covers topics on mounting, inverters, installation and operations.

Jensen Huang's Nvidia exit casts shadow over Wall Street’s $500 billion AI financing bet

📅 2026-11-12  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Leadership change at Nvidia has the potential to threaten financing of the artificial intelligence boom and test Jensen Huang's $500 billion vision to turn AI chips into tradable debt securities.

The Nvidia CEO's resignation was announced Thursday following revelations that he had two extramarital affairs — one with an employee and the other with a woman believed by U.S. intelligence to be a Chinese spy.

Shares in the tech giant — which has become central to the AI build-out in recent years — initially plunged more than 13% following Huang's resignation, before paring losses.

Nvidia has never identified a successor to Huang, who has led the chipmaker since its founding in 1993. Colette Kress, chief financial officer, has been named interim CEO.

Follow CNBC's live coverage of Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang's resignation

The company has been dubbed the "central bank" of the AI build-out, with Huang seen as the "hypeman" for the sector and its chips considered the industry's gold standard.

Huang's departure raises a central question: whether his successor can carry forward a financing strategy designed to transform GPUs from rapidly depreciating hardware into bankable infrastructure assets

The $4.2 trillion firm's industry dominance, with Huang front and center, allowed it to sign memorandums of understanding with some of Wall Street's biggest asset managers, intended to secure third-party capital for a landmark $500 billion AI financing drive.

The preliminary agreements, involving Apollo Global Management, BlackRock, Blackstone, Brookfield, Goldman Sachs, and KKR, sought to tap institutional investors and private capital to finance a range of computing infrastructure, including data centers and chip factories. The funding would involve specialized financing vehicles, with Nvidia's AI chips used as underlying collateral, in arrangements comparable to real estate or toll roads securitizations.

The financing arrangements are still preliminary and subject to final agreements.

BlackRock and Goldman Sachs declined to comment on today's resignation. The other firms weren't immediately available for comment.

The arrangements could now face heightened scrutiny in the aftermath of Huang's departure.

Sunaina Sinha Haldea, global head of private capital advisory at Raymond James, said a leadership change gives every firm involved a "natural moment to pause and look at the terms again."

"The best thing a new CEO can do is leave these partnerships alone in the first few months and focus on getting the final agreements signed," Haldea told CNBC via email. "Jensen brought these firms to the table with his vision, and any potential successor will keep them there by showing that the business behind that vision hasn't changed."

She added: "I don't think the capital walks away, because these lenders are underwriting Nvidia's chips and the customer contracts behind them, but they could want to be paid more for the uncertainty."

Charu Chanana, chief investment strategist at Saxo, said Huang's successor would need to reassure lenders that Nvidia's technology roadmap, customer relationships and strategic vision remain intact.

"I wouldn't expect Nvidia's financing partnerships to unravel simply because Huang leaves, but a leadership shake-up could make Wall Street more cautious, potentially slowing commitments or tightening financing terms," Chanana told CNBC via email.

"The bigger risk is a ripple effect across AI financing, particularly when rising debt, circular financing and uncertain investment returns were already under scrutiny," Chanana added. "This is not a financing crisis yet, but it could become a catalyst for a broader repricing of risk across the AI ecosystem."

Haldea added that she expects to see more discipline among investors in the aftermath of the scandal. "Lenders will ask harder questions about who is really carrying the risk in these structures, and that scrutiny will spread throughout the AI infrastructure deal market."

Exodus’ ‘cost reset is real’ as payments offset crypto wallet slowdown, StoneX says

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

Exodus targets $30 million in annual savings as StoneX keeps its $12 price target and sees adjusted breakeven on the horizon.

StoneX kept a Buy rating and a $12 price target for Exodus Movement Exodus Movement, Inc. (EXOD) on Thursday.

The basis of its argument is that Exodus' push into payments and lower costs are helping offset weaker activity in its legacy crypto wallet business, bringing it closer to breaking even on an adjusted basis. "The cost reset is real," StoneX analyst Mark Palmer wrote in a Thursday report.

Exodus shares were up over 8% to $4.68 on Thursday morning, with StoneX's $12 target implying a further gain of around 150%.

The company reported third-quarter earnings on Monday, reaching revenue of $32.5 million and topping analyst estimates of between $29 million and $31 million. Its adjusted EBITDA loss shrank to $3.9 million from $6.7 million in the second quarter, while its net loss totaled $8.3 million.

Exodus' payments segment contributed nearly $11 million in its first full quarter following the acquisitions of Monavate and Baanx in May. That helped offset a 29% year-over-year drop-off in wallet-related revenue to $21.6 million. The company noted that around 64% of payments revenue came from traditional payments, while the other 36% came from onchain finance.

Exodus also cut its headcount from 326 to 214 employees between May and Monday's earnings, with management expecting roughly $30 million in savings going into 2027.

"Our cost base is lower. If exchange volumes rise, revenue can grow without a proportionate increase in overhead," CEO JP Richardson said on Monday's earnings call.

However, Richardson also warned that many of the company's milestones still depend on regulatory approval and execution.

Exodus is still waiting on approval in Latvia that would allow the company to resume issuing its payment cards in Europe. The company said it has already resumed issuing cards in the U.S.

In Moscow, Witkoff and Kushner Ask Putin to Include Europe in Peace Talks

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The Kremlin, taking a hard line, is pushing to widen negotiations to overhaul European security structures.

President Trump’s envoys went to Moscow on Thursday to seek agreement from President Vladimir V. Putin to bring Europe into talks on the war in Ukraine, as the Kremlin pushes to widen the negotiations to overhaul European security arrangements.

The envoys, Steve Witkoff and Jared Kushner, arrived in Russia one day after mediating peace talks in Abu Dhabi between Russian and Ukrainian representatives, who spoke directly for the first time in nine months. After meeting with Mr. Putin in the Kremlin, the envoys were scheduled to travel to Dublin to meet with President Volodymyr Zelensky of Ukraine.

Mr. Witkoff and Mr. Kushner are pressing to involve Europe in the talks as Russia makes clear that it will not consider a cease-fire until its broader demands to redraw the European security architecture are addressed.

The Trump administration, seeking a foreign policy win after Republicans’ drubbing in the midterm elections, is making another push for peace even as Russia gives no indication that it will stop the war anytime soon, and in fact appears to be hardening some of its positions.

Russia has intensified attacks on Ukrainian cities ahead of a winter bombing campaign, taking advantage of Ukraine’s lack of air defenses. On Thursday, Mr. Zelensky said that the Ukrainian armed forces were again facing restrictions on Starshield, the military version of the satellite internet network Starlink, and had asked the United States for information.

Andy Robertson wants equalling Scotland caps record to be Kenny Dalglish tribute

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: World  |  Read Source →

Please refresh the page or navigate to another page on the site to be automatically logged inPlease refresh your browser to be logged in

Want to bookmark your favourite articles and stories to read or reference later? Start your Independent Membership today.

Andy Robertson believes reaching his 102nd international cap should serve as an opportunity to celebrate Sir Kenny Dalglish rather than focus on himself, as he prepares to equal the record held by his fellow former Liverpool player.

The 32-year-old will draw level with Dalglish when he captains Scotland at Hampden against North Macedonia in Friday's Nations League match.

"Tomorrow’s not really about me," the Tottenham left-back said. "It’s about him in my opinion. For how long the record stood, for what he achieved in his career, for the person he is.

"It’s another reason for our country to tip our hat to one of the greatest players that have played the game, never mind in Scotland, he’s definitely the greatest in Scotland."

Robertson spoke on the 40th anniversary of Dalglish’s final match for Scotland, a 3-0 victory against Luxembourg delivered during his second season operating as Liverpool player/manager.

"That tells you everything that you need to know, the fact that he’s juggling all that and still showing up for his country," Robertson said.

"He reminds me all the time that 102 caps back then was a lot harder than 102 caps now, and he’s right because there were less games.

"In terms of numbers, I’ll stand side by side with him, but I don’t want people to forget how important this man has been for our country.

"He was arguably the best player in the world at his time and he’ll always be the greatest in this country."

Dalglish recently joked that he hoped Robertson would hang up his boots before establishing a new record.

"We’ve had a lot of jokes, but I also know deep down how proud he is of me and how happy he is for me as well," the former Dundee United player said.

"I always remember when I got the captaincy, he was one of the first people to text me and say, ‘I know I’ve been out of the game maybe a little bit, but if you need help, if you need somebody to bounce anything off, then I can be that person’.

"To have somebody like that, that you can bounce anything off and rely on, him and his family have been such a massive help to my family, especially in my time at Liverpool.

"I’ve got no doubt there’ll be a couple of texts that will get exchanged. I know we laugh and we joke, publicly and privately, but I know he’s proud of me and he’s happy for what I’ve achieved as well."

Robertson is expected to surpass Dalglish in Switzerland on Monday, and he shows no signs of slowing down.

"I’m still hungry to achieve more for the country," he said. "I want to get Scotland back to another major tournament, I want to try and get out the group stages.

"Secondly, I want to try and leave the squad in the best possible place in terms of the quality coming through, the characters they’ve got, the mentality they’ve got.

"If I can leave that behind, then I can maybe enjoy Scotland games a bit more and I can join the fans and party with them."

Pentagon backs war memorial guard who reprimanded Hegseth's group

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

US Army guard reprimands group that includes US Defence Secretary Pete Hegseth at Arlington National Cemetery.

The Pentagon has given its backing to a US Army guard who reprimanded a group that included US Defence Secretary Pete Hegseth at the Arlington National Cemetery.

A video of the Veterans Day observance event on Wednesday showed Hegseth, his wife, and senior advisers laughing and talking while posing for photos in front of the Tomb of the Unknown Soldier.

The footage showed the army sentinel on duty stepping towards Hegseth's group and shouting: "All visitors will maintain an atmosphere of silence and respect at all times."

Chief Pentagon spokesman Sean Parnell said in a statement: "While commemorating this solemn day, a soldier correctly and respectfully reminded everyone to maintain decorum."

"The Secretary and the entire Department have always honored - and will always honor - the sacrifices our military men and women have made to keep America safe."

On Thursday, he presented an award to the soldier who reprimanded his group.

Hegseth "welcomed the 3rd U.S. Infantry Regiment, the Old Guard to the Pentagon where he proudly awarded Specialist Thomas Iglesias the Joint Service Commendation Medal for the execution of his duties at the Tomb of the Unknown Soldier on November 11, 2026," the Pentagon said in a statement.

In a post on X showing the soldier, Hesgeth said: "We love soldiers. We love standards. Well done, Specialist."

The Arlington National Cemetery etiquette requests that visitors speak quietly and do not engage in disrespectful or disorderly conduct.

The tomb, which is one of the country's most sacred war memorials, marks the final resting place of an unknown soldier who was killed in World War I.

A service member from the 3rd Infantry Regiment stands watch over the tomb 24 hours a day and has done so since 1948.

It is not unusual for them to admonish visitors to the site, according to the BBC's US partner CBS News. They reportedly do so a few times a day.

Defence Secretary Pete Hegseth and First Lady Melania Trump bow their heads in prayer during a Veterans Day ceremony at Arlington National Cemetery

Earlier, President Donald Trump, Vice-President JD Vance and other top officials attended the annual wreath-laying ceremony at the cemetery.

During his speech at the ceremony, Trump offered his gratitude to uniformed service members who have fought for the country. He also touted what he sees as his administration's success in the Iran war.

"Under this administration, and it was about time, somebody fought back. All of us fought back," he said.

"And we won," Trump said of the Iran war. "It's just a question of when we want to end it. We want to make sure we have a total victory, not a 93% victory."

Trump also pointed out Hegseth during the ceremony, saying he's done a "fantastic job" as defence secretary.

Trump has repeatedly defended Hegseth amid Democratic lawmakers' push to impeach him. On Tuesday, an effort to impeach the defence secretary under accusations that he waged war on Iran without congressional authorisation failed on Capitol Hill.

Hegseth has called the impeachment efforts a "farce" and a "stunt".

8 findings from the EPA on radon, the invisible gas in your home

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Free daily briefing on global business news.

Eight findings from the EPA's radon page cover who bears the risk, how the toll estimates changed, and why a home test matters.

Radon can't be seen, so a family can live for years with the gas in an ordinary house and never know it is there. Researchers agree that radon gives people lung cancer, according to the US EPA. The uncertainty lies in the size of the toll. The yearly death figure that health agencies cite is a best estimate drawn from a model, not a count of people who died. The open questions are how many people radon kills and which people. A household can't wait for those answers before finding out whether it is exposed.

Protection against radon has to come before the disease, because lung cancer is hard to catch in time. From 2019 to 2023, close to half of lung cancer cases had already reached distant organs by the time doctors diagnosed them, according to the CDC. Many cases of the disease can be prevented, and keeping radon out of the home does more good than counting on doctors to find the cancer early. A household also can't rely on a neighbor's result, because radon levels in homes next door can differ very much, according to the NIH. Late diagnosis and a neighbor's reading both leave a family without an answer. A test of its own home is the practical line of defense.

The U.S. Environmental Protection Agency set out to show how radon in homes causes lung cancer and how serious that harm is. Each of those findings matters less on its own than in combination. A reader who weighs them together learns who carries radon's risk and how firm the numbers behind it are, and that is enough to decide whether to test.

Smoking is not the only major cause of lung cancer in the U.S., and the next one on the list is a gas inside ordinary homes. Only smoking causes more lung cancer than radon, and for Americans who have never smoked, radon causes more than anything else, according to the U.S. Environmental Protection Agency. About 21,000 Americans die each year of lung cancer caused by radon. Radon is a cause in its own right rather than a side note to smoking, and a family can live with it without knowing.

The CDC cites the same estimate of 21,000 deaths a year. That figure is a midpoint, and the EPA's full estimate runs from 8,000 to 45,000 deaths a year. The true toll could be well below the familiar number or well above it, and nobody can yet say which. Even the low end of that range means thousands of deaths a year. No household has a reason to put off learning its own level while the experts work out the national number.

Cigarette smoking has become uncommon among U.S. adults, only 12.5% of whom smoked in 2020, according to the CDC. Yet about 2,900 radon deaths a year occur among people who never smoked, and those deaths don't depend on anyone's cigarette use. Doctors who treat the disease see it changing. "This is not your grandfather's lung cancer," said Dr. Timothy Mullett, medical director of the Markey Cancer Center Affiliate Network at the University of Kentucky.

Those deaths among never-smokers can come down only through repairs to the house.

Lung cancer leaves few of its patients alive for long, and that is why radon's harm is counted in deaths rather than in illnesses people recover from. The EPA puts the share of patients who live five years past their diagnosis at 11 to 15 percent. Most lung cancer patients never reach a point where they have been treated and are well again.

That low survival, repeated across a large number of patients, produces a death toll that returns every year. In 2024, 128,663 Americans died of lung cancer, according to the CDC. With so few patients surviving five years, those deaths are the expected result of the disease rather than a handful of unlucky cases. Radon-related cases are part of that national toll, not a separate one, and with survival this low, each new case is more likely to end in death than in recovery.

Measured in deaths, radon is a bigger lung cancer threat than the secondhand smoke many people already go out of their way to avoid. The agency estimates that about 21,000 people die each year of lung cancer from radon, while the yearly toll it ties to secondhand smoke comes to an estimated 3,000. Radon gas in a basement deserves at least the caution people show toward a smoker in the room.

In a disease this hard to survive, a household gains the most by lowering its exposure before any cancer starts, and a radon test shows whether there is exposure to lower.

For a smoker, radon is not a separate hazard added to the cigarettes. The risks from the two are compounded, and the danger from each is greater when the other is present. The EPA estimates that about 62 in 1,000 smokers could develop lung cancer in homes with radon at 4 pCi/L. NIH reports that the EPA wants homes repaired once radon reaches 4 pCi/L or above, and that smokers account for most of the people who die of radon-related cancer. For anyone who smokes, the official line for action already marks serious risk rather than giving an early warning. In any household, the smoker has the most riding on the radon reading.

A reading under the action level is not a safe reading for a smoker. For readings from 2 to 4 pCi/L, the EPA's advice is that homeowners think about fixing the house, according to NIH. Yet at 2 pCi/L, the lowest reading in that band, about 32 smokers in 1,000 could develop lung cancer. Repairs also have a limit, because getting a home's radon level under 2 pCi/L is hard to do. A smoker whose home is fixed down to that level still lives with the risk that comes with it. Repairs can shrink radon's added danger for that smoker, but they can't erase it. For a smoker, that middle band is better treated as a reason to fix the house than as a reason to wait.

The odds of lung cancer are higher at each step up in radon, so a smoker needs to know exactly where the house sits, not roughly. A test that runs for months gives that answer better than a short one does. A long-term radon test lasts more than 90 days, according to NIH. That is long enough for a smoker to get a steady picture of the home's level before deciding whether to fix it, and how far.

A smoker can't undo the harm of past smoking, but with a careful test, a smoker can learn how much of the danger comes from the house itself.

Many people think of lung cancer as a smoker's disease and assume that a life without cigarettes keeps them clear of it. Radon in the house can still cause the disease in someone who has never lit a cigarette. The EPA estimates that about 7 in 1,000 lifelong nonsmokers could develop lung cancer in homes with radon at 4 pCi/L. Avoiding tobacco does not cancel out the risk from the air in the house. Because that risk comes from the home rather than from a habit, quitting or avoiding anything won't lower it, and the way to reduce it begins with a test of the house.

A never-smoker's odds depend on how much radon is in the air at home. For lifelong nonsmokers in a house at 20 pCi/L, about 36 in 1,000 could get the disease, a far more serious risk than at the lower level. Next-door neighbors with no smoking history may carry very different odds, and the only thing separating them is the radon in each home. For them, the useful question is not whether they are exposed but how much, and only a test of the home can answer it. Nothing about a person shows whether they live with a small risk or a serious one.

Experts disagree over where radon ranks among the causes of lung cancer in lifelong nonsmokers. In the EPA's estimates, radon is the leading cause among non-smokers. The researchers' estimates that the CDC cites put secondhand smoke ahead, with about 7,300 lung cancers a year in never-smokers linked to secondhand smoke and about 2,900 linked to radon. Both sets of estimates count radon as a real cause of the disease in this group, and they differ only on whether it outranks secondhand smoke. Even with those figures putting secondhand smoke ahead, the CDC advises never-smokers to test their homes for radon. Either ranking leads a household to the same step.

For someone who never smoked, the reading in their own house matters more than radon's rank on any national chart.

In 2005, researchers moved the proof of radon's harm out of the mine shaft and into the house. Until then, the case against radon in homes rested on people who worked underground, so its effect in kitchens and bedrooms was an inference drawn from miners. Researchers who studied homes directly had produced mixed results, with some finding a link, some a weak one and some none at all, according to the University of Iowa. The EPA reports that two 2005 studies, one in North America and one in Europe, supplied the direct evidence from homes that earlier work lacked. R. William Field, an associate professor of occupational and environmental health and epidemiology at the University of Iowa, says the European researchers reached the same answer as his North American team, which pooled data from seven home studies. That answer matched the risks the miner studies had predicted, so the danger in houses was no longer a projection.

The 2005 results mattered to homeowners because the risk showed up in ordinary houses rather than only the worst ones. In the North American analysis, prolonged exposure to radon at an average of about 3.0 pCi/L, a level below the EPA's action level, was linked to an 11 to 21 percent rise in lung cancer risk, according to the University of Iowa. That added risk turned up in houses whose owners could have been told no repair was needed. The finding supports the view of Tom Kelly, former director of the EPA's Indoor Environments Division, that even low radon levels can cause the disease. Field says an earlier study of homes in Iowa suggests the true risk from years of breathing radon at home may be slightly higher than the pooled range shows.

A homeowner should read a radon test as a number rather than as a pass or a fail.

By the end of the 1990s, the evidence on radon in homes needed one authoritative account that could support a national death estimate. The National Academy of Sciences published that account in 1999 in its BEIR VI report, which the US EPA calls the most definitive scientific collection on indoor radon. Later work built directly on it. The EPA's later range of radon deaths was the agency's own calculation, and it started from the methods BEIR VI laid out. The newer federal figure therefore grows out of this report's work rather than setting it aside.

The report is usually summed up as a single number, and that number makes it sound more certain than it was. The figure most often tied to BEIR VI is about 15,000 radon deaths a year, yet the US EPA reports that the report's estimate ran from about 15,000 to 22,000. That makes 15,000 the bottom of the report's range, not its final word. The range expresses doubt about how many people radon kills, not about whether it kills them. A later figure above 15,000 does not break with BEIR VI on that fact alone.

That range starts at a yearly toll already in the thousands. Any shift within it leads to more deaths, not fewer. A family waiting for scientists to agree on one number would be waiting on a question about how large the danger is, not whether it exists.

Only a test of the family's own home shows whether its air adds to that toll.

Refining the science on radon made its toll look worse. The U.S. Environmental Protection Agency reports that in 2003 it combined the National Academy of Sciences' BEIR VI findings into one model and raised its best estimate of radon deaths to about 21,000 a year. The agency consulted its Science Advisory Board on the revision, so the higher number reflects a scientific review and not only one office's calculation. When the EPA refined the estimate, it ended up with a larger toll. That leaves less room for the hope that earlier fears about radon were overblown.

The 21,000 figure is a best estimate inside a wide range, not the worst case. By the EPA's reckoning, the yearly toll could be as low as 8,000 deaths or as high as 45,000. Even the low end of that range means thousands of Americans dying of lung cancer every year, and no reading of the uncertainty makes radon a minor risk. The doubt in the estimate gives a household a reason to treat the familiar figure as a best guess and no reason to relax.

Federal health agencies give the public the number the EPA calculated in 2003. The CDC repeats the EPA's estimate of yearly U.S. deaths as the national toll on its radon page, which it updated Aug. 26, 2026. The EPA also said it would revise A Citizen's Guide to Radon, the handbook it writes for homeowners, to reflect its new reckoning of the risk. That revision carries the updated science to people deciding whether to test their homes rather than leaving it in a technical report.

The estimate can't show whether any one house adds to that toll. Only a test of that house's air can answer that.

Every official warning about radon ends in the same instruction to households: test your home. The U.S. Surgeon General's warning sets the point for action at 4 pCi/L (150 Bq/m3) or more, according to the U.S. Environmental Protection Agency. That number tells a family when to fix the house, not when the air is safe. "There is no known safe level of radon," the CDC said. A reading under 4 means only that the home sits below the level at which the government urges repairs.

The concern reaches far past American homes. As many as 15% of the world's lung cancers may come from radon, the World Health Organization estimated in 2009. That estimate is an upper limit, but even as a limit it makes radon a global health problem rather than a local worry. In many countries, radon in homes accounts for 50% of the natural radiation people are exposed to. The house is a major source of that exposure, and it is also the place where people can find and fix the problem. U.S. and international health officials give the same advice: measure the air where you live, because no reading taken anywhere else can stand in for it.

Radon stays in homes through neglect rather than difficulty. Dr. Michael Repacholi, coordinator of WHO's Radiation and Environmental Health Unit, says the world has paid little attention to a risk that people can lower without much trouble. The fixes are ordinary home repairs, such as sealing cracks and covering the bare earth floors of crawl spaces, according to Department of Housing and Urban Development advice reported by the CDC. The CDC also recommends testing again after repairs, so a household measures whether the work lowered its level instead of assuming it did.

The national and international warnings differ in reach but send a family through the same steps: measure the air, repair the house if the reading calls for it, and measure again.

Michael Carrick urged Manchester United to sign Morgan Rogers ahead of Chelsea in the summer transfer window, according to reports.

13 foods tied to less inflammation, and what the evidence actually shows

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Free daily briefing on global business news.

Thirteen foods linked to lower inflammation, from berries to cherries, sorted by what research measured in people and what it didn't.

Inflammation is supposed to end. The body sends immune cells to an injury or an infection, they do their work, and the swelling goes down. Long-term inflammation is different, because it lingers instead of ending. That is why food is such an appealing answer to it. Groceries are cheap, people buy them every week, and no one needs a prescription for a carton of berries, so it is easy to believe that prevention can come home with the shopping. Many foods owe their reputation to a molecule inside them rather than to the food itself. How much a food can do depends on where researchers found that molecule's effect and at what dose.

The strongest results in people often come from doses that no ordinary serving supplies. The trials behind turmeric tested curcumin plus piperine supplements rather than the spice in a curry, according to Frontiers in Nutrition. Behind other foods, the newest research hasn't reached people at all. Swinburne University of Technology says its researchers are looking for money to test broccoli's key compound in people, a step the work has not yet taken. USDA ARS reports that intervention studies are still needed for its mushroom findings as well. A promising result and a proven one are not the same thing. None of this makes the foods useless. A bowl of mushrooms or a head of broccoli supplies a smaller and less certain version of what researchers measured in a lab or a capsule. The sensible approach is to treat these foods as a pattern rather than a prescription: eat them often and together, and don't expect any one of them to work like a drug.

Healthline set out to find foods that ease long-term inflammation in the body, a search that took in turmeric, dark chocolate, mushrooms and cherries, among others. These foods don't stand on equal footing. Some have a measured drop in an inflammation marker in people who ate or took them, while others have only a believable explanation of how they might work. Several foods also depend on company, because the body absorbs them better or they work better alongside a partner food, so how people eat them matters as much as whether they eat them at all. The useful test for each food is how strong its proof is and which meal it belongs in.

Strawberries have a result measured in people who ate them, not just a molecule that looks promising in theory. Strawberry eaters in a 2016 study, all of them adults carrying excess weight, ended up with less of the inflammation markers connected to heart disease, according to Healthline. A finding tied to eating a fruit is easier to act on than one tied to an extract, because it involves food a shopper buys, rinses and eats. The people in that study shared a health profile, though, so the result may not hold the same way for a lean, healthy reader. It shows a link between eating strawberries and lower markers, and that link is a reason to keep berries in the weekly rotation. It does not make them a treatment for heart disease.

Berries are usually treated as one family of fruit, but only one member of it has a measured result behind it. Blueberries, raspberries, blackberries and strawberries all carry a group of antioxidants called anthocyanins, and that compound is why berries count as anti-inflammatory food. It is also the whole reason blueberries, raspberries and blackberries are included, since no measured drop in inflammation markers is reported for any of the three. A bowl of blueberries has the same kind of compound as a bowl of strawberries without the same proof in people. For a shopper, the sensible reading is that strawberries have the evidence and the other three have the promise. A mix of berries is a good habit for anyone eating to ease inflammation, as long as they know that the measured result belongs to strawberries alone.

Fatty fish is on the list because of a specific claim that names two fats and one measurable sign of inflammation. Salmon and other oily fish are rich in the omega-3 fats EPA and DHA, which lower CRP, a blood marker of inflammation, according to Healthline. That gives a reader something concrete to weigh, rather than a loose promise that fish is good for you. The important word is "fatty." The benefit comes with the fat, so the oily fish are the ones that count, and seafood in general does not offer the same thing.

Part of the benefit appears only after the meal, when EPA and DHA are converted inside the eater's body into resolvins and protectins. A person who eats the fish takes in the raw ingredients, and that conversion completes the work. The fats are part of the fish itself, with no capsule involved. That makes fish a dinner choice rather than a medicine, and it suits a way of eating that people repeat week after week.

Salmon is the obvious choice, but it is only one of several good sources. Herring, sardines, anchovies, and mackerel are all rich in EPA and DHA as well, which lets a cook switch fish from one meal to the next. That range makes the advice easier to keep up over many months than a single "superfood" purchase would be. A tin of sardines at lunch and a salmon fillet at dinner both qualify.

Put oily fish on the menu often, and choose whichever kind you enjoy most.

Broccoli is on this list because of one compound that targets inflammation at its source, not because of anything measured in people who ate the vegetable. Its sulforaphane suppresses inflammatory cytokines and NF-κB signaling, according to Harvard Health Publishing. Those are the chemical messengers and the signaling inside cells that keep inflammation going, so the claim is that sulforaphane turns down the process itself, and that is why broccoli sounds so promising. That account explains how broccoli might help, but it says nothing about how much it would help. No serving size, dose or drop in an inflammation marker comes with it, so a reader who wants to eat broccoli for inflammation has no portion to aim for. Broccoli's case rests on a believable process, with no measured result in people attached to it.

The newest serious research on sulforaphane is aimed at a rare inherited nerve disease, not at everyday inflammation. Swinburne University of Technology reports that sulforaphane increased levels of frataxin, a protein, in work on Friedreich ataxia led by Associate Professor Faith Kwa. Approximately 200 Australians live with that disease, and the work serves that small group of patients rather than shoppers deciding what to cook. The research asks whether the compound can raise frataxin, not whether broccoli calms inflammation, so its results can't confirm the vegetable's reputation on this list.

Scientists' interest in sulforaphane is a fair reason to keep eating broccoli, but it signals potential, not a promise about anyone's inflammation.

The case for avocados rests on people who ate the fruit itself, at the table, rather than on an extract of one of its compounds. Researchers recorded lower IL-1β and CRP, two markers of inflammation, after 51 adults spent 12 weeks eating avocado in a 2019 study, according to Healthline. That is a measured result in people from an ordinary food that anyone can buy and slice. The study was small, though. It is a real signal in humans, but it says less about the wider public than a larger trial would. Its length also matters for anyone planning meals around it, because the participants kept eating avocado for the full 12 weeks. Whatever avocados do for inflammation, they do it through steady eating over time, not through one serving or one salad.

Avocados also contain more than one family of compounds that interest researchers, so their appeal does not depend on a single molecule. A lower risk of heart disease has been tied to two of those families, the fruit's tocopherols and carotenoids. That link is an association, which makes it a reason for hope rather than a proven effect. It is also softer evidence than the drop in inflammation markers among the adults in the 2019 study, and that trial remains the stronger reason to put avocados in the shopping cart. A reader who eats them for their heart is acting on promise, while a reader who eats them for inflammation has a result in people behind the choice.

Keep avocados on the plate through the week as part of a way of eating, and don't count on them as heart medicine.

Green tea is on the list because of one molecule in it, a compound called EGCG, rather than because of the drink as a whole. Harvard Health reports that EGCG limits the output of inflammatory cytokines, the proteins that drive inflammation, and shields the fats in cells from damage. That gives the compound a role at two stages, curbing the signals that start inflammation and reducing the harm it causes once it begins. Both effects play out inside cells, though. They explain how green tea could help without showing that it helps anyone who drinks it, which places green tea with the foods whose case rests on a mechanism alone.

Green tea's healthy image comes from a different kind of evidence than the inflammation claim needs. The drink's good name rests on its links to lower disease risk rather than on a measured drop in an inflammation marker. That leaves open whether what EGCG does in a cell carries over to what a daily cup does in a person's body. A reader who drinks green tea for its general reputation should keep that reputation apart from the narrower claim that it eases inflammation.

None of this is a reason to stop drinking green tea. It is cheap and easy to make, and it fits the pattern of eating this slideshow describes, in which many good foods eaten often matter more than any single one. Drink it because you enjoy it and because it belongs in a sound diet.

Just don't brew a pot expecting it to lower your inflammation.

Peppers make the list for a group of everyday nutrients rather than one famous molecule. Healthline reports that the vitamin C, quercetin, and sinapic and ferulic acids in peppers may help calm inflammation. Those are common nutrients, and ordinary peppers in any produce section contain them. The kind of pepper a shopper picks still changes what ends up in the cart. Sweet bell peppers supply the quercetin, and both bell peppers and chilies provide vitamin C. Only chilies carry capsaicin, the compound that makes a pepper hot, according to Tokyo University of Science. A shopper who treats peppers as one food misses that the heat may be the more interesting part of the story.

Chilies have the stronger case in the lab, because capsaicin had the strongest effect of any compound tested alone, according to Tokyo University of Science. Even so, researchers saw the largest effect only when they paired capsaicin with other compounds. "When capsaicin and menthol or 1,8-cineole were used together, their anti-inflammatory effect increased several hundred-fold compared to when each compound was used alone," said Gen-ichiro Arimura, a professor at Tokyo University of Science. That increase describes a mixture of purified compounds, not a chili on a plate.

Nobody has measured the effect of the nutrients or of capsaicin in people who eat peppers. No human trial or inflammation marker supports the nutrient claim. The capsaicin result came from mouse immune cells in a lab dish, according to Tokyo University of Science. Peppers therefore belong with the foods backed by a plausible mechanism. They are worth eating, but they are not a proven remedy.

Eat peppers, sweet or hot, for the vitamin C they supply and in well-seasoned dishes, and treat the capsaicin finding as a reason for curiosity, not a dose to chase.

Mushrooms are on this list for the compounds they contain, not for any effect measured in a person's body. Healthline says the phenols and antioxidants in mushrooms give anti-inflammatory protection. That claim names compounds, and no dose or drop in an inflammation marker comes with it. The closest test of mushrooms against inflammation was a cell culture experiment focused on brain inflammation, according to USDA ARS. That leaves mushrooms with a plausible mechanism and nothing more. They are also very low in calories, so a cook who adds them to meals takes on little risk even if the benefit stays uncertain. That makes them an easy, low-risk part of a way of eating, not a treatment.

The word "mushroom" covers thousands of varieties around the world, and the lab evidence covers only one of them. The researchers tested a single species, the king oyster, Pleurotus eryngii, USDA ARS reports, and their results can't be assumed to apply to all edible mushrooms. Commercially grown mushrooms such as truffles, portobello and shiitake were not part of that work. A cook who grills a portobello has no test result for that mushroom to rely on.

The lab work did find real anti-inflammatory activity in the king oyster. Nitrite production, one marker of inflammation, was significantly lower in cells treated with every extract and compound the researchers tested, according to USDA ARS. Researchers at Universite Grenoble Alpes got their strongest results from an acetone extract and an ethanol extract, both rich in N-acetyltryptamine, or NAT, and from pure NAT. Ergothioneine, another compound they tested, was not among the best performers. None of these results has yet been tested in people who eat king oysters.

Grill or sauté whichever mushrooms you like, and count the king oyster findings as early lab work rather than a reason to eat more of them.

Grapes are prized for two compounds inside them, anthocyanins and resveratrol, rather than for the fruit as a whole. Researchers can therefore study the benefit, and companies can sell it, without anyone eating a grape. The human evidence for grapes comes from people who were already seriously ill. In a 2020 study, 60 heart-failure patients showed lower IL-6, a marker of inflammation, according to Healthline. A result in heart patients tells a healthy reader little about what grapes would do for them. The patients also took resveratrol rather than eating the fruit, so the finding belongs to one compound taken at a steady dose. Any real benefit from grapes depends on the compounds, so the question that matters at the table is how much of them a person needs.

Grape compounds lowered inflammation in people only when people took them daily for months, the way a patient takes a treatment. In one high-quality systematic review, CRP, another inflammation marker, dropped only for people who took more than 500 mg of grape polyphenols daily and kept it up for 12 weeks or longer, according to News-Medical. The heart-failure patients likewise took their resveratrol every day for 3 months. A person who snacks on grapes now and then gets a smaller and less regular amount of these compounds, so neither result carries over to a fruit bowl. Even at those doses, the improvement was a number in the blood, and a better blood reading is not the same as fewer heart attacks or strokes.

Eat grapes as a sweet, easy part of a diet full of fruit, and don't expect a handful to do what months of daily doses did.

Turmeric earns its place with a result measured in people, not a process seen in a lab dish. In a 2015 trial, people who took 1 gram of curcumin, turmeric's active compound, with piperine every day had lower levels of CRP, a blood marker of inflammation, according to Healthline. Researchers compared them with a group given a placebo, Frontiers in Nutrition reports, and everyone in the trial had metabolic syndrome. The drop showed up in patients with a diagnosed condition, not in healthy people eating a spiced meal. That makes turmeric one of the few foods here with a result in people, though the result belongs to a pairing.

Black pepper supplies much of what makes curcumin count, and piperine raises the body's absorption of curcumin by 2,000%. Piperine is a compound in black pepper that slows the rate at which the body clears curcumin and helps the gut take more of it in, according to a review of trials in Frontiers in Nutrition. Much of the trial's success therefore depends on an ingredient that isn't turmeric at all. Researchers tested the two together, and no one has yet worked out how much of the benefit comes from each. Anyone who cooks with turmeric should put black pepper in the same pot. That is the closest kitchen version of the tested pairing, but a pinch in a curry is a sensible habit, not a measured dose.

Curcumin's record in people is real but uneven, and it is more modest than the label "strong anti-inflammatory" suggests. Most of the trials in the review lowered CRP or IL-6, but some did not, according to Frontiers in Nutrition. The size of the drop also varied, with a significant CRP decrease in the 2015 trial and only a marginal one in a 2024 trial. The 2015 result sits at the strong end of that range. No trial ran longer than 12 weeks, so nobody yet knows whether the drop holds over the long stretch that chronic inflammation lasts.

Turmeric's promise depends on its partner and its dose, so use it as a seasoning alongside black pepper, not as a treatment.

Extra virgin olive oil earns its place on two counts: an antioxidant it contains and its role in Mediterranean cooking. Healthline reports that the effect of oleocanthal, an antioxidant in the oil, has been compared to that of ibuprofen, and that research suggests a Mediterranean diet combined with the oil may significantly lower inflammatory markers. The ibuprofen comparison applies to oleocanthal's effect, not to the oil as a whole. A drizzle over a salad is therefore not a dose of a painkiller. The comparison describes how one compound behaves, and it does not mean a bottle of oil can do a pill's job.

Any drop in inflammatory markers is tied to the oil and the diet together, not to the oil by itself. The oil is a staple of Mediterranean cooking rather than an occasional extra, so its possible benefit is hard to separate from the meals it usually appears in. People who hope for that benefit would look for it in using the oil across many meals as part of how they eat. A spoonful taken by itself each morning is not the habit the research points to.

What a shopper fully controls is the bottle. People get less anti-inflammatory benefit from refined olive oils than from extra virgin, so a cheaper refined oil gives up part of what makes olive oil worth buying for inflammation. The words "extra virgin" on the label are the part of the name that carries the claim.

Buy the extra virgin bottle and cook it into Mediterranean-style meals, and treat it as an everyday habit rather than a cure.

Cocoa earns its place with a change researchers measured in people's arteries within hours of a dose of its flavanols. In a 2022 study, blood vessels worked better 3 to 8 hours after people took those compounds, which are credited with arterial health, according to Healthline. That timing sets cocoa apart from foods whose case rests on a process seen in cells alone, because the change showed up on the same day as the dose. The improvement came in how well the arteries did their work.

The result came from a deliberate, repeated dose rather than casual nibbling. The study's participants had their 852 mg flavanol dose twice each day, a schedule that turns a treat into a routine. Whatever their arteries gained came with keeping up that intake, not with an occasional square after dinner. The figure also counts flavanols, the active ingredient, rather than chocolate. No shopper can read that dose off a wrapper.

The usual advice for choosing a bar is to pick chocolate with at least 70% cocoa, which means more cocoa and less of everything else in the bar. That rule is buying advice and does not come from the 2022 study. One number counts cocoa content and the other counts flavanols, and nothing on a label connects the two.

Buy the darker bar because it holds more cocoa and less candy, but don't expect a few squares to match what people in the study got from their twice-daily dose.

Tomatoes are on this list because of a single compound, and that compound comes with a condition about how they are cooked. Lycopene may help lower pro-inflammatory compounds linked to several types of cancer, Healthline reports, and the body takes in more of it when tomatoes are cooked in olive oil. The possible benefit and the cooking instruction arrive together. How a cook prepares tomatoes therefore matters as much as whether they end up on the plate. That condition follows from the kind of compound lycopene is, a carotenoid.

The body absorbs carotenoids better when they are eaten with fat, and that is why olive oil goes in the pan with tomatoes. A cook who wants the benefit can simmer tomatoes in olive oil, an easy step in a sauce or a stew. That turns the tomato into part of a two-food habit, and it shows why these foods work better as a way of eating than as a set of separate remedies.

Even when they are cooked well, tomatoes support a narrower claim than readers might expect. The compounds that lycopene may help lower are the ones tied to cancers. The case for tomatoes cites no measured drop in CRP or any other inflammation marker in people who ate them. Tomatoes therefore have a plausible mechanism and a sensible cooking tip, not a proven result. They belong in the meal as a regular ingredient, not as a fix to check against a lab number.

Cook tomatoes in olive oil whenever you can, and count them as one good habit among many rather than a remedy.

Cherries have an anti-inflammatory effect that researchers measured in people's blood, not only in cells in a dish. In a 2019 trial, 37 seniors who drank cherry juice ended up with lower levels of CRP, a marker of inflammation, according to Healthline. The fruit's anthocyanins and catechins are the compounds credited with that drop. A lower CRP reading is a change inside the body rather than a theory about how a compound might behave. The seniors drank the juice for 12 weeks, so anyone hoping for the same result would need a daily habit rather than an occasional glass. They also got their dose from a drink anyone could pour at breakfast instead of from a concentrated pill.

One small trial in older adults cannot settle the case for a whole fruit. A shorter 2021 study gave tart cherry juice to healthy younger adults and found no such effect. The two results describe different groups of people, so they don't cancel each other out. The lower CRP may hold only for older adults like the ones in the 2019 trial. Cherries pass the test of showing a measured effect in one group of people, but not the test of working for everyone. Shoppers who want to act on the research should look for tart cherries, the variety researchers have studied most.

Eat cherries as one regular part of an anti-inflammatory diet, not as a food to count on to lower inflammation by itself.

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/health-tech/zus-health-secures-33m-build-infrastructure-ai-powered-clinical-workflows" hreflang="en">Zus Health secures $33M to build the infrastructure for AI-powered clinical workflows</a>

📅 2026-11-12  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →
Zus Health secures $33M to build the infrastructure for AI-powered clinical workflows" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

As healthcare companies race to build AI copilots and agents, startup Zus Health is betting that the bigger opportunity is creating the connected patient data layer those applications need to work reliably.

Zus Health, Jonathan Bush's health tech company, picked up $33 million in series B funding to build out the data foundation for healthcare AI. Drive Capital led the series B round. Sanofi Ventures, HealthX Ventures and Alumni Ventures, as well as existing investors Andreessen Horowitz (a16z), F-Prime, Maverick Ventures and JAZZ Venture Partners also backed the round.

The financing brings Zus's total capital raised to more than $100 million, and the fresh funding will support the company's continued investment in its intelligent patient record platform, national healthcare data network and AI-powered clinical workflows.

Zus has built its platform in three layers, according to Bush. The company first focused on building a nationwide patient record, a data network that pulls information into that record, and increasingly, AI-enabled workflows that automate care tasks using a more complete view of the patient.

Electronic health record systems (EHRs) succeeded at digitizing healthcare but failed to make patient information portable across organizations, Bush, founder and CEO of Zus Health, told Fierce Healthcare. Clinicians and care teams still spend valuable time searching for records and reconciling incomplete information. For patients, that can mean repeating their medical history at every appointment or relying on memory to recall past treatments. Advances in artificial intelligence now offer an opportunity to make healthcare information not just accessible, but actionable, according to Zus Health executives.

Launched in 2021, Zus Health developed what Bush calls an "always-on, FHIR-native, API-first medical record." It offers a shared health data platform designed to accelerate healthcare data interoperability by providing easy-to-use patient data at the point of care via a standalone app, API (application programming interface), embedded components and direct EHR integration. At the center of its approach is an intelligent, continuously updated patient record that serves as a shared foundation for healthcare applications.

The company's Zus Aggregated Profile (ZAP) brings fragmented clinical information together into a unified, longitudinal patient record, supported by AI-powered clinical intelligence that makes the information easier to use across applications and workflows, according to the company. The platform also has nationwide connectivity across EHR networks, pharmacy data, care event notifications, labs and other clinical data sources that continuously updates the patient record without requiring customers to build their own connectivity infrastructure.

"We're finally on to the last and critical piece of what I think the future of medical records looks like, which is standing up workflows that automate all of the tasks of patient care in a very bespoke way," Bush told Fierce Healthcare. "[Zus Health] developed the first data-rich networked medical record platform in the industry's history. Whether Zeus wins or somebody else beats Zeus at it, all electronic medical records in the future will be national."

He added, "And now, with AI, we have an extraordinary opportunity to build workflows that actually make delivering care easier, faster and more effective."

The company is betting that the next competitive player in healthcare AI will be the data infrastructure underneath the applications.

Zus Health, a Fierce 15 of 2025 honoree, wants to use its nationwide data network and longitudinal patient record as the infrastructure on which AI-powered clinical applications can run. The company's platform enables EHRs and other applications to work from a more complete, cross-provider view of the patient. Zus Health built AI solutions and flexible integration capabilities to put connected information to work across clinical and operational use cases, while enabling healthcare organizations to build applications tailored to their needs.

Zus works with value-based care organizations, digital health companies, provider organizations, as well as diagnostics and precision medicine companies. The company claims that in August alone its nationwide network supported more than 110 million networked transactions, while its AI-native clinical platform generated 39 million evidence citations year-to-date across its solutions—demonstrating both the scale of its connectivity and how patient information is being put to work across healthcare.

Zus supports solutions spanning pre-visit preparation, transitions of care, risk adjustment, quality gap closure and population health management. Its clinical intelligence capabilities, including AI-powered generated patient summaries (GPS), turn fragmented patient records into information care teams can use more efficiently and confidently.

The company plans to use the series B funding to build out its AI-powered clinical intelligence and capabilities and broaden its healthcare data network connectivity. Zus plans to expand its solutions to automate population health and care management workflows, while enabling customers and developers to create AI-powered workflows across value-based care, specialty care and other high-value use cases.

The company is seeing rapid growth in specialty care, particularly oncology, where complex patient records are spread across multiple providers. Zeus’s continuously updated, aggregated patient data enables more comprehensive and efficient reviews of treatment eligibility, clinical trial criteria and genetic testing—tasks that traditionally required extensive manual work by nurses and analysts using fragmented legacy EHR systems, Bush noted.

"We believe healthcare is entering a new era in which AI can fundamentally change how care is delivered, but that transformation depends on having reliable, connected patient information," said Molly Bonakdarpour, general partner at Drive Capital, in a statement. "Zus has built the foundation to address that challenge at scale. Its combination of technology, market opportunity and leadership gives us conviction in the company's ability to help shape the next generation of healthcare."

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/digital-health/medical-transport-booking-platform-medhaul-raises-41m-expand" hreflang="en">Medical transport platform MedHaul, focused on high-needs patients, raises $4.1M</a>

📅 2026-11-12  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →
Medical transport platform MedHaul, focused on high-needs patients, raises $4.1M" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

MedHaul, a platform for coordinating medical transport, has raised $4.1 million in an unnamed round, Fierce Healthcare has learned exclusively.

The strategic round, which brings MedHaul’s total raised to date to $7 million, was led by Black Ops VC, with participation from Pier 70 Ventures, Seae Ventures, Stanford University, 450 Ventures (BCBS of Alabama), LaunchTN and American Cancer Society’s BrightEdge.

The latest capital will be used to expand deeper in oncology, which represents 60% of rides booked on the platform, and to support a forthcoming Epic integration.

Millions of Americans are affected by transportation barriers to care, and the Centers for Medicare and Medicaid Services estimates that these barriers account for a quarter or more of missed clinic appointments. Missed appointments are associated with mortality among patients with chronic conditions and cost the system billions annually, according to one 2017 estimate.

MedHaul is a B2B platform working with providers and payers. Its tech helps providers match patients with medical transport according to acuity and accommodation needs, such as a wheelchair or stretcher. Care teams can use MedHaul to schedule and track transportation, with same-day and advanced bookings available. They can also manage billing, communicate with patients and identify those at greater risk of missing an appointment. MedHaul also rolled out a newer clinical trial product designed to support CROs and pharma clients.

The existing medical transport ecosystem is “very antiquated” and “heavily manual,” MedHaul founder and CEO Erica Plybeah told Fierce Healthcare. Many transport companies are small businesses that are not tech-savvy, or that need business development support. When MedHaul first entered the market, in 2019, some vendors didn’t even have a website or a social media page, Plybeah noted. And manual back-and-forths between care teams and ride providers means it can take upwards of two hours just to book a ride.

MedHaul’s network spans 17 states, primarily serving patients in the cancer, kidney disease, maternal health and behavioral health spaces. It also supports hospital discharges and rural patients. It has about 600 transport companies in its network.

MedHaul has also launched an enterprise platform, designed for large health systems and multi-location practices. Through a partnership with Uber Health, ride share and non-emergency medical transport bookings are available in one place, on the platform. The platform also provides real-time analytics into patient mobility, enabled by AI.

"There are opportunities in healthcare where AI is bridging the gap to solve for unmet needs—and that’s exactly what Erica and her team are doing at MedHaul,” James Norman, co-founder & managing partner at Black Ops VC, said in a statement. The user-friendly interface and focus on high-acuity communities “make MedHaul an attractive investment, and we’re proud to help them scale.”

Plybeah, who grew up in the Deep South with a grandmother who had diabetic and a double-leg amputee, saw how transportation issues affected her access to care. Throughout her career in clinical and research informatics, including at Epic Systems and St. Jude Children’s Research Hospital, Plybeah “saw how transportation impacted institutions, when patients don’t show up on time, don’t show up at all, show up at the wrong facility … and it brought me back to being a little girl in the Mississippi Delta.”

Over half of MedHaul’s rides require mobility assistance, and 42% live in rural communities. MedHaul’s riders averaged eight rides per year in 2025.

Patients typically don’t pay anything for the rides booked on MedHaul. Instead, the MedHaul client—a provider or payer, for instance—sponsors the rides, often from an existing medical transport budget. In specialties with high transport volumes, providers may also use a patient’s benefit to find rides.

Same-day rides are available within 30 minutes to an hour, depending on acuity. For higher-acuity patients, a booking can take two to three hours, depending on the complexity of the transport required. MedHaul sees a 97% ride completion rate. Providers using MedHaul have reduced missed appointments by 25% and improved patient appointment timeliness by 52%, the company claims.

MedHaul has long used AI across its business. It has a conversational AI agent for older adults who prefer phone calls to texting. It also leverages AI agents to book rides within seconds for highly complex patients, and offers care teams MedHaul GPT, which they can use to ask questions about their programs and patients.

“We really prioritize good implementation,” Plybeah said, noting that there has been an explosion in digital health point solutions, but implementation is a whole different question. “We want this process to be as easy as possible for the end users.”

The company, which launched in 2019, is a public benefit corporation and a certified B Corp. While it’s still a for-profit business, its structure requires it to pursue a stated public benefit alongside financial returns. “We wanted our mission to extend beyond profit and to attract the right partners, investors, employees and customers who shared that commitment,” Plybeah said. “Our social mission is embedded in how we operate, build technology, serve patients and choose the people and organizations we work with.”

Plybeah is excited that there is more industry awareness of the social determinants of health. In the early MedHaul days, “folks seemed to think of transportation as a separate service,” she said. “And now I am grateful to see that folks are really considering transportation as part of the patient’s care journey, because it is.”

Mauricio Pochettino faces nightmare Man United start as it emerges he WILL take charge of USA game in early hours of Wednesday - cutting preparation time for debut trip to Liverpool

📅 2026-11-12  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

Published: 10:58 EST, 12 November 2026 | Updated: 12:14 EST, 12 November 2026

Mauricio Pochettino will face a brutal start to life at Manchester United with the prospective Red Devils boss set to manage the United States days before his debut at Liverpool.

United have moved closer to putting Pochettino in charge at Old Trafford after reaching a verbal agreement with the US national team boss on Wednesday. The club then made a formal approach to US Soccer.

Daily Mail Sport understands Pochettino will take charge of both of the United States matches during the international window.

The US take on Haiti in the CONCACAF Nations League quarter-final first leg on Saturday, with the second fixture then taking place in Cincinnati on Tuesday evening, but would likely finish at around 2am GMT.

Pochettino would then have to travel to Manchester following the match to link up with the Red Devils' squad for the first time, likely on Thursday.

The Argentine would then have limited time with his players before a daunting trip to Anfield to face rivals Liverpool on Sunday in a blockbuster first match in charge.

Mauricio Pochettino will take charge of the United States upcoming matches against Haiti

Pochettino will coach in the early hours on Wednesday morning in Cincinnati before travelling to Manchester

The Argentine would have limited time to prepare his side for Sunday's match at Anfield

Man United are said to be relaxed over Pochettino taking charge of his final two matches as US boss before starting as their new head coach.

It will cost Pochettino around £7million to buy himself out of his contract with the US Soccer Federation, with United ready to foot the bill.

The cost of bringing in his back-room team, including trusted assistants Jesus Perez and Miguel D’Agostino, will take the cost above £10million.

The 54-year-old only signed a new deal after the World Cup meaning that United could have got him for nothing in the summer.

Pochettino is set to take over a Man United side languishing in the Premier League relegation zone following just one win in their opening ten matches.

The trip to Anfield would be the first of a run of eight matches in 29 days, as Man United juggle Premier League and Champions League football.

Pochettino is set to succeed Michael Carrick, whose departure as the Red Devils' boss was officially announced on Wednesday morning.

Pochettino will face a tight turnaround to prepare his new side for the daunting clash on Merseyside

Carrick arrived at United's training ground at 7.50am this morning and was dismissed. He left at 10.18am, less than two and a half hours later. He is the first Premier League manager to be sacked this season, followed hours later by bottom-of-the-table Crystal Palace boss Pierre Sage.

In a statement released at 10.37am, Carrick's sacking was confirmed. United's statement read: 'After a disappointing start to the season, the club has come to the extremely difficult conclusion that a change is necessary in order to achieve our ambitions.

'Michael is, and always will be, a Manchester United legend. Just as he did as a player, he has conducted himself with the highest standards of dedication and professionalism throughout his time back with the club. His leadership and achievements last season will always be fondly remembered.'
