Jensen Huang's exit from Nvidia raises critical questions about the future of AI

📅 2026-11-12  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Michael Dell described Jensen Huang as a "visionary of the AI age." Deutsche Telekom CEO Tim Höttges said a photo with the Nvidia CEO produced more media coverage for his company than its iPhone launch. During the keynote of his annual Dreamforce conference, Salesforce's Marc Benioff said, "Jensen, we're all following you, bro," as the two paced through the audience.

Follow CNBC's live coverage of Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang's resignation

His fame extended way beyond tech. In May, Huang joined President Donald Trump on Air Force One for a trip to China to meet with President Xi Jinping. Three months later, Huang gathered six of the biggest names at the Nasdaq to announce on CNBC a proposed $500 billion artificial intelligence buildout plan, cementing his central role at the intersection of Wall Street and Silicon Valley.

As Nvidia became the first company to rip past a $5 trillion market cap, bending the laws of physics with its advanced graphics processing units and pushing the AI boom to seemingly endless bounds, the now 63-year-old Huang, clad in a black leather jacket, traversed the globe as the face of the brand powering the next industrial revolution.

Over the past week it all came crashing down. Following two reported extramarital affairs — one with an alleged Chinese spy and another with an employee — Huang is leaving the company he co-founded 33 years ago in crisis, and thrusting the AI market into chaos. The board is now left to pick a successor for a company that's been run by a singular figure with no second-in-command. Chief Financial Officer Colette Kress has been named interim CEO. Harvey Jones has been appointed chairman.

Nvidia has not responded to multiple requests for comment.

"How companies handle a crisis determines their future," former Cisco CEO John Chambers, now a startup investor, told CNBC's "Fast Money" on Wednesday. "How they handle this will determine if they're a great company and a great leadership team beyond one person."

It had been a tumultuous stretch for Nvidia, even before the Huang saga led the board to launch an investigation of the CEO's behavior and the potential national security implications. The stock is on pace for its sixth straight weekly drop, which would be the longest losing streak since 2012. That includes a 9% slump last week after the midterm elections resulted in sweeping Democratic victories in both chambers of Congress, and the passage of dozens of ballot measures that could slow the buildout of new data centers.

Nvidia is scheduled to report quarterly results on Tuesday, and expectations remain high, with analysts projecting 92% year-over-year revenue growth to $109.2 billion, according to LSEG. That's up 18-fold from the same quarter in 2022, just before OpenAI launched ChatGPT and kicked off the generative AI era.

It's a historic level of growth for any business, and particularly notable for a company that spent most of its first few decades as a niche technology provider for video games.

Huang founded Nvidia to make chips for computer graphics, both for video games and rendering. In the early 2000s, he and other executives saw an opportunity to put GPUs to work for computationally intense projects. That technology eventually turned Nvidia into the most important player in AI and a key American asset for national defense.

In the process, Huang became the ultimate decider, choosing how, when and where GPUs get allocated. The biggest buyers were the hyperscalers — Amazon, Google, Meta and Microsoft — which needed the chips to build their own AI services and make them available to cloud customers. OpenAI, meanwhile, was snapping up GPUs for its powerful models and consumer apps.

To expand his customer base, Huang designated compute capacity to a number of so-called neoclouds like CoreWeave and Nebius, putting equity into the companies and helping them build big businesses around renting out GPUs.

Oracle gobbled up billions of dollars worth of GPUs, going heavily into debt in the process. Elon Musk's xAI, now part of SpaceX, did the same to build its Grok model, before eventually starting to rent out excess capacity to Anthropic and Google.

Huang wanted the world standardizing on Nvidia. Last year he demanded that Oracle buy additional Nvidia networking hardware if it wanted allocation to its state-of-the-art Grace Blackwell chips, said two people familiar with the deal who asked not to be named because the negotiations were private.

In August, Nvidia said it will provide up to $105 billion in financing for an OpenAI data center in Ohio that could be the largest of its kind in the world.

"He created the product that started the industry," said Chris Miglino, CEO of neocloud Axe Compute. "Without his leadership we may be many years behind where we are today with AI chip technology, which is the driving force behind the neocloud market."

When it comes to investing cash in fledgling startups, Huang is known to host entrepreneurs at Nvidia's Silicon Valley headquarters. While there could be 30 Nvidia employees in the room for the pitch meeting, only Huang asks questions, according to people with knowledge of the matter, who requested anonymity because they're not authorized to speak publicly about it.

One source told CNBC that key aspects of project finance are so reliant on Huang's sign-off that, when he travels, those involved in the deal are forced to wait for his plane to land before learning if they can go forward or if terms have to be renegotiated.

Huang's ambitions extended well beyond the U.S. He wanted to be a force in China, which became a difficult market for Nvidia during the Biden administration and was virtually off-limits after Trump implemented strict export controls in his second term. Huang's aggressive pursuit of China may have been his undoing.

The woman, with whom the Nvidia CEO had a romantic affair, was identified by U.S. intelligence as having been recruited by China's Ministry of State Security to gather information on Huang, according to people briefed on the intelligence cited by The New York Times. She has business ties to Megaspeed International, a Singapore-based cloud computing firm and an Nvidia customer that's facing scrutiny over suspicions that it facilitated billions of dollars of restricted Nvidia chips to China.

U.S. lawmakers are up in arms, with many demanding investigations as well as Huang's resignation. Senate Banking Committee member Kevin Cramer, R-N.D., vocalized his concerns on Wednesday.

"What bothers me at the end of it is what we don't know is what she knows about him that can be utilized," Cramer told CNBC, right before Huang resigned and referring to the affair. "This is a guy that's right now so vulnerable to extortion, blackmail. I just don't think, as a national security matter, we can run the risk."

But industry experts are quick to emphasize how important Huang was to the health and growth of the broad AI ecosystem. He had the leading names on Wall Street on speed dial as he tried to turn GPUs into what he called an "investable asset" with an expanded roster of financial backers, and he had personal relationships with all the key figures in AI like OpenAI's Sam Altman and SoftBank's Masayoshi Son.

On the deal-making side, Huang's top lieutenants were Kress and Vishal Bhagwati, head of corporate development, sources said, though they add that all the big decisions were made by the CEO. And when seeking a financial backstop, the person behind Huang was Nico Caprez, the CEO's son-in-law and the company's vice president of global AI infrastructure growth, three people said.

Executives at numerous Nvidia customers, partners and portfolio companies told CNBC that Huang frequently emailed or called CEOs to offer his help in challenging times.

Tomás Puig, founder and CEO of enterprise AI startup Alembic, said that following the collapse of Silicon Valley Bank in 2023, his company was "down to weeks of cash and Nvidia was our biggest customer."

"I emailed Jensen, and they prepaid two years of invoices so we could keep the lights on and people kept their jobs," Puig told CNBC.

That approach persisted, as recently as this week. On Monday, a day after publicly apologizing for being unfaithful to his wife, Huang emailed the CEO of AI infrastructure startup Fireworks AI, according to a person with knowledge of the matter. Fireworks, which Nvidia first backed in 2024, was dealing with a series of high-profile cyberattacks.

Huang, whose name was suddenly front-page news for all the wrong reasons, was checking in, the person said, just to see if he could help.

— CNBC's Lora Kolodny, Jordan Novet and Seema Mody contributed to this report.

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/health-tech/texas-ag-drops-allegations-epic-restricts-parents-access-health-data-antitrust-lawsuit" hreflang="en">Texas AG narrows lawsuit against Epic, dropping claims over parental access settings</a>

📅 2026-11-12  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →
Texas AG narrows lawsuit against Epic, dropping claims over parental access settings" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

Texas is dropping a part of its lawsuit against Epic Systems, specifically the claims about how the company's software handles parental access to a child's electronic health records.

In December, Texas Attorney General Ken Paxton filed a lawsuit against Epic, alleging that the health tech company monopolizes the electronic health record market and cements its dominance in the market through an "anticompetitive playbook."

The lawsuit, filed in Tarrant County District Court, also accused the company of engaging in deceptive practices that restrict parental access to their minor children’s medical records. On Tuesday, the Texas AG's office filed a notice of partial non-suit indicating it was dropping the deceptive practices claims against Epic.

The notice of partial non-suit states that the decision was made "in light of Epic’s recent changes to its software."

In previous statements, an Epic spokesperson said the company "does not determine parental access to children’s medical records."

"Decisions about parental access to children’s medical records are made by doctors and health systems, not by Epic," the spokesperson said.

The state is still moving forward with its antitrust claims under the Texas Free Enterprise and Antitrust Act. The Texas Attorney General's office alleges Epic "uses a wide range of exclusionary tactics to prevent potential competition from its partners, customers and even its own employees." According to the suit, these anticompetitive practices harm Texas hospitals and Texas patients by raising costs and blocking innovative technologies.

In the lawsuit, Paxton alleged that Epic "exploits its control" over patient medical records to automatically hide children’s medication lists, treatment notes and provider messages from parents when their child reaches 12 years old. Texas alleged that Epic’s default proxy-access configurations impede parents’ ability to see their children’s health information.

"These deceptive practices undermine the fundamental right of parents to direct their children’s healthcare" and violate Texas law, which grants parents complete and unrestricted access to their children’s medical records, the attorney general's office said in the lawsuit.

In a statement issued in December, Paxton referred to Epic as a "woke corporation" and stated that Epic's default proxy-access configurations "undermine the sacred rights of parents to protect and oversee their kids’ medical well-being."

In a statement provided to Fierce Healthcare on Wednesday, an Epic spokesperson said the Texas lawsuit includes "significant inaccuracies, including claims about MyChart's customer proxy settings."

"The state filed a voluntary dismissal of those claims on Tuesday. Ken Paxton's office requested that we change our customers' system settings. We said no because those decisions should be made by doctors and health systems and not by Epic. The lawsuit followed. The State should dismiss the remaining claims," the Epic spokesperson said.

In a Jan. 20 filing in Tarrant County District Court, the electronic health record giant said it makes "highly configurable software so that its customers, healthcare organizations across all fifty states, can determine what workflows and functionalities are needed to achieve their own compliance obligations—including with respect to parental access rights to medical records."

"What might be compliant in Texas might not be compliant in another state and vice versa, and those laws may change from time to time, as the law just did roughly four months ago in Texas. Configurability helps customers bridge that gap," the company wrote in its filing.

Epic said it would not "unilaterally go into each of its customers’ systems to change their individualized settings."

Epic also argued, in its court filing, that the state failed to produce facts or legitimate legal bases to support any of its claims. "When Epic asked the State to identify any Epic customers it claimed were not in compliance with the new law, the State did not identify a single Epic customer. What appears to be going on here is that the State has decided to target Epic over a politicized issue regarding proxy access that Epic does not belong in the middle of. The State confirms as much in its press release, calling Epic a 'woke corporation' that supposedly 'undermine[s]' parents’ rights," Epic wrote in its answer to the lawsuit.

In its Answer and Affirmative Defenses, Epic said that the state's antitrust claims against it are pre-textual and baseless. Epic denied Paxton's allegations of anticompetitive behavior, citing a lack of evidence and factual flaws in the lawsuit.

The case will carry over into a changing political landscape in Texas. Paxton, who launched the lawsuit, is leaving the Texas attorney general's office after more than a decade, after his unsuccessful bid for the U.S. Senate. Democrat Nathan Johnson defeated Republican Mayes Middleton in the Nov. 3 midterm election, becoming the first Democrat elected Texas attorney general since 1994.

The leadership change could bring a shift in the office's litigation priorities. Paxton's tenure was marked by high-profile legal battles over social and cultural issues, including transgender healthcare and parental rights.

Last year, the Texas Office of the Attorney General secured a settlement with Austin Diagnostic Clinic that required full restoration of parental proxy access for children aged 12 to 17. Paxton has also issued civil investigative demands to other EHR software providers over parental access to minor children's medical records.

The case will also have a new judge. Democrat Katherine Kim defeated Republican incumbent H. Dustin "Dusty" Fillmore III in the race for Tarrant County's 236th Judicial District Court.

Disney's 'Star Wars' is coming to Cosm domes ahead of 50th anniversary as part of multiyear film deal

📅 2026-11-12  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

On Thursday, the immersive entertainment technology company announced a new studio partnership with The Walt Disney Co. that will bring the original "Star Wars" to its domes early next year as part of a multiyear, multifilm collaboration.

The financial terms of the deal were not disclosed.

Cosm's massive concave screens will drop film fans into a galaxy far, far away starting in March, just ahead of the Academy Award-winning film's 50th anniversary in May.

"We are thrilled to add the Disney partnership," Alexis Scalice, head of Cosm Studios, told CNBC. "We do look for those opportunities of these like anniversary and commemorative moments. We look for those opportunities to celebrate with the fans, but also find this mutually beneficial partnership between both parties."

This will be Cosm's sixth theatrical collaboration with Hollywood, having already partnered with Warner Bros. to bring "The Matrix," "Willy Wonka & the Chocolate Factory," "Harry Potter and the Sorcerer's Stone" and "It" to its locations. The company is set to launch immersive screenings of Universal's "Shrek" in December.

Each Cosm dome has stadium-style seating, with booths and tables set on each level. The dome's width fills much of the viewers' periphery, giving the sensation that they are part of the onscreen environment.

In this case, "Star Wars" will be displayed in a large rectangle in the center of the dome while the background is transformed into the iconic planets of Tatooine and Yavin IV that are featured in the film as well as the infamous Death Star. Audiences will also see elements that will make it appear like they're traveling at lightspeed and getting trapped in an intergalactic trash compactor.

Cosm is working directly with studios Lucasfilm and Industrial Light and Magic as well as creative studio MakeMake to create the visuals and the sound mixing for the unique presentation of the film.

"We look for those moments where sound, music ... listening to Darth Vader's breath for the first time ... you feel those moments differently in an 87-foot diameter dome and 12k resolution with the sound system we have," Scalice said. "And it's one of the things that with experiential cinema and our technology that we were so excited and honestly privileged that we can take these defining moments for fans and work closely with our partners that doesn't really change the authenticity of it, it just enhances it in a different manner."

The premium experience will also be paired with a themed menu. Cosm has done this with other titles, such as "Harry Potter and the Sorcerer's Stone," serving guests throughout the screening with house-made shepherd's pie, pretzel wands with cheese dip and a box of Bertie Bott's Every Flavour Beans.

Cosm is trying to establish itself as a destination for fandom — both for sports and for film. It has a wide variety of live event offerings, which it telecasts using proprietary cameras mounted in arenas that broadcast back to the dome, as well as its expanding library of movies.

"What I love is that we really are kind of building these Cosm communities built on fandom," Scalice said. "It's the No. 1 foundation for our business."

And Star Wars is a powerful fandom. The franchise remains one of the top-ranking pieces of intellectual property across film, television and retail annually, and Disney is gearing up for a massive celebration of its semicentennial including merchandise launches, theatrical re-releases, its biannual Star Wars Celebration convention and collaborations with other brands.

Correction: This story has been updated to correct the spelling of Alexis Scalice's name.

NASA Ames Stars of the Month: November 2026

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Science  |  Read Source →

The NASA Ames Science Directorate recognizes the outstanding contributions of (pictured left to right) Alyssa Villanueva, Steve Howell, and Samuel LeBlanc. Their commitment to the NASA mission represents the entrepreneurial spirit, technical expertise, and collaborative disposition needed to explore this world and beyond.

Alyssa Villaneuva is an engineer at Blue Marble in the Space Biosciences Division on the LEIA (Lunar Explorer Instrument for space biology Applications) team. Alyssa is recognized for technical excellence, dedication and boundless support to the rest of the team. She goes above and beyond in contributing to LEIA every day and has been essential in all the test efforts, in particular with preparation and execution of engineering validation testing.

Steve Howell is recognized for his leadership of scientific teams for numerous international space missions. His pioneering work in astronomical instrumentation have significantly advanced both space- and ground-based telescope imaging. In kind, the Astronomical society has recently recognized Steve’s lifetime of major contributions to the field with the prestigious Catherine Wolfe Bruce Gold Medal from the Astronomical Society of the Pacific.

Samuel LeBlanc is a research scientist for the Bay Area Environmental Research Institute in the Earth Science Division. Sam is recognized for exceptional technical leadership and innovative thinking in advancing Ames’ flight planning capabilities for NASA airborne science missions. Sam’s contributions have placed Ames at the forefront of airborne science flight planning within and outside the Agency, enabling successful and cost-effective missions that maximize scientific returns.

NASA explores the unknown in air and space, innovates for the benefit of humanity, and inspires the world through discovery.

Google Wallet just turned your Android phone into a digital house key – how it works

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Home & Office > Smart Home > Home Security

Google is moving digital keys out of individual smart lock apps and into the Google Wallet.

Google is adding digital home keys to Google Wallet, letting users tap their phones to unlock compatible smart locks. The home key functionality, which will integrate with Google Home, will roll out this month.

Also: This tiny box gives your older tech smart home superpowers

Apple introduced Home Keys in 2021 with iOS 15, allowing users to store digital keys in the Apple Wallet. This lets you tap your iPhone or Apple Watch on the lock to unlock it. More recently, the addition of Express Mode to Apple Home lets users unlock smart locks without first unlocking their iPhones with Face ID.

Google’s digital keys use tap-to-unlock via NFC on smart locks. Like the Apple Home Key, you don’t need to unlock your phone to use your digital key, though you can change your verification settings as you wish.

Google’s announcement confirms that the company is embracing Aliro and UWB unlocking technologies as well. UWB unlocking for smart locks is supported on Google Pixel 10 Pro and newer models, as well as select UWB-enabled Samsung Galaxy phones. Aliro is a common standard for compatible smart locks and devices.

Before Google’s announcement, compatible smart locks could be controlled on Android through their accompanying app and Google Home. Smart lock companies also offered their own digital access methods, like NFC cards, Bluetooth, and geolocation.

One digital key will be created for each Google Home, so it can unlock all compatible smart locks in that home. You won’t need to search through digital keys in Google Wallet to find the one for your front door, back door, or bedroom door — just tap your phone and Google Home will recognize the lock as one in your smart home ecosystem.

At launch, Google Wallet will support digital keys for the Aqara U400 with UWB and NFC, and NFC on the Schlage Sense Pro and Nuki Keypad 2.

Google Wallet already supports digital car keys for compatible vehicles.

Exowatt Redesigns P3 Solar-Thermal System for AI Data Centers

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Exowatt announced P3 Gen 2 on November 12, 2026, redesigning its modular solar-thermal system for utility projects and large energy users, including AI data centers. The company also introduced Exobloc, a standardized 1 MW validation block, and detailed plans for Athena, a 2.4 GW data-center development in West Texas.

Exowatt says P3 Gen 2 incorporates lessons from deploying P3 Gen 1 and more than 7,000 hours of testing. The revised architecture is intended to improve performance, simplify installation and maintenance, and make manufacturing easier. Its higher capacity factor increases the amount of firm power the system can deliver.

P3 collects sunlight as heat, holds it in long-duration thermal storage and converts it into electricity when needed. Solar collection, storage and generation are integrated into a standardized architecture that can be manufactured at scale and assembled into larger systems to match customer demand. Exowatt labels this method neo-thermal, distinguishing its solar heat input and storage-based dispatch from conventional thermal generation.

CEO and co-founder Hannan Happi said, “AI is creating demand for power faster than the grid can be built. We believe the answer is to productize energy infrastructure: make it standardized, repeatable and less expensive with scale.” That presents Gen 2 as one piece of a broader manufacturing and deployment strategy for AI power infrastructure.

The Exobloc block is designed for customers to test P3’s performance and its operation alongside other energy resources in real-world conditions before pursuing larger installations. Exowatt’s stated objective is to reduce deployment risk and accelerate scale-up through that validation process.

Exowatt is also developing an AI-powered energy management system to coordinate P3 and other generation and storage resources as one dispatchable power plant. The design combines real-time monitoring and control, forecasting and automated dispatch so the software can adjust energy generation, storage and delivery as conditions change.

Critical controls would run on site under an edge-first architecture, independently of cloud connectivity, while industry-standard protocols would support integration with existing energy infrastructure. The design therefore pairs local continuity for essential controls with coordination across a changing mix of power resources.

Exowatt says Athena is the first site for ExoRise, its data-center development arm launched in January 2026. The West Texas project belongs to the company’s Frontier Land strategy, which targets AI infrastructure development in regions with abundant land and renewable resources and the potential to build power at scale.

Exowatt says the site is designed to run on 80% renewable energy. The proposed resource mix combines more than 800 MW of on-site and adjacent solar with high-capacity-factor wind and P3 generation, plus 2.4 GWh of storage. A separate connection to the ERCOT grid is planned as part of Batch Zero, with 1,200 MW across two phases.

The three announcements present a connected strategy: redesign the hardware, validate it in standardized blocks and coordinate it with other resources through software. Athena supplies a specific project for that approach, although its energy mix and grid connection remain plans. For Exowatt’s scale-up ambitions, Exobloc is the practical validation step: larger AI power deployments require evidence that P3 performs and integrates as intended under real-world conditions.

Only 29% of Security Teams Can Routinely Report the Sensitive Data Employees Send to AI Tools

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

This article is based on findings from the Cloud Security Alliance’s (CSA) 2026 survey report, DLP in the AI Era, which Jazz commissioned.

In June and July, CSA asked 433 IT and security professionals whether their team could produce a report showing how many times sensitive data was sent to GenAI tools in the past 30 days. Twenty-nine percent said they could produce it routinely.

That figure would matter less if respondents considered GenAI a minor channel. They ranked it as their top data-exfiltration risk, with 48% placing it in their top three.

I lead engineering at a company that builds data loss prevention, and most of my work is about where, inside a system, the evidence of data movement and why its risk gets created. From that angle, the gap between what teams believe they can see and what they can show follows directly from how GenAI controls were designed.

Just over half of respondents rated their visibility into GenAI at 4 or 5 out of 5. Yet only 29% could routinely back that rating with a report that simply counts how many times sensitive data went to an AI tool.

Source: Cloud Security Alliance, DLP in the AI Era (2026), n=433. Recreated from the report’s charts on pp. 22 and 23.

Enforcement follows the same pattern. Only 22% of respondents strictly block sensitive data on the GenAI channel. Removable media, which respondents ranked as their lowest exfiltration risk, has the highest strict-block rate, at 31%. Strict blocking clusters on the channels with the oldest tooling.

Source: Cloud Security Alliance, DLP in the AI Era (2026), n=433. Recreated from the report’s charts on pp. 23 and 26; bars scaled to 100% where rounding put a row at 99% or 101%.

Jazz also published telemetry from its customer environments alongside the survey. The average organization in that data had 169.5 distinct AI tools in use, of which 9.8 were approved, leaving 94.2% outside the approved list.

The standard response to GenAI risk is to approve a handful of AI services under enterprise agreements and discourage or block the rest. The 94.2% figure shows how far that list falls behind in practice. An engineer with a deadline uses whichever tool solves today’s problem, because procurement for the approved one takes weeks.

A complete list still wouldn’t produce the report CSA asked about. Your incident team, or an auditor, will ask about a specific event in which data left, and a list of tools contains no events.

To show what happened in that event, you need four facts: what sensitive data was involved, which tool received it, which account it went through, and why the person sent it.

The account is where tool-level governance breaks first. The same website serves both your company’s enterprise account, with its retention settings and contract terms, and an employee’s free personal account, which has neither. An approved tool used through a personal login gives you about as much evidence as an unapproved one.

Purpose is harder, and it decides whether anything went wrong. A finance analyst pasting a revenue model into a chatbot to fix a broken formula is doing her job. The same paste from someone who handed in their notice on Friday deserves a very different conversation. What separates them is the context around the person, which never shows up in the payload.

The survey found no dominant approach to controlling sensitive data sent to AI tools. Network-level blocking of AI domains, browser-based content inspection and acceptable-use policy alone each drew between 21% and 23% and enterprise AI platforms with built-in DLP drew 10%.

Each of those controls was designed around a single inspection point. A domain block allows or stops a connection and records which site it went to. A browser extension sees what’s typed into that browser and misses anything sent from a desktop app, a coding assistant inside a code editor or a command-line tool. An enterprise AI platform’s built-in DLP sees what enters that platform. Every one of these picks one place to look and hopes that’s where the data goes.

The single inspection point also explains the enforcement numbers. If a control can’t tell a corporate account from a personal one, or a routine paste from a risky one, it has to block both or neither. Strict blocking then stops legitimate work along with the risky kind. Asked for the main reason they haven’t moved more aggressively to active blocking, 46% of respondents cited fear of disrupting business workflows and 39% cited insufficient confidence in DLP accuracy.

The survey numbers are self-reported, and the survey didn’t test the visibility ratings against actual coverage. Respondents skew toward the Americas (80%) and toward organizations with fewer than 5,000 employees (67%), so the results may not carry over evenly to large global enterprises. The telemetry comes from organizations that chose to deploy this kind of visibility, which may skew towards those already concerned about AI sprawl.

As approved SaaS products add AI assistants, the question of which tool received the data often has two answers: the application the employee opened and the AI model provider behind it. AI agents complicate it further. When an agent pulls a file and sends it somewhere for an employee, the who and the why now belong partly to software. As a recent Unite.AI piece on prompt injection in production agents argues, an agent can be steered by content it reads, so its own account of what it did can’t stand in for evidence of purpose.

Take the last 30 days and pick the most sensitive piece of data you believe reached an AI tool. Then check whether your team can produce the following without building it by hand:

If the first two come back quickly and the last two require interviewing the employee, your governance covers your tool list and very little of your data.

Yonatan Zohar is co-founder and CTO of Jazz, a data loss prevention company, where he leads R&D, data science and product development. Before Jazz, he built and led the agent development and research group at Toka, and he has led more than 100 engineers over his career. He spent eight years in the Israel Defense Forces' Unit 81 as an endpoint agent specialist, moving from developer and vulnerability researcher to leading technical groups that built cyber capabilities.

Anthropic Lets Customers Self-Host Claude Managed Agents

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Anthropic launched self-hosted Claude Managed Agents on November 12, 2026, allowing customers to run the full service and its sandboxes in their own cloud account or on-premises data center. Agents, sessions and session history are stored on that infrastructure, while model requests can go to a chosen inference provider.

Anthropic’s earlier self-hosted sandboxes moved an agent’s tool execution onto customer systems while Anthropic continued running the agent. Tool inputs and outputs still flowed to Anthropic, which also stored skills and memory stores. The new option extends customer control to the API, agent loop and session storage.

The self-hosted architecture centers on cma-server, which serves the Managed Agents API, runs the agent loop, stores agents, sessions and their histories, and calls the configured inference provider. Separate workers execute shell and file tools inside customer sandboxes; the server itself never runs those tools.

The customer-run server exposes the same Managed Agents API as Anthropic’s hosted service, apart from documented feature differences, so the Claude SDK and ant CLI can connect to it. A persistent deployment requires PostgreSQL 16 or later. Without a database, the server retains data only in memory and a restart erases it. Customers must contact their Anthropic account team to request the download and install each release themselves.

Model calls go to the Claude API by default. Customers can instead select Amazon Bedrock, Claude Models on Google Cloud (Google managed), Microsoft Foundry or a model gateway they operate through the server’s provider settings. Agents continue naming models by their Claude API IDs, which the server translates for providers serving those models, allowing an agent definition to work unchanged.

Self-hosting the service does not by itself keep model traffic inside the customer’s network. Each model call sends the conversation so far, including prompts, tool inputs and tool results, to the selected inference provider. The security documentation says the server connects to Anthropic only when the Claude API is that provider, and that Anthropic has no remote access to the customer-run server or its log.

The migration guidance says nothing is copied from Anthropic’s hosted service, including session history. Customers must recreate agents, skills, vault credentials and memory stores on their own server. Organizations already running self-hosted sandboxes can connect the same worker software to the new server, although workers triggered by webhooks will not operate because the self-hosted server sends none.

Customers also assume responsibilities that Anthropic holds for the hosted service: securing the server, hosting memory stores, detecting unusual usage and installing releases. Each deployment serves one tenant at one trust level. Separate tenants, or workloads with different trust levels, therefore require separate deployments with their own databases, server API keys and workers.

Some hosted-service features are unavailable, including scheduled deployments, dreams and webhooks. The web search and web fetch tools remain off until explicitly enabled, and their availability then depends on the inference provider. Web search works with the Claude API, Claude Models on Google Cloud (Google managed) and Microsoft Foundry; web fetch works with the Claude API and Microsoft Foundry.

Session retention is also customer-managed. Sessions and their histories remain in the customer database until deleted rather than expiring by age. Deletion removes them from that database but does not remove copies in backups, exported logs and traces, memory stores or, on Kubernetes, the session’s workspace volume.

For customers, the important boundary is therefore twofold: the agent’s state and execution can stay within their own deployment, but each conversation still goes to the inference provider they select. The added control comes with the operational work of securing and updating that deployment, isolating tenants and accepting gaps in the hosted feature set.

David Mařák, CTO and Co-Founder of YORD – Interview Series

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

David Mařák is CTO and Co-Founder of YORD. With over a decade of experience in immersive technologies, he leads the studio’s technology strategy and development at the intersection of artificial intelligence, extended reality, spatial computing, and interactive digital experiences. His work spans AI-powered applications, virtual and augmented reality, digital humans, and real-time generative technologies.

YORD is a creative technology studio that designs and develops AI and XR experiences for brands and organizations. Its work includes interactive installations, immersive brand experiences, museum environments, training solutions, and custom digital products, combining creative development with emerging technologies.

YORD builds for Apple Vision Pro and other headsets. How do Samsung’s glasses compare from a developer’s perspective, and what do they mean for immersive retail?

Vision Pro is a closed, highly optimized device with a futuristic look, and as a developer, you know exactly what you’re working with. Samsung’s glasses are built to look like normal glasses, and Android XR will run on hardware from several manufacturers, so it’s more open and varied, and not perfectly tuned for any one device.

A few years ago I’d have seen that fragmentation as a big problem. Today I don’t think it matters that much. With AI in the development process, it’s a lot easier to optimize for a specific device, read through someone’s documentation, or port between platforms, so the open platform tends to win because more people build for it. In the long run I’d bet on Android XR for that reason. For immersive retail though, there’s nothing on these glasses right now. No display means we can’t build anything the customer would see.

Is there any retail interaction that becomes possible once people wear these glasses in a store, something a phone can’t do?

Not yet. I should say that I’m a fan of glasses in general. We’ve been living digital lives through our phones for 15 years, it’s just not comfortable, you have to look down at a small rectangle and hold it in one hand the whole time. Glasses are the natural next step.

But Samsung’s glasses don’t solve that. You can look at a product and ask Gemini about it without getting your phone out, which is nice, but the phone can already do the same thing; the camera has just moved to your nose. The interaction that would be new is looking at a product and seeing the price, material, reviews or stock availability directly on the shelf. Samsung’s glasses can recognize the product, and that’s where it ends.

What’s the biggest limitation of a display-free device for brands, and what should they do until display versions arrive?

You can’t show anything. You can have great product data, and a clever assistant behind it, and the customer still gets a spoken description instead of seeing the thing.

I wouldn’t build any immersive strategy around these glasses. If a brand wants to do XR, do it on hardware that supports XR today, and keep the content in a format that can move to other platforms later. Good 3D models of your products, clean product data, experiences that aren’t locked to one device. When light glasses with a real display arrive, and I think they will, that’s what you’ll be able to reuse.

Warby Parker stores are the demo site. Why does a physical store make sense for this launch?

I don’t see it as a testing ground so much as the place where the glasses get sold. They’re prescription glasses with a camera in them, and prescription glasses get sold at the optician. You try them on, they fit your lenses, you walk out wearing them. Samsung and Google needed a chain with hundreds of stores and their own optical labs, and Warby Parker is that. Meta did the same thing with Ray-Ban and EssilorLuxottica.

I think it’s the right call. Glasses are a fashion item and a medical device before they’re a gadget, and selling them in an electronics store would probably not work. It also tells you what the product is. The frames are what’s for sale, and Gemini is something you get with them.

Camera glasses make people uncomfortable. How should brands deal with that in stores and public spaces?

I don’t think the camera itself is the issue. We walk around with a phone camera in our hand all day, and nobody calls that a surveillance device. The glasses just move a camera you already have to a different place.

The difference is that with a phone you can see when someone is pointing it at something, and with glasses the people around you can’t tell. So, at minimum, there has to be a recording light that is bright, visible from the front, and can’t be turned off, and I’d prefer that to be a platform rule rather than something each manufacturer decides. Then there’s the question of what gets recorded, how long it’s stored, who has access to it, and what developers like us can see. Samsung and Google haven’t said much about any of that. Until they do, I wouldn’t build in-store experiences that rely on continuously watching the customer.

For retailers who want to do something useful today, what’s the low-hanging fruit?

Making existing product information available through voice, and then testing whether anyone actually prefers it to taking out their phone. I don’t see much more than that.

For any glasses project, the test is whether it does something better than what the customer already has in their pocket. If the same thing works just as well on a phone, there’s no reason to pay for a glasses version. If a retailer wants a real immersive experience, that’s possible today on other hardware, and we can build that. Trying to do XR on a device with no display will end up being a gimmick.

YORD works across many platforms. How does that shape the advice you give brands about investing in XR?

The main thing we’ve learned is not to mistake a hardware announcement for a development opportunity. Vision Pro, Quest and the others are quite different, but on all of them we can build something visual that reacts to the user’s surroundings. On Samsung’s glasses, we can’t, so before asking how to port an experience, you have to ask whether the device can run it at all.

Otherwise the advice hasn’t changed. Don’t tie your XR plans to a single manufacturer. We’ve been through enough hardware generations to know the devices will look different in two years. The content you’ve built and the users you’ve reached are what stays with you.

How do you measure whether an XR experience builds loyalty, compared to a normal digital touchpoint?

I wouldn’t count how many people tried it, because everyone tries new hardware once. I’d look at what happened afterward. Did they understand the product better? Did they decide faster? Did they buy? Did they come back? These are the same metrics you’d use for a website or an app, and the XR version has to beat them; otherwise, there’s no reason to do it.

With real XR, there’s a decent chance it does, because you’re showing someone the product in their own space rather than on a screen. With glasses that have no display, I’d be careful about promising anything like that. Measure it against a phone app and see what comes out.

What’s the biggest misconception brands have about XR right now, and does Samsung’s launch change it?

That anything with glasses, a headset, or AI in it is XR. It isn’t. XR refers to digital content that’s tied to the physical space around you, so the device has to understand the environment and be able to change what you see.

Google Glass had a display but almost no understanding of what you were looking at. Samsung’s glasses are the opposite; they understand the surroundings through the camera and Gemini but can’t show you anything. Each has one half of it. A few years ago, the industry put the metaverse label on a lot of things that had nothing to do with immersive computing, and I’m a bit worried we’re about to do the same with AI glasses. Samsung’s product might be a good wearable assistant. It’s not an XR device just because the name says so.

As a CTO, what are you watching over the next 6 to 12 months to tell whether XR glasses are heading for the mass market or staying niche?

Two things: whether the hardware gets there and whether developers get access.

On hardware, I think glasses will eventually replace the phone, because everything we do on a phone we’d rather do without holding it. What’s missing is a single device that has a see-through display, understands the space around you, and comes with a frame you can wear from morning to evening. Vision Pro, Galaxy XR or Quest are too heavy and too expensive for that. Samsung’s glasses are light enough but can’t show anything. Snap’s Specs, which shipped this autumn for around $2,200, are the closest so far. Still too expensive and a bit thick, but they have a real display, they understand the room, and we can develop for them. That’s the direction I’d expect things to go, and Samsung is currently behind it.

On developers, I want to see whether we’ll be able to ship our own apps, render spatial content, and work with the data the device uses to understand the environment, and whether the camera rules get written down. If the platforms open up, people will build on them quickly because development has become much cheaper with AI. If they stay closed around one assistant, we’ll end up with a slightly better Alexa.

Thank you for the great interview, readers who wish to explore how XR, AI, and spatial computing can create useful retail and brand experiences should visit YORD.

As a futurist, he is dedicated to exploring how these innovations will shape our world. In addition, he is the founder of Securities.io, a platform focused on investing in cutting-edge technologies that are redefining the future and reshaping entire sectors.

Expo Opens Paid-Plan Access to Cloud Simulators for AI Coding Agents

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Expo announced on November 12, 2026 that Expo Simulators is available to everyone on its paid plans, giving AI coding agents cloud iOS simulators and Android emulators on which to run and verify app changes. Usage costs $0.10 per simulator minute on either platform.

The paid-plan launch expands the limited-access EAS Simulator preview Expo introduced on September 14, 2026. An Expo blog post dated November 10, 2026 was still directing developers to the waitlist, making broader paid-plan availability the new step rather than the first public demonstration of cloud devices.

Expo’s premise is that code generation has shifted the bottleneck to verification, with agents producing changes faster than engineering teams can test and review them. Mobile adds a hardware constraint: iOS simulators run only on Apple hardware, while cloud agents typically run on Linux. An agent that cannot run the app has to assess code without seeing the resulting behavior.

The local alternative also consumes developer-machine capacity. In Expo’s example, seven agents working in parallel can require seven simulators or emulators, and Android emulators can each use 4 GB or more of RAM. Remote sessions shift that compute to Expo infrastructure and let teams run an iOS simulator from Linux, Windows through the Windows Subsystem for Linux, or a cloud sandbox without a local Mac.

The intended loop starts with a bug report in a tool such as Linear. An agent reproduces the issue, writes a fix and tests it on cloud devices in parallel, away from the developer’s machine. It can place a screen recording, screenshots and logs on the pull request, while teammates open the running build from a link without requiring a TestFlight build. The service works with Cursor, Claude Code and Codex, and adds no AI model of its own: Expo is supplying the device runtime and evidence rather than another coding model.

Expo co-founder and CTO James Ide described the intended review model: “The agent was the fast part. Expo Simulators gives every agent a simulator, so a fix arrives for review already tested on several simulators, and the only decision left is whether it’s the right code. Watch it run, then merge.”

A session can be started through EAS CLI, the REST API or an authenticated creation link, according to the Expo Simulators documentation. The session can install an Expo Build, a build whose native code matches the project’s fingerprint, an application archive or Expo Go; without an application source, it starts with a blank device. Agents and test jobs can control apps through agent-device, Argent or Appium, while a browser preview lets people watch the run. The official Expo Simulators skill packages access checks, build preparation, session limits, device control, evidence collection and cleanup for agents.

The product page lists support for React Native, Swift, Kotlin, Flutter, Unity and other frameworks. Apps do not have to be built with Expo or React Native, but the build must run on an iOS Simulator or Android Emulator and the project must be linked to Expo Services. Each session runs in an isolated environment with session-scoped credentials and access-controlled previews, and stopping it disables the simulator and controller endpoints.

On Expo’s pricing page, the Starter, Production and Enterprise plans each include 60 simulator minutes per month until 2027, with usage-based pricing beyond that allowance. Paid sessions can run for up to 115 minutes.

Parallelism does not create unlimited account capacity. The usage and billing guide says simulator sessions draw on the account’s job concurrency and queue when no capacity is free. Ending a CI job or returning from a non-interactive command also does not stop the remote device. Teams must stop it explicitly or rely on a duration or idle limit; otherwise, an unattended session continues consuming usage. That makes cleanup and session limits part of cost control for automated workflows.

The limitations guide adds three material qualifications: the simulator CLI commands remain experimental, the service does not provide physical devices, and recordings may miss frames and therefore cannot prove or disprove a brief frame drop. A cloud runtime can verify app behavior, but it does not eliminate the need for physical-device testing or profiling where those tools are required.

Expo’s next disclosed step is Agent Sessions, a planned enterprise layer for triggering agents from sources such as customer feedback and protecting against prompt injection, where malicious input could direct an agent to expose data. Expo expects to share details by the end of November 2026; this is a roadmap item rather than a capability included in the current launch.

Simulators adds a verification stage to Expo’s existing build, CI/CD, app-store submission, over-the-air update and monitoring services. The result is a division of labor rather than an autonomous release process: agents supply tested changes and runtime evidence, account limits govern how much runs at once, and people retain the merge decision.

MLS roundup: AI, fraud prevention and data sharing take center stage

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Multiple MLS organizations announced new partnerships focused on AI assistants, fraud prevention, and cross-market data sharing. Deals include Property Shield with Big Sky Country MLS, Scout inside Flexmls, RPR View data sharing, and RealReports for 15,000 agents.  AI Summary

MLS organizations are expanding their technology offerings with new partnerships focused on artificial intelligence, property fraud prevention and access to listing data. The latest developments include tools designed to streamline agents’ work, protect listing information and improve property research across markets.

The MLS will also use Property Shield’s automated IDX compliance monitoring to identify potential violations across licensed data feeds. The partnership addresses the growing problem of scammers copying legitimate listings to solicit deposits from unsuspecting buyers and renters.

FBS has introduced Flexmls Scout, an AI assistant that allows real estate professionals to search MLS data, analyze market activity and access business insights using natural-language questions.

Built directly into Flexmls, Scout uses permissioned MLS information and enterprise controls to help agents research properties and understand market trends without switching platforms. The product is available to Flexmls MLS customers as an enterprise solution.

Stellar MLS in Florida and MIBOR Broker Listing Cooperative in Indiana have announced a data-sharing agreement through Realtors Property Resource’s RPR View platform.

RealReports combines property research, prospecting, comparative market analysis and client engagement tools with information covering more than 150 million U.S. properties. Its AI assistant, Aiden, helps agents identify potential business opportunities and provide clients with more detailed property insights.

The program provides tools to help agents educate homeowners about deed theft, seller impersonation and other property fraud risks. The partnership is designed to help agents maintain relationships with clients beyond closing while offering preventive protection.

Platinum is HAR.com’s subscription-based suite of marketing and productivity tools designed to help real estate professionals grow their business, strengthen their brand and work more efficiently. Unlock expands home equity agreements to Georgia

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Unlock Technologies expanded its home equity agreement product to Georgia, bringing availability to more than half of U.S. states. Unlock cited $248,000 in average home equity for Georgia homeowners in Q1 2026 and a 37.7% equity-rich share in Q2 2026.  AI Summary

Unlock Technologies announced on Thursday that it has expanded its home equity agreement (HEA) product to Georgia, bringing its availability to more than half of U.S. states.

More than 25,000 homeowners nationwide have used an Unlock HEA to access their home equity, the Tempe, Arizona-based company said in its release.

The agreements allow homeowners to receive cash in exchange for a percentage of their home’s future value without taking on an additional monthly payment. Homeowners retain ownership and can continue living in their homes. They also avoid refinancing an existing mortgage, which can be advantageous in a higher-rate environment.

Georgia homeowners with a mortgage held an average of $248,000 in home equity at the end of the first quarter of 2026, according to Cotality data cited by Unlock. Separately, 37.7% of mortgaged homes in the state were considered equity-rich in the second quarter, meaning homeowners owed no more than half of their property’s estimated market value, according to ATTOM data cited in the announcement.

Georgia was the second-highest state for inbound interest on Unlock.com among states where the company had not yet launched, said Michael Micheletti, Unlock’s chief marketing officer. “That level of interest reinforces what we’re seeing across the country: homeowners want greater flexibility in how they access and use their home equity to address critical life needs,” he said.

“Homeownership is getting more expensive, and Georgia homeowners are feeling the impact of rising property taxes, insurance premiums and maintenance costs,” Micheletti added. “Many homeowners have built substantial equity, but their monthly budgets don’t always reflect that. Unlock gives homeowners a way to access that equity without adding another monthly payment.”

Zero-down VA loans help push veteran homeownership to 77%, study finds

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

This article analyzes new 2024 ACS data showing veterans have a 77% homeownership rate versus 60% for non-veterans, with especially large advantages for buyers under 35 in markets like Memphis, Fresno and Virginia Beach.   AI Summary

Veterans own homes at much higher rates than non-veterans, a gap driven in part by access to specialized mortgage programs that are also helping narrow racial homeownership disparities, according to an analysis of 2024 American Community Survey data for the nation’s 50 largest metro areas.

The veteran homeownership rate was 77% in 2024, compared to 60% for non-veterans. Among adults under 35, 46.6% of veterans were homeowners versus 31.9% of non-veterans, showing that military service is associated with earlier entry into homeownership.

Homeownership remains the primary path to wealth for U.S. households. Federal Reserve data show the median wealth of a typical homeowner is more than 40 times the median wealth of a typical renter, underscoring why access to mortgage credit and affordable payments is central to closing wealth gaps.

Among the 50 largest metro areas, Virginia Beach, Virginia, has both the highest concentration of veterans and the largest homeownership advantage for that population. In Virginia Beach, 80% of veterans own homes, compared with 57.4% of non-veterans.

Other large metros where veterans significantly outperform civilians on homeownership include:

Veterans also see a notable edge in high-cost coastal markets where civilian homeownership is constrained by high prices. In places like New York, San Diego and Fresno, access to 0% down payment financing through the VA loan program can provide a substantial relative boost for eligible buyers compared to civilians reliant on conventional financing.

The homeownership advantage is particularly pronounced for veterans under 35. In Memphis, 66% of young veterans are homeowners, compared with 46.9% of young non-veterans, a 19.1 percentage point gap.

Other top metros for young veteran homeownership include:

While younger veterans still have lower homeownership rates than older veterans, they are substantially more likely to own homes than their non-veteran peers. For lenders and real estate agents, that indicates a distinct demand segment for first-time buyer products and education tailored to veteran households.

The analysis also finds that veteran status is associated with reduced racial disparities in homeownership. Across the 50 largest metros, overall homeownership rates are 72.1% for white households and 49.5% for non-white households, reflecting a long history of segregation and discrimination in housing.

Among veteran households, however, the white-nonwhite homeownership gap shrinks to 13.6 percentage points, as military service appears to elevate non-white homeownership rates.

Several large metros show particularly small racial gaps among veterans:

For policymakers and housing practitioners focused on equity, these patterns suggest that veteran-specific benefits and underwriting frameworks may offer lessons for reducing racial gaps in the broader market.

Veterans benefit from a web of federal, state and nonprofit housing resources, with the U.S. Department of Veterans Affairs’ purchase loan program at the core. In fiscal year 2025, the VA guaranteed 323,835 purchase loans, according to the analysis.

VA purchase loans allow eligible veterans and service members to buy with zero down payment and no ongoing monthly mortgage insurance. Because the loans are backed by a partial federal guarantee, they typically feature lower interest rates, capped closing costs and more flexible qualification standards than conventional loans.

Beyond VA loans, veterans may qualify for:

Yet many eligible veterans are not fully aware of these benefits or how to access them. For lenders, servicers and real estate agents, that awareness gap represents both a business opportunity and an avenue to support wealth-building and housing stability among veteran households.

For mortgage lenders, veteran households — especially those under 35 — represent a resilient and often under-served segment of potential homebuyers. Understanding VA eligibility rules, pricing structures and documentation requirements is critical to capturing this demand and ensuring borrowers are matched with the most advantageous products.

Real estate agents who can navigate VA appraisal, property condition standards and seller perceptions around VA offers are better positioned to guide veteran clients in competitive markets, particularly in high-cost coastal metros where the relative advantage of VA financing is largest.

Housing nonprofits and local governments can leverage this data to target outreach and counseling in metros where veterans cluster and where racial homeownership gaps among veterans are already narrower, using those markets as benchmarks for broader equity strategies.

The analysis is based on 2024 American Community Survey 5-year microdata for the 50 largest U.S. metropolitan areas. Home price data by metro are sourced from Realtor.com.

Affordability calculations assume a 0% down payment, a 30-year fixed-rate mortgage with a 6.5% interest rate and average property tax and homeowners insurance costs by market. A home is considered affordable if the monthly housing payment does not exceed 28% of household monthly income.

How You Collaborate With AI on Creative Work Makes All the Difference

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

A new study dives into the consequences of collaborating with AI on creative endeavors, and what it means for output and satisfaction at work.

Did you use AI at your job today? Maybe it helped you work faster. But too much AI may reduce both the quality of your work and your satisfaction with the job, according to a study published Nov. 10 in the PNAS Nexus science journal.

The team behind the study knew from prior research that generative AI was a mixed bag, capable of dramatically boosting productivity, but also prone to throttling creative diversity and worker satisfaction.

“The question we wanted to answer was whether these trade-offs are inevitable,” Kartik Hosanagar, a University of Pennsylvania professor of technology, digital business and marketing, and one of the study’s authors, told CNET via email. “Our research suggests they aren’t.”

AI is changing work quickly, with dizzying contradictions. Tech companies like Microsoft and Meta are spending millions on integrating AI into their business for greater efficiencies and output, while getting rid of human workers through mass buyouts and layoffs. Workers who use AI report some advantages, like saving 40 to 60 minutes a day at work, while suffering consequences like loss of confidence, loss of skills and a tougher labor market.

Teachers have also sounded the alarm, saying AI is essentially ending homework. Researchers say that if we don’t pay attention to how we collaborate with AI, we could start losing critical thinking along the way.

“Think about GPS navigation. It’s incredibly useful, but many of us have lost the ability to navigate even familiar neighborhoods without it,” Hosanagar said. “Something similar could happen with AI and cognitive skills.”

The study by Hosanagar’s team focused on the different ways we collaborate with AI on creative tasks. They found that the way we use AI — not just whether or not we use it — has a big effect on the final product and on our perception of the work.

“The future of work isn’t just about how capable AI becomes,” Hosanagar said, “but how we design the collaboration between humans and AI so as to support a broader set of goals.”

The researchers tasked 285 students, split into three groups, with writing assignments over two days. On the first day, no students used AI for writing. On the second day, each group used a different process for writing with AI.

Each of the groups used a separate “collaboration design” when working with the AI on the writing assignment. The collaboration designs represent different ways people interact with AI on the job. All three approaches delivered similar productivity benefits, but differed significantly in quality, satisfaction and creative diversity, according to Hosanagar.

In the first group, Human Confirmation, the AI planned, outlined, wrote and edited the assignment, with the humans just approving the work during each step. In the second, Human Creativity, humans did the outlining and planning, while AI did the writing and editing. In the third, Copilot, humans and AI collaborated during the entire process.

The researchers found that using AI in creative writing did not improve the quality of the work in any of the groups, but it did speed up the task.

People in the Human Confirmation produced the lowest quality and least diverse work, and participants reported feeling unsatisfied with it. But the other two groups, Human Creativity and Copilot, produced better work at the same speed, with participants reporting a more positive experience.

The study showed that the best outcomes happen when humans are actively involved in creative tasks, especially generating ideas and creating outlines, while AI assists with execution, Hosanagar said. “The goal shouldn’t be to eliminate human effort, but to direct that effort toward the parts of the process where human judgment and creativity matter most.”

It would be good to be wary, too, of the unintended consequences of people playing a smaller role in their daily work as they collaborate with AI.

“Our research also suggests that people derive satisfaction from having a meaningful creative role, not simply from producing a good final output,” Hosanagar said. “So, we should be thoughtful about what we automate.”

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/providers/bon-secours-mercy-health-acquire-majority-stake-surgery-partners" hreflang="en">Bon Secours Mercy Health bumps up offer for majority stake in Surgery Partners to $6B valuation</a>

📅 2026-11-12  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →
Bon Secours Mercy Health bumps up offer for majority stake in Surgery Partners to $6B valuation" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

Bon Secours Mercy Health bumped the price tag on its pending transaction to acquire a majority share in healthcare services company Surgery Partners in response to an unsolicited offer the short-stay surgical platform received from a third party, the organizations shared this week.

The transaction now values the acquiree at $6 billion, or $25 per share, up from the initial $5.7 billion and $23.50 per share (see that story below). Additionally, the parties agreed to an additional $1.61 per share in ticking fees if the deal doesn't close by March 1, 2027.

The companies' announcement does not name the third party, which offered $28 per share in a Nov. 3 proposal. A report from Bloomberg, citing an unnamed source, names UnitedHealth as the extra bidder whose offer was rejected over concerns about deal complexity and ability to close.

Surgery Partners’ business platform includes ambulatory surgery centers, surgical hospitals and about 5,000 affiliated physicians spanning 30 states. It reported $3.31 billion in revenue during 2025 and $0.47 adjusted earnings per share.

Affiliates of Bain Capital Private Equity currently control about 39% of Surgery Partners. Under the current deal, Bain would roll over its shares while the remainder would be acquired by a Bon Secours affiliate, HealthSpan Partners.

Bon Secours Mercy Health is acquiring a majority share in healthcare services company Surgery Partners in a take-private deal valuing the acquiree at $5.7 billion, the organizations announced Monday morning.

The transaction is expected to close in the first quarter of next year, pending approvals, and with over 200 locations in tow, would help the major nonprofit health system create “one of the country’s largest outpatient surgical care platforms,” they said.

“As healthcare continues to evolve, it is important to grow in ways that allow us to serve more people, in more communities and through a broader range of care settings,” John M. Starcher, Jr., president and CEO of Bon Secours Mercy Health, said in the announcement. “Our relationship with Surgery Partners represents an important step toward advancing that commitment."

Currently, affiliates of Bain Capital Private Equity control about 39% of the public Surgery Partners. Under the terms of the deal, Bain Capital would retain and roll over those shares, while HealthSpan Partners, an affiliate of Bon Secours, would purchase the remainder with cash at $23.50 per share.

Surgery Partners’ short-stay surgical platform includes ambulatory surgery centers, surgical hospitals and about 5,000 affiliated physicians spanning 30 states. It reported $3.31 billion in revenue during 2025 and $0.47 adjusted earnings per share.

The company has been public since 2015, with a 2017 merger making it the largest standalone, independent surgical facilities operator at the time. Bain Capital made an offer in January 2025 to fully acquire the shares it didn’t own for $25.75 apiece (roughly $6.7 billion enterprise valuation), though that offer was rejected a few months later.

Surgery Partners has also been slimming down some of its acute care holdings in recent months, a strategic move its leadership said would better position the company as a purely short-stay surgical provider while limiting its exposure to Medicaid.

“We believe this transaction represents the best path forward for Surgery Partners, delivering an attractive all-cash premium to our shareholders while creating lasting benefits for our patients, physicians and other stakeholders,” Surgery Partners CEO Eric Evans said Monday.

Bon Secours Mercy Health is a Catholic nonprofit with 47 hospitals, more than 1,200 care sites and nearly 60,000 employees. It reported just shy of $14 billion in total operating revenue through 2025, as well as $324 million of recurring operating income (2.3% recurring operating margin) and a $1.1 billion bottom line. Of note, its holdings include 41% ownership in revenue cycle management company Ensemble Health Partners, for which a summer strategic recapitalization transaction with a new private equity investor injected $670.8 million in cash into the system, per recent financial statements.

Bon Secours’ HealthSpan Partners is financing the Surgery Partners purchase with a combination of cash on hand and newly secured debt. Upon closing, the parties plan to keep Surgery Partners’ operations, brand and headquarters in place, while also keeping the business as a secular organization.

Strategically, the deal is evidence of large health systems’ growing investments in ambulatory care networks, where many expect to see rising demand as consumers prioritize lower-cost care settings. Healthcare executives have bluntly described these investments as a “top priority,” particularly as regulatory and policy activity such as the wind-down of Medicare’s inpatient-only list pushes care outside of the hospital.

“As more care moves beyond the hospital setting, patients are looking for high-quality care that fits more seamlessly into their lives,” Wael Haidar, M.D., Bon Secours Mercy Health’s president of clinical operations, said in the announcement. “Surgery Partners understands that care should be designed around the needs of patients, and that approach aligns closely with our mission. Together, we have an opportunity to expand access to high-quality outpatient care while supporting physicians and helping patients receive the right care, in the right setting, at the right time.”

The most apparent analogue for Bon Secours’ deal comes from Ascension, an even larger Catholic nonprofit, which closed a $3.9 billion deal over the summer to acquire AMSURG, an ambulatory surgery management services company with over 300 locations. Ascension CEO Eduardo Conrado has described that deal as both a means to flesh out his system’s existing markets—improving overall volumes and allowing hospitals to focus on high-complexity patients—and a way to strike new business partnerships with other local health systems in markets beyond its prior footprint.
